## Instructions

You are a research assistant performing **Stage 2 of Thomas & Harden (2008) thematic synthesis**: grouping coded evidence into **descriptive themes** for a systematic literature review on quantum computing in financial services.

The evidence has already been open-coded at the text level (Stage 1 = A1). You receive the coded evidence in the form of analytical memos (A2) and, for multi-silo papers, silo-specific overlays. You DO NOT see the raw papers. Every theme you generate must be grounded in specific code IDs from the memos so a reviewer can trace back to the exact text span.

## Inputs

### Silo context
Silo: Fraud Detection (FD)
Batch ID: 1 of 1
Papers in this batch: 105
Minimum supporting papers per theme: 3

### Evidence blocks (one per paper)
Two block types appear; treat both uniformly as evidence of what that paper contributes:

- `type: central` — 9-dimension analytical memo (problem framing, quantum method, results, classical comparison, limitations, future work, novelty, methodological notes). Each dimension cites `support_codes` = A1 code IDs.
- `type: central+overlay` — same central memo PLUS a silo-specific overlay highlighting `key_points` most relevant to THIS silo. Multi-silo papers use this form; the overlay emphasises silo-X-specific readings but the central memo remains the evidence spine.

Both block types carry code IDs (e.g., `C-001`). You must cite these IDs in your themes.

=== PAPER 01877ad38ab2 (type: central) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper tackles payment card fraud detection as a binary classification problem where the main business challenge is balancing false positives and missed fraud, formulated as improving fraud-specific KPIs such as hit rate/recall and false alarm ratio using QSVM-based models on transaction features. The authors hypothesize that quantum computing, particularly via feature-space exploration, may help discover feature subsets that yield more accurate fraud classification than purely classical preprocessing. They also question whether classical feature selection prior to QML might remove nuanced information useful for a quantum model.
    [codes: C-001, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-016]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The study employs a Quantum Support Vector Machine (QSVM) with a quantum feature map ρ(x)=|ψ(x)⟩⟨ψ(x)| to embed transaction data into a quantum Hilbert space, using Z and ZZ feature maps with varying entanglement and depth. A bespoke quantum feature selection procedure iterates over combinations of p features (starting from 3 up to 7) out of m to identify subsets that maximize QSVM performance, and the selected features are then used in QSVM classifiers whose predictions are later combined with a classical XGBoost model in a hybrid ensemble. The quantum method is evaluated primarily on a drastically reduced, balanced dataset tailored to fit current quantum hardware constraints, with about seven qubits and circuit depth around 70.
    [codes: C-003, C-005, C-020, C-023, C-033, C-037, C-039, C-043]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Empirically, the authors find that fraud-detection performance is highly sensitive to feature selection and the algorithms used to select features. The QSVM-based feature selection provides a complementary exploration of the feature space, yielding feature subsets that differ from those found by classical methods, and improving QSVM performance compared to using features ranked by classical classifiers. More entangled quantum feature maps (e.g., ZZ vs Z) perform better in their experiments, and integrating QSVM with a classical XGBoost classifier in a mixed quantum–classical ensemble increases test accuracy up to about 81% on the reduced dataset. They also report that simpler meta-classifiers over the disagreement subset between QSVM and XGBoost perform best, and that the classical rule engine shows signs of overfitting compared to its test performance.
    [codes: C-006, C-007, C-027, C-029, C-030, C-035, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-046, C-047, C-048, C-049]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The quantum approach is benchmarked against two classical baselines: a deterministic, expert-guided rule generator and classical machine-learning models such as XGBoost trained on both original and balanced datasets. The rule generator can exploit the full, highly imbalanced dataset and natively handle categorical features but exhibits overfitting, with training KPIs substantially exceeding test KPIs. Classical ML models, tuned via XGBoost cross-validation, achieve high accuracy and AUC and produce feature-importance rankings that differ between classifiers, highlighting model-dependent views of feature relevance. For a fair quantum–classical comparison, both QSVM and classical models are trained on a drastically reduced and balanced dataset; on this subset, the authors observe similar overall performance between standalone classical and quantum models, but complementary pointwise predictions that can be exploited via a hybrid ensemble that slightly improves accuracy beyond the best classical model alone.
    [codes: C-012, C-018, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-044, C-045, C-047, C-048, C-049]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors emphasize that their quantum experiments require a drastically reduced dataset to conform to current quantum hardware limits, which constrains the realism and scalability of the evaluation compared to the full 2.4 million–transaction dataset. They note that integration of quantum models into IBM Safer Payments is technically feasible via external model import, but latency requirements and associated architectural considerations are explicitly left outside the scope of this work. They also highlight that under-sampling used to balance classes affects the false alarm scale on ROC*-like plots, requiring rescaling by a factor of about 500 when interpreting results, and that using classical feature-importance rankings to select features for a QSVM might bias outcomes and potentially undercut the quantum model’s full capabilities. In addition, some standard dimensionality-reduction techniques, such as PCA and FAMD, are found to be unsuitable or ineffective for their largely categorical fraud data, limiting the available classical baselines for feature selection. Finally, they point out that circuit depth around 70 and noise considerations (handled only in simulation here) may challenge execution on near-term hardware, and that all quantum results in this paper are obtained on simulators, with physical hardware implementation deferred.
    [codes: C-008, C-014, C-015, C-018, C-020, C-023, C-032, C-034, C-036, C-043, C-050]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The authors state that extending their simulated QSVM and hybrid quantum–classical fraud-detection experiments to actual quantum hardware is planned and will be reported in a separate manuscript, implying future work on hardware-level performance, latency, and robustness. This likely includes investigating execution of ~70-depth, seven-qubit circuits using realistic noise models and integrating such workflows into production fraud-detection platforms.
    [codes: C-050]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper positions itself as presenting one of the first end-to-end applications of a QSVM to a real-world financial payment fraud-detection problem, from raw card-payment data through to evaluation in fraud-specific KPIs. Its main methodological contribution is a quantum-native feature selection algorithm that leverages QSVM feature maps to search over feature combinations, instead of relying on classical feature-importance rankings that might suppress quantum-relevant structure. Additionally, the authors claim novelty in demonstrating that QSVM-based feature selection can yield feature subsets distinct from and empirically superior to classical selections on their dataset, and in constructing a mixed quantum–classical ensemble that exploits complementary predictions between QSVM and XGBoost to improve fraud-detection accuracy on the reduced dataset.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-010, C-011, C-036, C-037, C-040, C-041, C-044, C-047, C-049]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses real-world card payment data (about 2.4 million transactions, 80% debit and 20% credit) with 48 engineered attributes, and defines domain-specific KPIs (hit rate and false alarm ratio) plus a modified ROC* curve tailored to fraud-prevention practice. The authors create three data references—from full, imbalanced data for rules, to balanced and then drastically reduced balanced subsets—for different use cases, and employ a chronological train–test split to reflect temporal deployment conditions. Classical models are tuned via cross-validation; under-sampling is repeated across multiple trials to mitigate sampling bias and they explicitly discuss how under-sampling distorts false-alarm rates. For the quantum side, they formalize the feature-map-based QSVM kernel, test alternative classical dimensionality-reduction methods (PCA, FAMD) and report when these fail to discriminate, and systematically vary feature maps and entanglement structure. The hybrid ensemble is constructed by training a meta-classifier only on the subset of samples where classical and quantum predictions disagree, and they quantify the disagreement rate on train and test sets. Overall, the paper provides reasonably detailed descriptions of preprocessing, feature engineering, and evaluation metrics, but some implementation details (e.g., latency impact, full deployment pipeline, and real-hardware behavior) are left for future work.
    [codes: C-002, C-004, C-012, C-013, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-028, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-037, C-039, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-046, C-048]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: None; all extracted codes could be assigned meaningfully to the defined dimensions.
    [codes: —]

=== PAPER 0525be4c60fb (type: central) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames quantum computing as a transformative technology to address inefficiency, inaccuracy, and fraud in U.S. healthcare billing, which is burdened by complex, large-scale datasets and administrative waste. It formulates core tasks—billing code and workflow optimization, fraud/anomaly detection, and secure data transmission—as computational problems where classical methods and manual processes are portrayed as inadequate and often NP-hard in the optimization case. These challenges are linked to broader cost impacts on firms, patients, and the U.S. healthcare economy.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-005, C-006, C-007, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-029, C-031, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors propose a suite of quantum methods: QAOA for formulating healthcare billing and workflow optimization as QUBO-like problems mapped to a Hamiltonian, Grover’s algorithm and quantum-enhanced SVMs for high-precision fraud and anomaly detection, and quantum cryptography/QKD for HIPAA-aligned secure data transmission. They describe using superposition and entanglement for parallel processing of large billing datasets, estimate qubit and gate requirements and runtimes (via a gate-throughput and efficiency-factor equation), and outline a procedural plan that includes anonymized HIPAA-compliant data collection, implementation of QAOA and QSVMs, and simulation of quantum cryptographic protocols. Comparative tables and a runtime model further characterize quantum systems’ specialized runtime and operational trade-offs, including high energy demands from cryogenic cooling.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-008, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-032, C-033, C-035, C-037, C-038, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-026, C-025, C-027]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The paper reports several quantitative performance claims, largely as preliminary benchmarks or cited examples: external quantum fraud detection pilots with 30% faster anomaly detection, quantum supply-chain optimization with 40% cost savings, and QKD deployments with zero breaches over 12 months. Within the paper’s own benchmarking narrative, a table attributes higher fraud-detection accuracy (e.g., 90%) and faster overall processing (~2 hours) to quantum systems compared with classical ones for healthcare billing tasks, and derived runtime estimates suggest a fraud-detection Grover search could complete in about 50 seconds on assumed hardware. The conclusion further states that preliminary benchmarks indicate up to a 70% decrease in processing time and a 15–20% improvement in fraud-detection accuracy, presented as indicative rather than fully validated results.
    [codes: C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-034, C-045, C-046, C-052]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The authors repeatedly compare quantum and classical approaches at the level of complexity, runtime, and accuracy: they characterize the billing optimization as NP-hard on classical systems while suggesting that QAOA-based formulations may be more tractable for specialized instances, and note that Grover’s O(√N) complexity outperforms classical O(N) search for fraud detection over large healthcare datasets. They qualitatively assert that classical systems may take minutes to hours for tasks that estimated quantum circuits could perform in tens of seconds, and a comparison table reports higher fraud-detection accuracy and much faster overall processing for the quantum system. They also argue that quantum systems may have dramatically lower long-term operational costs for large-scale optimization and search tasks, while acknowledging higher integration and energy costs at present.
    [codes: C-007, C-031, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-024, C-045, C-046, C-043, C-044, C-027]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper acknowledges several limitations and barriers: current quantum hardware is in the NISQ era, with limited qubit counts, significant decoherence, and nontrivial error rates that cap the size and complexity of solvable billing problems. High costs, limited availability of qubit resources, and difficulties integrating quantum solutions with existing legacy billing systems are cited as major adoption constraints, compounded by the need for specialized expertise in both quantum software and healthcare informatics. The authors also highlight operational trade-offs, such as high energy demand due to cryogenic cooling, implicitly limiting scalability and practical deployment in the near term.
    [codes: C-047, C-048, C-049, C-051, C-044, C-043]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Looking ahead, the authors suggest that future improvements in quantum hardware and more streamlined algorithms could increase the practical benefits of their proposed methods for healthcare billing and fraud detection. They also posit that, as the technology matures and operational costs decline for specialized optimization and search tasks, the long-term economic case for quantum adoption in such workflows may become stronger.
    [codes: C-036, C-050]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper positions itself as a theoretical and algorithmic exploration of how quantum computing could transform U.S. healthcare billing by jointly addressing optimization of coding/workflows, fraud detection, and data security within a unified framework. It claims novelty in mapping billing optimization to QAOA-based QUBO/Hamiltonian formulations, proposing quantum-enhanced fraud detection via Grover’s algorithm and QSVMs, and arguing that quantum cryptography can help maintain HIPAA-compliant security, all motivated by system-level benefits for patients, firms, and the broader healthcare economy.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-004, C-008, C-009, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-029, C-030, C-032, C-033, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-011]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study is largely theoretical and simulation-based: it relies on high-level formulations (e.g., QUBO-style optimization and asymptotic complexity for Grover search), benchmark-style runtime equations based on assumed gate throughput and efficiency factors, and illustrative external examples rather than detailed empirical experiments on concrete billing datasets. The procedure section mentions the use of anonymized, HIPAA-compliant billing data and defines performance metrics (accuracy, speed, cost-effectiveness), but does not specify particular datasets, splits, or preprocessing pipelines. Quantum cryptographic techniques are evaluated via simulation, and comparative tables present performance figures without fully documented experimental protocols, indicating limited reproducibility and an emphasis on conceptual feasibility over rigorous empirical validation.
    [codes: C-004, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-032, C-033, C-035, C-037, C-038, C-045, C-046, C-052]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper includes a comparative table of computing paradigms that summarizes integration trade-offs, cost, power use, and security characteristics of quantum versus classical approaches more broadly, situating healthcare billing applications within a larger technological landscape.
    [codes: C-043]

=== PAPER 0757b9aa9a3b (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper tackles feature selection for financial risk assessment tasks—specifically fraud and credit default detection—by formulating feature selection as a Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization (QUBO) problem to be solved on quantum annealers. The motivation is that high-dimensional datasets with irrelevant or redundant features are computationally expensive to train on and can cause overfitting, while feature selection is NP-hard for classical computers, suggesting potential benefits from quantum optimization in these finance applications.
    [codes: C-001, C-004, C-005, C-006, C-007]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors use quantum annealing as the quantum computing paradigm, arguing it is well-suited to solving QUBO formulations with relatively strong reliability despite being less versatile than gate-based devices. They implement feature selection by building QUBO matrices from information-theoretic and correlation-based criteria, then solve them on D-Wave hardware via a hybrid solver that decomposes larger, densely connected QUBOs into smaller subproblems due to current QPU connectivity and size limits; feature subsets are then evaluated by training ML models and measuring F1 and Gini.
    [codes: C-001, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-024, C-025]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Experiments indicate that QUBO-based feature selection on quantum computers can, in aggregate, perform better than classical feature-selection methods on the evaluated fraud and default datasets, though the gains are often modest and formulation- and k-dependent. For small fraud data with k=13, parametric-MI QUBO yields the best F1 while MI-Pearson gives the best Gini, whereas at k=25 a classical MI-Spearman method dominates F1 and Pearson-QUBO dominates Gini; MIQUBO is the worst technique on the small fraud dataset but performs best on the larger fraud dataset when only a small number of features are selected. Across datasets, MIQUBO, MI-Pearson, and parametric-MI slightly outperform a full-feature baseline and, on the default dataset, MIQUBO, parametric-MI QUBO, and MI-Pearson QUBO show similar ML performance for smaller feature subsets. The authors also find that the parametric scaling (alpha) materially affects performance when only a small number of features are selected but becomes largely irrelevant as k increases.
    [codes: C-003, C-018, C-019, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The study explicitly compares quantum QUBO feature selection against classical approaches including Random Forest feature importance and mutual-information plus Spearman correlation feature selection, as well as a classical full-feature baseline. The authors state that QUBO-based quantum feature selection consistently performs better than classical methods overall, but detailed results show a mixed picture: on the small fraud dataset, classical MI-Spearman dominates F1 at k=25 while QUBO formulations lead for some other k and metrics, MIQUBO can be worst in certain regimes, and on larger datasets the main quantum QUBO methods only slightly outperform the baseline. They also report that simulated annealing, a classical optimizer, achieves similar performance to quantum annealing for parametric-MI QUBO, which they attribute to analogous mechanisms for escaping local minima.
    [codes: C-003, C-008, C-012, C-028, C-029, C-031, C-032, C-035]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge that current quantum annealers have limited connectivity and can only handle small or sparsely connected QUBOs directly, necessitating the use of a hybrid solver that decomposes larger problems, which constrains scalability and may dilute any pure quantum contribution. They further note that one of their parametric formulations may introduce an artificial increase in distances, and suggest that the scaling factor might be compensating for this artifact, indicating a modeling limitation. The discussion also concedes that quantum annealing and simulated annealing exhibit comparable performance on their instances, implying that any quantum advantage is not observed under current hardware and problem settings.
    [codes: C-024, C-025, C-037, C-035]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The authors propose that quantum advantage for these QUBO-based feature-selection methods is more likely to emerge on problem instances whose energy landscapes have tall, narrow barriers, provided that coherence in quantum annealers improves substantially. They thus suggest future work on both hardware development and targeting harder optimization landscapes where quantum tunneling could yield performance beyond classical techniques.
    [codes: C-036]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims to introduce three new QUBO formulations for feature selection that improve efficiency and flexibility of downstream ML models: a parametric mutual-information-based QUBO grounded in the mRMR framework, a hybrid MI-Pearson QUBO that combines linear and nonlinear dependence measures with lower computational complexity than traditional MIQUBO, and a parametric distance-correlation-based QUBO designed to efficiently handle continuous variables. They also emphasize that parametric control (via alpha) in these formulations allows practitioners to adjust the trade-off between relevance, redundancy, and cardinality without explicitly specifying the number of selected features, especially affecting performance when only a few features can be chosen.
    [codes: C-002, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-018, C-019, C-033]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the authors construct QUBO matrices from various dependence measures (mutual information, Pearson, Spearman, distance correlation) and then evaluate the selected feature subsets by training ML models and reporting F1 and Gini scores on held-out test sets. They explicitly include classical feature selection baselines such as Random Forest importance and MI+Spearman, and describe preprocessing steps that remove non-numeric, zero-variance, and all-NaN features before QUBO construction. The paper also discusses computational complexity and notes that the newly proposed parametric-MI and MI-Pearson QUBOs have lower complexity than MIQUBO, making them more practical for large datasets, and it provides some analysis of how the parametric scaling influences performance as a function of the number of selected features.
    [codes: C-011, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-020, C-022, C-023, C-025, C-026, C-018, C-019, C-033]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper offers additional interpretive insights into why certain formulations behave differently: for example, it suggests that MIQUBO underperforms on the small fraud dataset because it emphasizes higher-order interactions at the expense of linear relationships, and notes that parameterization effects are more pronounced when constrained to small feature sets; it also highlights that similar performance between quantum and simulated annealing likely stems from analogous local-minima escape mechanisms. These points contextualize when specific QUBO formulations and solvers may or may not be advantageous in practice.
    [codes: C-029, C-035, C-037, C-038]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is directly relevant to fraud_detection because it studies feature selection for fraud datasets using QUBO formulations on quantum annealers, with explicit evaluation on fraud classification metrics (F1 and Gini). Its main value for the silo is showing which quantum/classical feature-selection methods work best under small feature budgets, a common constraint in fraud detection models.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: QUBO-based feature selection is applied directly to fraud detection datasets and evaluated with fraud-relevant metrics.
    [codes: C-001, C-011, C-004  dim_source: dimension_1]
  KEY_POINT 2: The parametric-MI formulation is especially useful when only a small number of features can be selected.
    [codes: C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019  dim_source: dimension_7]
  KEY_POINT 3: On the small fraud dataset, different methods win depending on the feature budget, with parametric-MI best at k=13 and classical MI-Spearman best F1 at k=25.
    [codes: C-027, C-028, C-029, C-031  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 4: MI-Pearson is attractive for fraud detection because it combines linear and nonlinear dependence while being simpler to compute than MIQUBO.
    [codes: C-020, C-021, C-033  dim_source: dimension_8]
  KEY_POINT 5: The paper’s quantum advantage claim is limited in practice because quantum annealing and simulated annealing perform similarly on these instances.
    [codes: C-024, C-025, C-035, C-036  dim_source: dimension_5]
  PRIMARY_CODES: C-001, C-011, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-024, C-025, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-031, C-033, C-035, C-036

  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames a core problem in finance as the inability of current FinTech and digital asset infrastructures to remain secure, scalable, and computationally efficient under rapidly growing transaction volumes and data complexity, especially for tasks such as real-time risk assessment, portfolio optimization, and transaction verification. It argues that traditional computational models are constrained by classical encryption limits and exponential data growth, creating systemic inefficiencies across digital asset ecosystems.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-011]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors conceptually examine several quantum algorithms—specifically QAOA, Quantum Fourier Transform, and Grover’s search—as candidate methods to enhance financial operations such as market forecasting, fraud detection, and asset pricing, without detailing concrete implementations. They also propose hybrid quantum–classical frameworks as an architectural direction to build more sustainable, adaptive, and transparent financial systems, and further investigate quantum-resistant cryptography as a complementary security layer for digital asset networks.
    [codes: C-005, C-006, C-007]

No quantitative performance metrics or experimental benchmarks are provided.
    [codes: C-008, C-015]

However, it does not present side-by-side empirical comparisons or benchmarks.

    [codes: C-009]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper’s contribution is presented as a conceptual examination of the transformative potential of quantum computing for reshaping financial technology infrastructures and digital asset ecosystems, including a discussion of how specific quantum algorithms and quantum-resistant cryptography might support more secure, scalable, and adaptive financial systems. It positions this as part of defining a paradigm of quantum financial intelligence in the context of accelerating digital finance transformation.
    [codes: C-002, C-004, C-005, C-006, C-007, C-009, C-010]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: The work is purely conceptual and does not report implemented quantum circuits, datasets, or experiments; accordingly, there is no discussion of experimental design, reproducibility practices, or data quality. The focus is on explaining foundational quantum properties such as superposition and entanglement and mapping them to financial use cases, rather than on empirical validation.
    [codes: C-013, C-014, C-015]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper situates its analysis within the broader context of the digital transformation of finance, driven by blockchain, artificial intelligence, and decentralized finance, and notes that the expansion of digital asset ecosystems is stressing existing computational frameworks for processing, securing, and optimizing complex financial operations.
    [codes: C-010, C-011]

=== PAPER 11ff02556b47 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames quantum support vector machines as tools for both classification and regression, applying them to benchmark image and fraud-detection datasets as well as a financial time‑series prediction task. Classification is posed as quantum support vector classification (QSVC), while real‑valued prediction tasks, including a financial regression dataset, are formulated as quantum support vector regression (QSVR) using a quantum kernel derived from a shallow circuit.
    [codes: C-001, C-019, C-026, C-027]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors use a quantum kernel method for QSVC and QSVR based on a shallow quantum feature map implemented as an angle‑encoding circuit with nearest‑neighbor entangling interactions, which defines the quantum kernel matrix used in SVM dual optimization. They simulate the method with both noiseless and noisy quantum‑circuit models that incorporate depolarizing noise and gate errors and then execute the same circuits on the IonQ Harmony trapped‑ion device; kernel alignment is computed via the Frobenius inner product to compare noisy and ideal kernels. Implementation details include use of an in‑house SDK (PhiQonnect), 500 shots per kernel entry, task‑specific training/test splits, and a scaling hyperparameter λ in the feature map, along with PCA and Box–Cox transformations for the materials regression dataset.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-007, C-008, C-010, C-011, C-012, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-023, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: For classification, QSVC on 4 qubits of IonQ Harmony achieved test accuracies (70% on credit‑card, 100% on MNIST and Fashion‑MNIST) comparable to noiseless simulations, with hardware kernel alignment values around or above 0.98, which the authors associate with reliable predictions. For regression, noiseless QSVR on the financial dataset slightly outperformed classical SVR in R², but hardware noise reduced R² to 0.868 on the financial dataset and to 0.628 on the materials dataset, with the latter more strongly affected by noise. Applying low‑rank approximation to noisy kernels and tuning ε and C in ε‑SVR improved hardware QSVR performance, with optimal ranks and hyperparameters reported for both financial and materials datasets.
    [codes: C-003, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-022, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The authors report that QSVC performance on IonQ Harmony was comparable to classical SVM classifiers on the same datasets. For regression, they state that the coefficient of determination for the classical SVR model on the financial dataset (R² = 0.930) was essentially matched or slightly exceeded by the noiseless QSVR model (R² = 0.932), while hardware‑executed QSVR showed degraded R² relative to both classical SVR and noiseless quantum simulations, particularly for the materials dataset.
    [codes: C-016, C-017, C-026, C-027, C-028]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The discussion notes that the scalability of quantum kernel methods is still an open question, warning that increasing qubit counts and two‑qubit gates may substantially degrade quantum kernel values and hence performance. The authors explicitly acknowledge that their chosen quantum feature map does not necessarily demonstrate any quantum advantage, positioning the work as a robustness and feasibility study rather than an advantage claim. They also observe that regression models are more acutely affected by noise than classification, implicitly limiting near‑term applicability of QSVR on current hardware.
    [codes: C-029, C-032, C-033, C-034]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work is implied around addressing open questions of scalability for quantum kernels, including understanding how increasing qubit numbers and entangling gates affect kernel values and exploring whether alternative, potentially more expressive feature maps could yield quantum advantage while retaining noise robustness.
    [codes: C-032, C-033, C-034]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper’s contribution lies in demonstrating that a shallow‑circuit quantum kernel can be effectively used for both QSVC and QSVR across diverse datasets, with empirical evidence of resistance to realistic noise on a trapped‑ion device, and in proposing kernel alignment as a practical indicator of prediction reliability under noise, together with a low‑rank kernel approximation combined with ε‑SVR hyperparameter tuning as an effective mitigation strategy for noisy QSVR. They also link the absence of exponential kernel concentration and successful training to the use of low‑depth circuits.
    [codes: C-004, C-005, C-006, C-014, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-030, C-031]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study employs both noiseless and noisy simulations and real trapped‑ion hardware, using an explicit depolarizing plus thermal‑relaxation noise model and quantifying noise impact via kernel alignment metrics. Experimental rigor is supported by clear reporting of circuit structure, shot counts per kernel entry (500), small but explicitly stated training/test sample sizes that vary by task, and detailed preprocessing such as PCA and Box–Cox transformations for high‑dimensional materials data. The availability of the in‑house SDK PhiQonnect and description of hyperparameter grid searches further support reproducibility, while observations about differences in the number of support vectors between noiseless and hardware kernels highlight careful model analysis beyond simple accuracy metrics.
    [codes: C-002, C-007, C-008, C-011, C-012, C-014, C-015, C-018, C-020, C-021, C-023, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: Aside from small observed differences in the number of support vectors between noiseless and noisy QSVC models on hardware, which the authors note without extensive interpretation, all other coded elements fit within the dimensions above.
    [codes: C-018]
[SILO OVERLAY]

=== PAPER 14777484b99d (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets unsupervised anomaly detection by proposing a quantum version of the Local Outlier Factor (LOF) algorithm, motivated by the high computational cost of classical LOF on large, high‑dimensional datasets. The authors frame the task as computing k‑distance neighborhoods, local reachability densities, and local outlier factors for unlabeled data points within a quantum computational model.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-007, C-028]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The quantum approach mirrors the three classical LOF steps, but replaces them with quantum subroutines: amplitude estimation and minimum search for k‑distance neighborhood determination, QRAM‑based parallel computation of local reachability density via a quantum multiply‑adder, and parallel LOF estimation via amplitude estimation followed by Grover search to locate abnormal data points. The method assumes data stored in QRAM with O(log mn) access, uses quantum adders and multiply‑adders with specified gate complexities, and encodes distance information into computational basis states to enable arithmetic in superposition.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-004, C-005, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-016, C-018, C-019, C-025]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The authors provide asymptotic complexity and error analyses for each step of the quantum LOF algorithm. They report step‑1 complexity O[max_i{n_i}·m^{3/2}·log(mn)/(ε₁ε₂)] with a specified distance‑estimation error bound, step‑2 complexity O[max{n_i}·log(m·max{n_i})], and step‑3 complexity O(√m·T·max{n_i}·log(m·max{n_i})/ε₃) with derived LOF error bounds. Aggregating these, they state an overall quantum LOF complexity and claim polynomial speedup in the number of data points m and exponential speedup in the data dimension n relative to the classical LOF algorithm.
    [codes: C-006, C-008, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-017, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper explicitly contrasts the quantum algorithm with the classical LOF baseline, noting that classical LOF is computationally expensive on big datasets and giving a stepwise classical complexity of O(m²nk) for neighborhood computation and O(mk) for density and LOF computation. Against this baseline, the authors argue that their quantum method achieves polynomial speedup in m and exponential speedup in n, based on the detailed quantum–classical complexity comparison for each step.
    [codes: C-007, C-008, C-014, C-017, C-019, C-022, C-023]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge several limitations and restrictive assumptions underlying their analysis. They explicitly state that selecting an appropriate neighborhood parameter k is crucial but outside the scope of their discussion. For the complexity analysis, they assume each data point is represented by a constant number of qubits, which constrains how data dimensionality is treated. In the LOF error analysis, they further assume that at least half of the neighbor‑distance values exceed a constant √P, which simplifies the derivation but may not hold universally.
    [codes: C-029, C-030, C-031]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: For future work, the authors suggest that the step‑2 routine for parallel local reachability density computation could be reused as a subroutine in other quantum clustering and dimensionality‑reduction algorithms, indicating broader algorithmic applicability. They also propose that their idea of encoding distance information into the computational basis could extend to other machine‑learning problems, such as density estimation and feature learning, and express the hope that their techniques will inspire more quantum algorithms with advantage for anomaly detection, particularly in unsupervised settings.
    [codes: C-024, C-026, C-027, C-028]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The contribution is framed as the design of a dedicated quantum LOF algorithm for unsupervised anomaly detection that closely parallels the classical three‑step LOF pipeline while leveraging quantum subroutines to obtain formal speedup guarantees. The novelty lies in combining QRAM‑based data access, quantum arithmetic (adder and multiply‑adder), amplitude estimation, and Grover search to compute LOF and identify anomalies in parallel with theoretically established polynomial speedup in m and exponential speedup in n compared with classical LOF.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-004, C-005, C-022, C-023, C-025, C-027]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the work is purely theoretical: it specifies circuit‑level building blocks (QRAM access with O(log mn) time, quantum adder and multiply‑adder with O(n²) and O(n³) complexity, respectively) and derives stepwise and overall asymptotic runtimes and error bounds, but does not report empirical experiments or datasets. Error tolerances for amplitude estimation are explicitly parameterized, and the analysis tracks how these propagate into bounds on distance and LOF approximation, indicating a focus on analytical rigor rather than implementation or reproducibility on current hardware.
    [codes: C-010, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-017, C-019, C-020, C-021]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: Beyond specific speedups, the authors highlight as a cross‑cutting methodological insight that encoding distance information into computational basis states enables the use of quantum multiply‑adders to compute means of reachability distances in superposition, and they suggest this idea could generalize to other machine‑learning primitives.
    [codes: C-025]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to fraud_detection because it presents an unsupervised anomaly-detection method (quantum LOF) aimed at finding outliers in unlabeled data, which aligns with fraud and AML settings where suspicious cases are rare and labels are often unavailable. Its main value for this silo is the proposed speedup for large, high-dimensional datasets and the explicit focus on anomaly identification rather than supervised classification.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: Quantum LOF is explicitly framed as unsupervised anomaly detection, making it directly applicable to spotting unusual transactions or accounts without labeled fraud examples.
    [codes: C-001, C-028  dim_source: dimension_1]
  KEY_POINT 2: The method is designed to find k-distance neighborhoods, local reachability densities, and LOF scores in parallel, which is the core workflow for ranking suspicious points as anomalies.
    [codes: C-002, C-004, C-005, C-016, C-018  dim_source: dimension_2]
  KEY_POINT 3: The paper’s main claim is polynomial speedup in the number of data points and exponential speedup in data dimension versus classical LOF, which is important for large-scale fraud screening workloads.
    [codes: C-006, C-022, C-023, C-008  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 4: The work is purely theoretical and depends on QRAM plus specific arithmetic subroutines, so its fraud-detection value is currently conceptual rather than deployment-ready.
    [codes: C-009, C-010, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-017, C-019, C-020, C-021  dim_source: dimension_8]
  KEY_POINT 5: The authors note that choosing k is crucial but outside scope, and they assume constant-qubit data representation and a simplifying neighbor-distance condition, which limits how directly the method can be transferred to real fraud data.

=== PAPER 1be7c19fc967 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper addresses credit fraud detection in high‑volume, real‑time payment systems, arguing that traditional rule‑based and ML approaches—constrained by static thresholds, dependence on labelled data, vulnerability to adversarial manipulation, and difficulty handling class imbalance—are increasingly inadequate for adaptive, real‑time anomaly detection. It frames the problem as detecting rare and evolving fraudulent transaction patterns under compressed detection‑response windows and regulatory interpretability pressures, motivating a need for smart, dynamic solutions that can operate at the scale of billions of transactions per day.
    [codes: C-001, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-033]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The quantum component is based on gate‑based quantum machine learning, specifically Quantum Support Vector Machines (QSVMs) and quantum neural networks used as anomaly detectors over high‑dimensional transaction data. Transaction features are encoded into quantum states via quantum‑friendly feature mappings such as angle and amplitude encoding, enabling parallel evaluation of many classification or anomaly‑detection hypotheses; quantum optimization is also proposed to select efficient intervention strategies over large decision spaces. The architecture includes a quantum detection engine within a dual‑layered hybrid system, with simulations implemented using Qiskit on unspecified quantum simulators.
    [codes: C-003, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-025, C-026, C-036, C-038]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: In simulator‑based experiments on credit card transaction data enriched with synthetic fraud, the hybrid QC + GenAI model is reported to achieve 97.8% accuracy with 2.3% false positives and 3.1% false negatives, and an inference latency of 27 ms, outperforming both traditional Random Forest and standalone QSVM or GAN+RF baselines. The authors state that this hybrid model significantly reduces both false positives and false negatives, delivers sub‑30 ms latency suitable for real‑time payment gateways and digital wallets, and exhibits superior adversarial robustness due to continuous synthetic fraud simulation and adversarial training. They further claim that these results indicate the transformative potential of integrating quantum computing and GenAI to construct next‑generation security systems for faster and more accurate fraud prediction and defense.
    [codes: C-005, C-007, C-020, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-048, C-051, C-052]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper benchmarks the hybrid QC + GenAI framework against traditional machine‑learning‑only and GenAI‑only baselines, reporting that the hybrid system outperforms them across accuracy, false positive rate, false negative rate, latency, and adversarial robustness. A Random Forest baseline is reported at 91.6% accuracy with 6.8% false positives, 8.2% false negatives, and 72 ms latency, and is criticized for high false positives that can cause customer dissatisfaction and transaction friction. A QSVM‑only model and a GAN‑enhanced Random Forest baseline perform better than pure Random Forest but are still inferior to the hybrid in both predictive performance and latency, with GenAI‑only models described as constrained by classical computational limits.
    [codes: C-042, C-043, C-044, C-046, C-047, C-048, C-049, C-050]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge several limitations and challenges. On the quantum side, they note hardware immaturity, decoherence and error‑correction challenges, and the scarcity and limited scalability of large‑scale quantum hardware, emphasizing that current work relies on simulators or early‑stage quantum cloud platforms. They also point to uncertainty because quantum algorithms are largely unregulated and to infrastructural and skills barriers, since deploying QC–GenAI systems requires expertise spanning quantum physics, AI development, and financial compliance. On the AI side, they highlight that GenAI models, especially for synthetic data generation, can be difficult to interpret and may raise bias and governance concerns. At the research landscape level, they state that most existing implementations are siloed (focusing on either GenAI or quantum), real‑time performance benchmarks in live environments are underreported, and compliance, interpretability, and ethical implications of quantum‑GenAI systems in regulated industries remain underexplored.
    [codes: C-021, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-047, C-054, C-055, C-056, C-057]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work envisioned by the authors includes developing more autonomous, self‑learning fraud detection agents that can adapt in real time and continuously learn from evolving fraud patterns, while ensuring strong ethical safeguards, cross‑sector collaboration, and supportive regulatory environments. They also call for full‑scale application of the proposed framework in production settings, establishing long‑term performance monitoring mechanisms in live environments, and designing responsible AI and policy frameworks that can appropriately govern and enable quantum innovation in financial security.
    [codes: C-058, C-059, C-060]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims novelty in proposing and simulating an integrated QC + GenAI hybrid architecture for credit fraud detection, addressing a literature gap where most prior work treats quantum computing and GenAI in a siloed manner rather than combining them. Its contribution is framed as a design‑science‑driven dual‑layered system that leverages quantum‑accelerated anomaly detection together with GenAI‑based synthetic fraud generation, federated learning, and adversarial risk‑mitigation techniques to enable proactive, real‑time anomaly detection and dynamic risk evaluation, with an emphasis on regulatory alignment via explainable AI and privacy‑preserving mechanisms.
    [codes: C-002, C-005, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-037]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the work follows a design‑science approach that combines theoretical analysis with system architecture modeling and component‑level simulation, rather than a full production deployment. The architecture comprises multiple layers—a quantum detection engine using QSVMs and quantum neural networks, a generative engine using GANs and transformer models to synthesize fraud scenarios, and an adversarial defense layer employing differential privacy, adversarial training, model regularization, and secure federated learning—to both augment data and harden models. Evaluation is described as a benchmark comparison between traditional ML‑only, GenAI‑enhanced, QSVM‑only, and the QC + GenAI hybrid models on real and synthetic fraud data, using metrics such as accuracy, false positive/negative rates, latency, and adversarial robustness, but the paper does not provide dataset identifiers, sizes, or detailed splits, and all quantum experiments run on simulators. Compliance considerations are methodologically embedded via explainable AI, differential privacy, and federated learning to satisfy mandates such as GDPR and PCI‑DSS.
    [codes: C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-027, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-053]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: None.
    [codes: —]

=== PAPER 1bf5fc02b95b (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames anomaly detection as a setting with scarce, highly imbalanced anomalous labels, motivating unsupervised or semi‑supervised methods that train only on normal data and detect anomalies via poor reconstruction. It positions quantum autoencoders as a reconstruction‑based anomaly detector that can be particularly suitable when labeled anomalies are unavailable, and targets healthcare (breast cancer diagnosis) and financial fraud detection as core application domains. The authors further argue that leveraging quantum properties such as superposition and entanglement may help address these imbalanced‑data challenges.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-016, C-019, C-023, C-029, C-051]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The method is a quantum autoencoder workflow that amplitude‑encodes classical features into log(N) qubits, uses a variational encoder–decoder pair with the decoder defined as the inverse of the encoder, and splits qubits into latent and “trash” qubits that are reset during compression. Anomaly scores come from reconstruction fidelity, evaluated via fidelity calculations in simulation and SWAP tests on hardware; training optimizes the encoder to maximize fidelity for normal data and stops when trash qubits are reliably reset and normal‑data fidelity is maximized. The proposed QEA‑FAD architecture uses variational quantum circuits instead of classical neural networks, employs angle and amplitude embeddings, introduces shared Bell pairs and direct entanglement with mid‑circuit measurement and classical control for coordination, and explores patch‑based and entanglement‑assisted designs, evaluated across healthcare and finance datasets.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-015, C-017, C-020, C-021, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-042, C-046]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The authors report that their entanglement‑ and patch‑enhanced QAE architectures achieve improved precision, better correlations between encoding and decoding, and statistically significant performance gains while using fewer parameters and fewer training iterations compared with baselines. On the breast cancer task, the proposed ansatz achieves around 90% accuracy in identifying dangerous (malignant) cells using a fidelity split threshold of 0.845 and outperforms two recreated prior ansätze that attain malign‑class accuracy of 0.24 and 0.81, respectively. For credit‑card fraud, they obtain a simulator‑level compression fidelity of about 0.89 for non‑fraudulent and 0.66 for fraudulent transactions, with hardware and simulation fidelity distributions compared; they also claim JAX‑accelerated optimization reduces training time on financial datasets and that MNIST benchmarks support scalability, though detailed metrics are not fully specified.
    [codes: C-005, C-007, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-019, C-022, C-023, C-043, C-045, C-047, C-048, C-049, C-050, C-051, C-054]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The study explicitly benchmarks the quantum autoencoder models against classical autoencoders, claiming comparable or better anomaly‑detection performance while using fewer parameters and fewer training iterations. However, the codes do not provide explicit classical performance numbers or detailed baseline architectures; the comparative statement remains largely qualitative at the code level.
    [codes: C-013, C-018, C-022]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge that straightforward uses of entanglement did not reliably improve performance: one entanglement‑driven design was too restrictive and performed worse than a non‑entangled version, and a second non‑interacting entanglement attempt had minimal effect, leading them to conclude that entanglement alone does not clearly enhance QAEs. In the credit‑card fraud hardware experiments, they report that fidelity‑histogram overlap on IBM’s Guadalupe processor makes accurate transaction classification challenging, and that separation is further reduced on Rigetti’s Aspen‑11 QPU relative to the simulator. In the conclusion they state that improvements from entanglement were more evident only in interconnected encoder designs rather than from entanglement by itself, reinforcing that their entanglement strategy remains sub‑optimal and sensitive to current NISQ hardware noise and constraints.
    [codes: C-040, C-041, C-052, C-053, C-055]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The paper proposes future work on optimizing how entanglement is used within quantum autoencoders, suggesting that better‑designed entanglement patterns might yield clearer benefits. It also emphasizes the need to improve NISQ‑era compatibility to make their methods more robust and scalable for practical anomaly‑detection applications in domains like finance and healthcare.
    [codes: C-056, C-057]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The main contribution claimed is an entanglement‑enhanced quantum autoencoder architecture (QEA‑FAD) that aims to optimize correlations between encoding and decoding via shared Bell pairs, direct qubit entanglement, and mid‑circuit measurement with classical control, combined with patch‑based designs to boost anomaly‑detection accuracy. The authors also present a hybrid evaluation methodology that pairs fidelity tests in simulation with SWAP tests on real QPUs, and claim that their approach delivers statistically significant anomaly‑detection gains with fewer parameters and training iterations, demonstrated on benchmark datasets in healthcare and finance where prior QAE work on such complex, real‑world benchmarks was limited.
    [codes: C-005, C-006, C-017, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-028, C-029, C-043, C-049]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the paper structures its approach into four components: training QAEs only on non‑anomalous data, using fidelity and SWAP‑test‑based scoring, enhancing architectures (patch‑based and entanglement‑assisted), and benchmarking on healthcare and finance datasets. State preparation uses amplitude encoding to pack many features into log(N) qubits, with angle and amplitude embeddings described for different encoders, and training continues until trash qubits are reliably reset and fidelity on normal data is maximized. Datasets include Breast Cancer Wisconsin (Diagnostic), a credit‑card fraud benchmark, and MNIST as a scalability check; the fraud objective is explicitly to maximize non‑fraud fidelity and minimize fraud fidelity, and classification decisions are made by thresholding compression fidelity. They also compare simulation and real‑hardware runs (IBM and Rigetti) and state that JAX‑accelerated optimization reduces training time, but explicit hyperparameters, classical autoencoder baselines, and detailed resource counts are not fully specified in the codes, which may limit reproducibility.
    [codes: C-017, C-021, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-044, C-046, C-050, C-051, C-054, C-008, C-009]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: None.
    [codes: —]

[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames the problem as improving intelligent supervision and risk identification in bank cross-border payment systems by applying quantum machine learning, specifically K-Means-based clustering, to detect risk characteristics and abnormal transactions. It motivates this by pointing to a research gap in concrete bank payment-system applications and aims to enhance regulatory efficiency, data security, and risk management in this context.
    [codes: C-001, C-006, C-009, C-010, C-033]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The approach combines the high parallelism of quantum computing with K-Means clustering, framed as an unsupervised learning method where assignment to the nearest cluster center and iterative E/M updates implement the K-Means objective. The methodological exposition is largely classical—covering distance-based similarity, parametric and nonparametric density estimation, histogram, kernel, and k-nearest-neighbor density estimation—and then positions a "quantum K-Means" variant conceptually on top of this, without specifying concrete quantum resource details in the codes.
    [codes: C-002, C-008, C-011, C-013, C-016, C-017, C-019, C-021, C-026, C-029, C-031]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The authors claim that experiments across different scenarios verify the effectiveness and reliability of their model for cross-border payment supervision, reporting that the quantum K-Means model significantly improves the accuracy and efficiency of payment risk identification. They further state that their quantum K-Means algorithm is superior in both data processing speed and abnormal transaction recognition accuracy, and that when processing millions of transaction records, quantum acceleration notably improves clustering efficiency and reduces false positive rates. Within an illustrative example, they also note convergence after the third M step of the K-Means iterations and observe that an indirect dynamic clustering algorithm is very efficient.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-018, C-034, C-040, C-041]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper explicitly compares its quantum K-Means method to traditional (classical) approaches, asserting that the quantum version is superior in both data processing speed and accuracy of abnormal transaction recognition, especially at the scale of millions of transactions where quantum acceleration is said to improve clustering efficiency and reduce false positives. It also contrasts indirect dynamic clustering’s high efficiency with its weakness in reflecting the true probability structure of the data, suggesting that the proposed approach aims to mitigate such shortcomings.
    [codes: C-034, C-035, C-040, C-041]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge multiple limitations. At the quantum implementation level, they note that current quantum computing hardware is limited, making practical application challenging and experimental verification difficult. They emphasize that practical deployment still faces challenges such as immature hardware and heterogeneous regulatory standards across countries. Methodologically, they list several statistical and clustering-related issues: model selection for density functions, unobservable variable problems, and the difficulty of high-dimensional parameter estimation ("dimensional disaster"). For nonparametric methods, they highlight the sensitivity of histogram estimates to bin width and their poor scalability to high dimensions, fixed kernel width limitations in kernel density estimation, and the trade-off in choosing K in k-nearest-neighbor density estimation that affects both accuracy and computational cost. For clustering dynamics, they state that indirect dynamic clustering often fails to reflect the probability structure of data, that noise can change the number of density peaks, and that with small samples it may be impossible to estimate the probability density function.
    [codes: C-007, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-027, C-028, C-030, C-032, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-042]

The authors also call for exploration of applications in different regulatory environments, indicating a need to adapt and test the method under varying jurisdictional rules.
    [codes: C-005, C-043]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims novelty in proposing a quantum machine learning method based on K-Means to optimize intelligent regulation of cross-border payments, aiming to fill a research gap in concrete bank payment-system applications of quantum machine learning. It further positions its contribution as helping to bridge the gap between existing theory and practice in quantum finance, while targeting improvements in the efficiency of payment regulatory systems, data security, and risk management.
    [codes: C-001, C-006, C-009, C-010]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: The methodological exposition is thorough on classical unsupervised learning and density estimation foundations, detailing K-Means mechanics, parametric versus nonparametric density estimation, and the influence of factors such as data construction, similarity measures, clustering criteria, and number of clusters on classification outcomes. However, the codes do not report concrete dataset specifications or quantum resource settings; instead, they describe verification "through experiments" at a high level and rely heavily on conceptual analysis and model construction. The authors also stress that effective use of domain expertise is needed to fill information gaps and to interpret cluster meanings, underscoring that cluster outputs alone are insufficient without financial-domain context.

  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper emphasizes that domain expertise is crucial for understanding and leveraging clustering results in financial applications: attention must be given to using application-specific expertise to fill information gaps, and the actual meaning of clusters is determined by relevant knowledge rather than by the algorithm alone.

=== PAPER 291abba8a19e (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets fraud and anomaly detection in IoT-assisted financial transaction networks, framed as a need for a pipeline that combines anomaly detection with verifiability and auditability rather than optimizing a single accuracy metric. It formulates the problem as ensuring that fraud detection outcomes are cryptographically verifiable, resistant to identity-correlation and replay attacks, and grounded in a trustworthy end-to-end channel between enrolled devices and on-chain decisions.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-005, C-020]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The quantum component uses GHZ–θ quantum state verification followed by a quantum support vector classifier/variational circuit that encodes fraud features via a ZZFeatureMap and a trainable ansatz composed of RY, RZ, and CX gates under a four-qubit constraint. GHZ fidelity checks with a threshold (F < 0.5 leads to rejection) are integrated before learning, and accepted samples are processed by the QSVC/VQC-style classifier with a fraud decision threshold at score ≥ 0.75.
    [codes: C-001, C-008, C-013, C-016, C-019, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-032]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Empirically, classical baselines such as Random Forest and XGBoost achieve balanced accuracy above 77%, whereas the QSVC alone attains about 42.1 ± 2.8% balanced accuracy under the stated NISQ-era constraints. Ablation studies suggest that amplitude encoding with angle re-uploading at moderate variational depth (L ≈ 6–8) improves discrimination before saturating, and SMOTE-ENN yields the highest recall under extreme class sparsity. Despite lower balanced accuracy, QuanFraud attains the maximum composite Trust Score and is presented as prioritizing verifiability and security over raw BA.
    [codes: C-003, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-037, C-038]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The authors report that strong classical baselines (Random Forest and XGBoost) outperform QSVC in balanced accuracy, exceeding 77% BA compared to around 42% for QSVC under comparable, leakage-safe settings. They emphasize that while classical models win on discrimination metrics, QuanFraud is distinguished by its integration of GHZ/θ verification, DIDs, kernel privacy, audit trail, and calibration, which classical-only baselines lack.
    [codes: C-003, C-029, C-011, C-038]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper acknowledges several limitations of its quantum classifier: the QSVC is restricted to roughly four qubits, limiting feature expressivity and entanglement depth, which impairs separability of the fraud classes. Variational layers are susceptible to barren plateaus, leading to vanishing gradients that impede training. Device or simulator noise is noted as disturbing kernel estimates and margins, with mitigation and depth control only partially reducing drift. The authors also state that identity mechanisms alone cannot stop attacks from legitimate but compromised devices, so detection and additional controls remain necessary, and they call for further validation using n-fold (10-fold) schemes beyond their current analysis.
    [codes: C-021, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work is described as focusing on improving QML accuracy without sacrificing verifiability by investigating stronger quantum neural architectures, richer feature embeddings with re-uploading, and tuned hybrid quantum–classical models with calibrated thresholds. The authors also explicitly call for additional validation through extended n-fold (10-fold) validation mechanisms to further substantiate their findings.
    [codes: C-035, C-040]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The claimed contribution is an assurance-first, deployable NISQ-era framework that tightly integrates GHZ–θ quantum state verification, QSVC/VQC-based fraud detection, DIDs/AnonCreds identity binding, and immutable on-chain logging to provide verifiable, auditable fraud detection. The authors argue that prior work has focused either on predictive accuracy or on blockchain immutability alone and has generally lacked the combination of verification, governance, and trust scoring that QuanFraud offers.
    [codes: C-001, C-004, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-014, C-036, C-038]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the paper emphasizes a leakage-safe evaluation design: data are normalized, reduced via PCA to match limited qubits, rebalanced with SMOTE/SMOTE-ENN, and augmented with temporal features under strict train-test splits. The evaluation uses nested, stratified 10-fold cross-validation with inner-fold calibration, standard metrics (AUROC, AUPRC, F1, ECE), and confidence intervals (DeLong and BCa bootstrap). The architecture enforces identity governance via DIDs/AnonCreds before quantum scoring, integrates GHZ fidelity thresholds into Algorithm 1, and encodes features through ZZFeatureMap before applying QSVC/VQC as described in Algorithm 2. The overall Trust Score aggregates checks for kernel privacy, θ-protocol verification, DIDs, audit trails, and calibration to compare schemes, and the authors explicitly distinguish protocol hygiene from headline discrimination when presenting their validation.
    [codes: C-012, C-013, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-036]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper also discusses system-level deployment choices such as modular microservices, resource-aware scheduling, and batched quantum jobs to cope with heterogeneous IoT latency and throughput while mitigating adversarial, drift, and Sybil/collusion threats. It further contrasts its approach with post-quantum cryptography and blockchain-only systems, arguing that PQC secures communication but not anomaly detection, and that blockchain alone increases immutability but lacks detection capabilities and quantum resilience.
    [codes: C-006, C-007, C-015]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is highly relevant to fraud_detection because it frames fraud as a verifiable detection problem, not just a classification task, and couples anomaly scoring with identity governance, replay resistance, and auditability. === PAPER 2a646809136c (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper addresses bank account fraud detection as a critical issue for corporate sustainability and loss minimization, emphasizing that fraud patterns evolve over time and are inherently challenging to detect, causing substantial revenue losses and severe harm to organizations. The authors frame fraud as a Devastating Evolutionary Dynamic Entity (DEDE), characterized by evolutionary, increasingly effective methods and large financial impact, and highlight technical challenges such as strong class imbalance and high‑dimensional, non‑normal data. Fraud detection is thus formulated as a difficult, dynamic classification problem where quantum machine learning might eventually offer benefits despite current practical constraints.
    [codes: C-001, C-006, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-007]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The study uses a Quantum Support Vector Machine (QSVM) with a quantum kernel built via angle encoding, mapping eight selected, scaled features to eight qubits after removing columns with high missingness and dropping remaining missing values. Due to hardware constraints, the quantum part operates in a hybrid quantum‑classical setting on a reduced, balanced sample of 750 instances (250 fraud, 500 non‑fraud), with the quantum model trained and evaluated over six repeated runs to obtain key performance indicators. The authors attribute their quantum design choices—including feature reduction to eight qubits and stringent preprocessing—to limitations of current quantum hardware and QSVMs’ difficulty handling missing data.
    [codes: C-002, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-031, C-034]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Classical state‑of‑the‑art models, specifically XGBoost, achieve high ROC‑AUC scores between 89% and 95% on the synthetic bank‑fraud datasets. The quantum model underperforms XGBoost but is described as doing a fair job or achieving a reasonable level of performance given the restricted number of qubits, limited samples, and quantum kernel limitations. Across the biased dataset variants, KPI metrics such as F1, ROC‑AUC, precision, and recall decrease relative to the base variant, with Variant 1 showing a slight drop, Variants 2 and 3 a more significant decline, and Variants 4 and 5 recovering somewhat yet still below the base and Variant 1.
    [codes: C-003, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-035]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The classical XGBoost model, tuned via RandomSearchCV, serves as the benchmark and attains very high ROC‑AUC scores (89–95%), setting the standard for evaluating the quantum approach. The authors explicitly state that under current hardware limitations the hybrid quantum‑classical method does not outperform classical methods and that the quantum model underperforms compared to XGBoost, though they regard its constrained performance as moderately good. This comparison underscores that, for this fraud detection task and current resources, classical methods remain superior while quantum models are still exploratory.
    [codes: C-003, C-017, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-036]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors repeatedly emphasize hardware constraints as the biggest limitation, noting that current quantum computers and quantum kernels restrict the number of qubits and usable samples, and generally require substantial computational resources and large datasets to reach performance levels comparable to classical models. These constraints forced them to remove columns with over 25% missing values, reduce the feature set to eight qubits, and work with a small, balanced sample of 750 instances, which they argue contributes to weaker generalization and lower KPIs for the quantum model. They also acknowledge that repeated kernel runs and limited samples introduce additional overhead and likely impair performance, and more broadly that quantum computing is promising but currently impractical for widespread corporate deployment due to resource demands and hardware limitations.
    [codes: C-002, C-007, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-038]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The paper suggests that as quantum hardware and algorithms advance, the performance gap between classical and quantum models is expected to narrow, implying future potential improvements in fraud detection. For methodological refinement, the authors recommend using synthetic data without missing values in future studies to better assess the full potential of quantum‑enhanced methods without heavy preprocessing constraints. They further propose exploring a broader range of quantum algorithms for fraud detection beyond the specific QSVM‑based approach used in this study.
    [codes: C-037, C-039, C-040]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The authors claim conceptual novelty by introducing a classification of Devastating Evolutionary Dynamic Entities (DEDEs) and positioning fraud within this category as an evolving, harmful phenomenon that is increasingly difficult to detect. On the technical side, they present their work as an early demonstration of a hybrid quantum‑classical fraud detection pipeline using quantum encoding and a quantum SVM on a bias‑aware synthetic bank account fraud dataset, arguing that the observed moderate quantum performance under stringent resource constraints is promising evidence of quantum computing’s potential in this domain.
    [codes: C-004, C-007, C-008, C-009, C-011, C-012, C-014, C-035]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study employs a synthetic, temporally structured dataset suite with six variants designed to emulate real‑world conditions, including induced Laplacian noise, missing values, and intentionally embedded biases such as group size and prevalence disparities, to test robustness and fairness in fraud detection. The classical XGBoost model uses this rich dataset with minimal preprocessing and undergoes systematic hyperparameter tuning via RandomSearchCV with 150 iterations and 3‑fold cross‑validation, while evaluation relies on metrics suited to class‑imbalanced problems (F1, precision, recall, ROC‑AUC) whose operational meanings are explicitly discussed. In contrast, the quantum pipeline requires more aggressive preprocessing—dropping high‑missingness columns and rows, reducing features to match available qubits, and limiting samples—and is evaluated over six repeated runs to obtain reliable KPIs, reflecting both experimental care and the current practical constraints of quantum implementations.
    [codes: C-011, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper argues that traditional rule‑based decision algorithms for fraud detection are widely used yet often inadequate in adaptability and efficiency, reinforcing the motivation to explore machine learning and quantum approaches. It also reiterates that fraud detection has attracted interest as a means to ensure corporate sustainability, minimize losses, and apprehend fraudsters, and closes by asserting that overall quantum computing has great potential for fraud detection despite current limitations.
    [codes: C-001, C-005, C-010, C-036, C-004]
