=== PAPER 2a9cd8a96604 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets anomaly detection formulated as a one‑class, unsupervised learning problem, where the goal is to characterize normal data despite limited label information. It adopts the support vector data description (SVDD) perspective of enclosing normal data within a minimum‑volume hypersphere in feature space.
    [codes: C-001, C-004, C-005]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors propose Quantum Support Vector Data Description (QSVDD), which replaces classical SVDD’s feature map with a quantum feature map implemented via amplitude encoding, a variational QCNN circuit, and a final embedding/measurement stage. The QCNN ansatz is chosen for its shallow depth, logarithmic parameter scaling, trainability (including avoidance of barren plateaus), parameter sharing, and pooling that halves the number of active qubits at each stage; the hypersphere center is typically fixed at zero and the regularization term on circuit parameters is omitted. The measurement plus last convolution layer are interpreted as a parameterized measurement, and the latent feature-space dimension (number of Pauli observables) is treated as a tunable hyperparameter, ultimately fixed at nine in experiments to balance performance and computational cost.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-005, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Empirically, the authors report that setting the hypersphere center to zero is practical and effective, and that QSVDD’s performance improves as the latent-space dimension increases up to nine, after which gains saturate. On MNIST, QSVDD achieves a mean AUC of 83.13%, outperforming QAE (70.54%) and DSVDD (70.32%), and on Fashion‑MNIST it achieves a mean AUC of 80.59% versus 76.69% (QAE) and 76.05% (DSVDD). Across tasks, QSVDD shows lower standard deviations than the baselines, and the authors emphasize its logarithmic growth in circuit depth and parameter count as an efficiency and stability advantage over QAE-based approaches.
    [codes: C-003, C-011, C-013, C-020, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-030, C-038]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: Classically, SVDD and Deep SVDD (DSVDD) are used as conceptual and empirical baselines: DSVDD replaces the kernel map with a neural network but still suffers from kernel‑matrix or network scaling issues. The authors argue that kernel methods incur poor O(m^2) scaling and memory requirements, and that QSVDD, with fewer parameters, nonetheless outperforms DSVDD in most cases. They also compare to a QAE-based quantum baseline, configuring QSVDD, QAE, and DSVDD with similar parameter budgets (75, 78, and 92 parameters respectively) and noting that QAE’s parameter and depth scaling is worse than QSVDD’s. The reported mean AUCs on MNIST and Fashion‑MNIST consistently favor QSVDD over both QAE and DSVDD.
    [codes: C-006, C-007, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-030, C-036, C-037]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors explicitly acknowledge that classical kernel-based SVDD methods suffer from poor computational scaling and memory usage, motivating alternatives such as deep or quantum feature mappings. They also note that increasing the latent-space dimension raises computational time due to the larger number of Pauli observables to be measured, which leads them to cap the latent space at nine dimensions in practice. Furthermore, they stress that shallow circuits are imperative given NISQ devices’ limits on reliably executable circuit size, implicitly limiting the depth and complexity of their approach.
    [codes: C-006, C-020, C-021, C-029]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: For future work, the authors suggest that QSVDD’s advantage may become more pronounced on inherently quantum data, indicating an intended shift from classical image benchmarks to quantum-native domains. They also call for further optimization of data encoding, VQC architecture, measurement design, and classical optimization algorithms to better tailor QSVDD to specific applications. Methodologically, they propose exploring more general quantum measurements, such as POVMs, beyond projective measurements, and investigating classical-to-quantum transfer learning as an additional extension.
    [codes: C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The core contribution claimed is the introduction of Quantum Support Vector Data Description (QSVDD), an unsupervised anomaly‑detection framework that combines SVDD’s minimum‑volume hypersphere principle with a compact, shallow QCNN-based quantum feature map, whose parameter count scales logarithmically with the number of input qubits. The authors position this as a compact, trainable, and near‑term‑oriented alternative to both kernel‑based SVDD and quantum autoencoder approaches for one‑class anomaly detection.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-005, C-007, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-028, C-031]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the paper grounds QSVDD in the classical SVDD and Deep SVDD formulations and clearly specifies circuit design choices such as amplitude encoding, QCNN structure, and measurement as a parameterized readout. The authors explore the impact of varying latent feature dimension, then fix it at nine for subsequent experiments due to computational overhead from additional observables; they also ensure comparable parameter budgets across QSVDD, QAE, and DSVDD to support fair comparison. In benchmarking, they simplify the QAE anomaly detector by omitting decoding, arguing it is unnecessary for AD, and provide complete numerical QSVDD simulation results in an appendix to support reproducibility claims.
    [codes: C-005, C-007, C-008, C-012, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-029, C-036, C-038]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The authors emphasize that the compact circuit and parameter design of QSVDD—enabled by QCNN depth and parameter scaling—makes it especially suitable for implementation on near‑term quantum devices with limited resources. They also reiterate the practical importance of shallow circuits for NISQ hardware and highlight that lower variability in QSVDD’s performance compared to the baselines may be beneficial in applications requiring stable anomaly detection performance.
    [codes: C-025, C-028, C-029, C-031]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to fraud_detection because it presents an unsupervised anomaly-detection method (QSVDD) designed for settings with limited labels, which matches fraud and AML workflows where labeled fraud cases are scarce. Its emphasis on compact, shallow circuits and strong anomaly-detection performance suggests a near-term quantum approach for detecting unusual transactions or behaviors.

    [codes: C-002, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-029  dim_source: dimension_2]

    [codes: C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-030  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 4: The paper highlights practical implementation choices that support deployment-oriented anomaly detection, including fixing the hypersphere center at zero and using a latent dimension of nine to balance performance and computational cost.
    [codes: C-010, C-011, C-018, C-020, C-021  dim_source: dimension_8]
  KEY_POINT 5: The authors position QSVDD as a near-term quantum alternative to classical kernel SVDD and quantum autoencoders, both of which have scaling drawbacks.
    [codes: C-006, C-007, C-022, C-028, C-031, C-036, C-037  dim_source: dimension_4]

=== PAPER 2e35d78fa802 (type: central) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets automation of auditing and consistency checking in financial documents by reframing the task of identifying which entries sum to reported totals as instances of the NP-hard subset-sum problem over table columns or entire tables. Rather than modelling human-understood table structure, the approach treats columns as ordered sets of numbers and iteratively solves a subset-sum instance for each entry to recover the sum structure across the table.
    [codes: C-001, C-004, C-005, C-006, C-007, C-008]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: No quantum algorithm is implemented; instead, the authors cast subset-sum as a quadratic unconstrained binary optimization (QUBO) problem using binary indicator variables and corresponding quadratic coefficients, and then solve it with classical Hopfield networks performing gradient descent on the associated Ising/QUBO energy landscape. They explicitly note the equivalence of Ising and QUBO formulations and give only an outlook that this QUBO architecture could later be mapped to specialized or quantum hardware.
    [codes: C-011, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-035]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: On synthetic subset-sum benchmarks generated under various sizes and number ranges, the Hopfield-based QUBO solver 'reliably finds solutions' for almost all configurations, with failure only on the hardest regime. The authors state that single-neuron updates in the Hopfield network monotonically decrease energy and converge in finitely many steps to a local or global minimum, and empirically observe that the magnitude of numbers has a stronger impact on runtime than the number of values. On real-world financial report columns, they report that all instances are solved within the maximum iterations, likely because the real data induces mostly unique-solution subset-sum instances, and conclude overall that this optimization approach is a reliable method for subset sum in real-world auditing contexts. They further report that computation time increases with table size.
    [codes: C-002, C-018, C-024, C-028, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The authors position their method against known classical subset-sum solvers by noting the naive 2^n search space and the existence of dynamic-programming algorithms that solve subset sum exactly in pseudo-polynomial time; they also contrast their structure-agnostic numerical approach with rule-based table-sum heuristics that must be specialized per table type and do not generalize well. However, they do not present empirical runtime or accuracy comparisons against these classical baselines.
    [codes: C-007, C-009, C-010, C-024]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper acknowledges that understanding table structure via hand-crafted rules is 'almost impossible' to generalize, motivating but also constraining rule-based baselines. For the proposed Hopfield approach, the authors note that random initialization does not guarantee convergence to the global optimum and that local optima generally do not correspond to valid subset-sum solutions, so a single run may fail; they mitigate this only heuristically with multiple random restarts. They also report an experimental limitation where the hardest synthetic configuration (n = 256 and large Xmax) was not completely solved within the allowed runs. More broadly, they recognize that runtime grows with problem size and number magnitude, and that certain configurations are inherently harder when many solutions exist.
    [codes: C-005, C-007, C-009, C-019, C-024, C-025, C-029, C-033]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The authors propose future work on implementing the QUBO-solving Hopfield architecture on specialized hardware such as FPGAs and exploring implications for quantum computing applications, including potential mappings of their formulation to quantum algorithms. They also suggest that parallel optimization of many independent Hopfield networks on GPUs is a promising direction and state an ambition to integrate the method into smart auditing software for direct use by auditors.
    [codes: C-003, C-022, C-035, C-036]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The contribution is framed as a generic, table-structure-agnostic auditing tool: by formulating the detection of sum relations in financial tables as a subset-sum QUBO and solving it via Hopfield-network gradient descent, the authors claim to provide a reliable binary-optimization-based method that works on both artificial and real financial data and is suitable for deployment in smart auditing software, with an explicit outlook toward compatibility with future specialized and quantum hardware.
    [codes: C-001, C-006, C-008, C-011, C-012, C-017, C-020, C-028, C-031, C-034, C-036]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the paper carefully defines the QUBO/Ising encoding, including binary indicator vectors and parameterization of the quadratic form, and leverages known convergence properties of Hopfield networks under symmetric, hollow weight matrices. The experimental design covers both artificial data—generated by uniformly sampling integers within specified ranges and constructing targets from random subsets—and real financial-report columns with large magnitudes and cent-level precision; the authors note how parameter choices (n, Xmin, Xmax) affect problem difficulty and solution multiplicity. They also emphasize that multiple random restarts are used to reduce the risk of being trapped in local minima, and that GPU-parallelizable, independent network runs could further support scalability.
    [codes: C-011, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-020, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-030, C-033]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: None beyond the above dimensions; all verified codes were incorporated into problem framing, methodological description, results, limitations, or future work.
    [codes: —]

=== PAPER 313a1d740d27 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper tackles real-time anomaly detection in high-velocity, non‑stationary data streams, emphasizing challenges such as complex nonlinear structure, high dimensionality, and concept drift that degrade classical statistical and kernel-based methods. It formulates the problem as streaming anomaly detection over temporally ordered data with evolving distributions, aiming to handle these streaming-specific complications. The authors situate this within emerging quantum machine learning and quantum kernel methods as a way to better capture non-linear relationships in such streams.
    [codes: C-001, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-018, C-019, C-021, C-011, C-012, C-023]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The approach is a hybrid quantum–classical architecture that uses parameterized quantum circuits to implement quantum feature maps and kernels tailored to streaming data, combining quantum-enhanced feature spaces with classical anomaly detectors such as one-class style methods. Specialized quantum feature maps encode temporal dependencies and distributional shifts of the data stream into quantum states, with adaptive encoding strategies to cope with non-stationarity, and the quantum kernel is computed via state overlaps estimated through measurement on quantum hardware or simulators. The system is organized as a layered, hardware-constraint‑aware pipeline: preprocessing and dimension reduction reshape raw streams into qubit-feasible inputs; quantum circuits balance expressivity with NISQ limits; and adaptation mechanisms such as ensembles and forgetting factors are integrated to handle concept drift.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-004, C-014, C-015, C-017, C-023, C-025, C-026, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-042, C-043, C-045, C-052, C-053]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The authors report that their quantum-kernel-based method achieves superior anomaly detection performance, especially on complex nonlinear, high-dimensional datasets and under gradual concept drift, with robustness also extending to adversarial or challenging conditions. Empirically, they claim improvements over classical baselines on both synthetic and real-world datasets, and ablation studies show that angle encoding, feature re-uploading, and trainable (parameterized) circuits provide notable performance gains. Their theoretical and empirical analysis suggests that quantum advantages grow with dimensionality and can emerge beyond specific thresholds determined by data and hardware characteristics.
    [codes: C-005, C-016, C-024, C-041, C-046, C-047, C-048, C-049, C-050, C-051]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper positions classical baselines—including statistical models, distance-based clustering, density estimation, kernel methods such as one-class SVMs, and deep learning—as either making restrictive assumptions, degrading in high dimensions, or being computationally and operationally costly for high-velocity streams, particularly under concept drift. Against these baselines, the authors claim empirical improvements for their quantum kernel approach across synthetic and real-world datasets, and highlight that classical kernel methods suffer from quadratic or cubic scaling in kernel matrix computation and support-vector storage where quantum kernels may alleviate some of these bottlenecks in certain regimes.
    [codes: C-009, C-010, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-016, C-024, C-040, C-049, C-050]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge several limitations tied to current quantum hardware and streaming deployment. Limited qubit counts necessitate aggressive dimension reduction before quantum encoding, constraining the size of feature spaces that can be natively represented, and circuit design must trade off expressivity against NISQ-era constraints such as noise, coherence times, and gate fidelities. They further note that quantum advantage is conditional rather than universal, emerging only beyond specific dimensionality thresholds that depend on hardware parameters, and that streaming use is constrained by latency and encoding overhead because reliable quantum measurements require repeated circuit executions. More broadly, they characterize streaming QML as a nascent area with limited prior work, and stress that practical deployment choices and the realization of advantage depend heavily on application context and available quantum resources.
    [codes: C-010, C-019, C-021, C-027, C-028, C-032, C-040, C-041, C-044, C-045, C-055]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: For future work, the paper points to the need for advances in quantum hardware, suggesting that growing capabilities in qubit counts and quality will expand the range of streaming anomaly detection and related application domains that can benefit from quantum kernel techniques.
    [codes: C-056]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The authors claim novelty in proposing a quantum kernel framework specifically tailored to streaming anomaly detection, including a new quantum kernel formulation and specialized feature maps that encode temporal and distributional properties of non-stationary streams. They also emphasize contributions in designing a hybrid quantum–classical processing pipeline with integrated concept-drift adaptation mechanisms such as ensembles and forgetting factors, alongside a theoretical expressivity analysis for quantum kernels in nonlinear, high-dimensional anomaly detection settings and an empirical evaluation across synthetic and real-world datasets. Additionally, the paper positions itself as addressing a research gap in practically identifying where and how quantum advantages can be leveraged within streaming analytics workflows.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-004, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-027, C-029, C-030, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-049]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the paper describes a layered real-time system architecture and a preprocessing pipeline that includes feature engineering, normalization, and dimension reduction prior to quantum encoding, reflecting attention to practical deployment. The experimental setup reportedly uses both simulation and real quantum hardware access, with time-series cross-validation to preserve temporal causality and evaluation metrics that cover accuracy and operational aspects such as latency and throughput. Theoretical analysis is used to frame expressivity–complexity trade-offs and to specify hardware-dependent thresholds for quantum advantage, and ablation studies explore design choices such as encoding schemes, feature re-uploading, and parameterized vs fixed circuits.
    [codes: C-037, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-046, C-047, C-048, C-052, C-053, C-054]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper offers general background positioning quantum machine learning and quantum feature spaces as an emerging paradigm for extending classical kernels and potentially representing classically intractable patterns, while also noting that streaming QML remains at an early stage of investigation. These contextual elements primarily situate the work within broader QML trends rather than adding method- or result-specific details.
    [codes: C-011, C-023, C-024, C-027]
[SILO OVERLAY]

=== PAPER 3506790b2a7a (type: central) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets financial transaction fraud detection as a critical task in intelligent financial systems, emphasizing difficulties such as highly imbalanced classes, nonlinear and high‑dimensional transaction features, and the need to generalize to novel fraud patterns over long temporal sequences. It frames these challenges as a sequence‑modeling problem where conventional deep learning and RNN-based architectures struggle with vanishing/exploding gradients and limited ability to capture complex nonlinear structure in dynamic data.
    [codes: C-001, C-008, C-009, C-010]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors propose a hybrid quantum–classical architecture (HQRNN-FD) that uses variational quantum circuits as a front-end feature extractor, combined end‑to‑end with a recurrent neural network and attention. The quantum module prepares a uniform superposition with Hadamard gates, then applies a dual-angle encoding (Ry and Rz) with nonlinear arctangent mapping and standardized, bounded rotation parameters, together with data reuploading and a hierarchical entanglement pattern (local ring and global alternating CNOTs) and trainable U3 single‑qubit gates; the resulting Pauli‑Z expectation values in [−1,1] form quantum-enhanced embeddings that are fed at each time step into an RNN whose hidden states are aggregated via self‑attention.
    [codes: C-002, C-013, C-014, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: On a large public fraud-detection dataset, HQRNN-FD reportedly achieves very high overall accuracy (around 0.972) and attains a fraud-class F1-score of 0.9009, with improved precision relative to a closely related hybrid baseline. The authors claim that SMOTE-based preprocessing increases the fraud proportion in the training data to 40% and improves generalization to novel fraud instances, and that removing this preprocessing degrades performance. They also report that the model maintains strong performance under simulated quantum noise (including bit-flip and phase-flip) and that classification accuracy tends to increase as the number of qubits grows, with a 6-qubit setting performing best across most monthly evaluations.
    [codes: C-005, C-016, C-036, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-046, C-047, C-048, C-050]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper presents comparative experiments indicating that HQRNN-FD outperforms conventional models by about 2.4% in accuracy and achieves the highest fraud-class F1-score among a set of classical and quantum baselines. To support a fair comparison, all models are constrained to operate on a 6-dimensional input feature vector after preprocessing; ablation studies further show that adding the attention mechanism increases the fraud-class F1 from 0.8703 to 0.9009 and that the RNN component is essential for processing temporal transaction sequences.
    [codes: C-006, C-037, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-048, C-049]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors explicitly acknowledge that their study is restricted to structured transaction data, implying that the current architecture has not been validated on unstructured or richer multimodal financial information (such as text or image-based evidence), which limits the generality of the reported results.
    [codes: C-051]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work is planned to extend HQRNN-FD to multimodal fraud-detection settings by incorporating diverse data sources beyond structured transaction records, suggesting an interest in adapting the hybrid quantum–classical framework to richer financial data ecosystems.
    [codes: C-052]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The contribution is framed as an end-to-end hybrid sequence-modeling architecture that tightly integrates variational quantum circuits and RNNs within a shared computational graph for fraud detection, augmented by a specifically designed quantum encoding module featuring dual-angle encoding with nonlinear preprocessing, data reuploading, and a hierarchical entanglement scheme. The model also introduces a two-tier attention structure—within RNN cells and at the output layer—to enhance temporal representation learning over transaction sequences.
    [codes: C-002, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-018, C-019, C-021, C-022, C-023]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses a large public fraud dataset with over 1.7 million transaction records whose temporal and class-distribution characteristics (including monthly volume variation and initial imbalance) are described. The preprocessing pipeline is detailed—covering data cleaning, feature and label encoding, Z-score standardization, SMOTE oversampling, and construction of tensor-based sequence inputs—with an 80/20 train-test split and SMOTE applied only to the training set to increase the fraud share to 40%. Experiments are performed via PennyLane-based quantum simulation with early stopping, and evaluation employs standard metrics (accuracy, F1, etc.); ablation analyses are reported to isolate the effects of attention, RNN components, and preprocessing, and the authors also study robustness under simulated quantum noise and performance trends versus qubit count, suggesting an attempt at systematic empirical validation even though implementation code is not referenced.
    [codes: C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-043, C-046, C-047, C-048, C-049, C-050]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper motivates the use of quantum computing in fraud detection by arguing that quantum models can perform nonlinear transformations in high-dimensional feature spaces and that, despite current NISQ-era limitations in accuracy, scalability, and stability, hybrid approaches may still offer benefits such as robustness and improved predictive performance as qubit counts grow.
    [codes: C-007, C-011, C-012]

=== PAPER 35505140af55 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper addresses fraud detection in large-scale, real-time payment (RTP) systems where transactions occur in milliseconds and conventional machine learning and rule-based systems struggle with high-velocity, highly imbalanced data, leading to delayed detection and losses. The authors motivate quantum approaches as a way to explore many candidate fraud patterns in parallel and to handle weak, nonlinear anomalies in massive transaction streams. Fraud is formulated both as a tabular binary classification problem and as anomaly detection on transaction graphs representing accounts and their interactions.
    [codes: C-001, C-006, C-007, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-017, C-028]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The study focuses on quantum machine learning models, specifically Quantum Graph Neural Networks (QGNNs) and Quantum Support Vector Machines (QSVMs), for RTP fraud detection. Implementations use Qiskit and Amazon Braket in a hybrid setup, with angle encoding feature maps (including ZZFeatureMap) on small (≈4-qubit) circuits, leveraging superposition and entanglement in large Hilbert spaces to evaluate multiple fraudulent patterns simultaneously. Transactions are modeled as graphs where nodes are accounts and edges are transactions for QGNN, and the models are trained and evaluated via Qiskit Aer simulation and deployment on Rigetti hardware through Amazon Braket, with platform-level QaaS comparisons noted.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-010, C-016, C-017, C-019, C-021, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The authors report that quantum-enhanced models, particularly the QGNN, achieve strong fraud detection performance, with QGNN showing the best overall metrics (e.g., accuracy, precision, recall, F1 and AUC) on the benchmarked dataset. They claim that quantum models offer robust detection accuracy, higher recall and F1-scores than competitors, and reduced computational latency, with QGNN maintaining F1-scores above 80% even under extreme class imbalance and achieving steeper ROC curves with higher AUC than alternatives. Analysis of latency attributes most overhead for QGNN to graph construction rather than quantum inference itself.
    [codes: C-005, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: Quantum models (QSVM and QGNN) are explicitly compared against classical baselines using accuracy, precision, recall, F1, AUC and execution time, with tables indicating that quantum models outperform classical models on recall and F1 across different imbalance levels. The QGNN is reported as the best-performing model in a head-to-head comparison table, and the ROC analysis states that quantum models have steeper curves and higher AUC than classical baselines. These comparisons are framed in the context of known weaknesses of classical models, such as overfitting, sensitivity to data shifts, bias due to class imbalance and heavy reliance on hand-crafted features.
    [codes: C-004, C-008, C-013, C-014, C-031, C-032, C-033]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper acknowledges several limitations. On the classical side, it notes that standard fraud models are biased by extreme class imbalance, can overfit and are sensitive to data shifts, and depend on non-scalable hand-crafted feature engineering. For quantum approaches, the authors highlight serious infrastructure constraints: most publicly accessible QPUs have fewer than 100 qubits, are affected by gate noise and require complex calibration, and cloud-based QaaS access introduces latency that can offset speed gains, which is critical given that added latency of even 50 ms is detrimental in RTP contexts. They also point out explainability and privacy challenges because QML operates on data embedded in Hilbert space, making models difficult to interpret and potentially problematic under regulatory expectations.
    [codes: C-008, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-038, C-039, C-040]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The authors position quantum fraud detection as an additive layer to existing classical systems and suggest hybrid deployment patterns where banks use quantum-enhanced models to pre-screen borderline transactions flagged by classical models in tiered fraud stacks. They advocate that financial institutions start investing in 'quantum readiness' to prepare for eventual integration of these technologies and explicitly propose future research directions such as federated quantum learning, enabling distributed quantum models that preserve data locality and privacy across institutions.
    [codes: C-037, C-041, C-042, C-043]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims to address a gap in the literature where, so far, only theoretical models and pilot programs exist for practical, scalable, real-time quantum-enhanced fraud detection. Its main contribution is described as implementing and comparing QGNNs and QSVMs on RTP fraud scenarios using real-world benchmark datasets (including IEEE-CIS) and synthetic real-time transactions, thereby providing a more systematic practical benchmarking of quantum fraud detection models.
    [codes: C-011, C-018, C-020, C-021, C-022]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses real-world fraud datasets such as IEEE-CIS and additional transaction logs, explicitly acknowledging and modeling the severe class imbalance characteristic of fraud data. The authors apply SMOTE combined with random undersampling as preprocessing to mitigate imbalance and discuss feature engineering and quantum encoding choices (e.g., angle encoding and ZZFeatureMap). They evaluate models on standard metrics plus an operational latency metric—"inference time per 1,000 transactions"—to reflect real-time constraints, and distinguish between simulation (Qiskit Aer) and deployment on Amazon Braket with Rigetti hardware. They also note that the overhead in QGNN latency arises largely from graph construction phases, which has implications for scalable deployment.
    [codes: C-020, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-034]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper situates its work within the broader RTP and industry context, citing the rapid growth in RTP volumes, the increase in scams and money laundering, and prior hybrid quantum-classical prototypes such as Deloitte Italy’s Amazon Braket pipeline. It also notes emerging promise of QGNNs for anomaly detection in transaction networks and describes active experimentation by firms like Deloitte, Paysafe and JPMorgan Chase, alongside comparisons of QaaS platforms such as Amazon Braket for hybrid workflow orchestration and the practical importance of higher recall for rare and complex fraud patterns.
    [codes: C-006, C-007, C-011, C-012, C-018, C-019, C-036]
[SILO OVERLAY]

=== PAPER 36cd7ccae10c (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames the problem as the inability of traditional and classical AI-based risk modeling methods to capture intricate, high-dimensional, and real-time dynamics of modern financial systems, including portfolio optimization, fraud detection, and credit scoring. It identifies a specific gap: the lack of scalable, efficient hybrid AI–quantum systems capable of real-time prediction and mitigation of financial risks, and emphasizes the growing need for faster and more accurate risk models in increasingly complex markets. The overarching formulation is that AI–quantum convergence can reframe financial risk modeling and enterprise decision systems as high-dimensional optimization and pattern-recognition tasks amenable to quantum-enhanced computation.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-013, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-041, C-045, C-048, C-071]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors conceptually discuss a family of quantum and hybrid methods rather than implementing a specific algorithm, focusing on quantum-enhanced ML (Quantum Neural Networks and Quantum Support Vector Machines using quantum kernels and data encoded into quantum states), quantum optimization approaches (QAOA and quantum annealing for exploring large solution spaces in portfolio optimization), and quantum-inspired algorithms that simulate quantum phenomena on classical hardware as an intermediate step. They also describe generic quantum computing constructs—qubits, superposition, entanglement, quantum gates, and high-connectivity quantum processors—as the implementation substrate, and highlight mapping financial data into quantum states for credit risk analysis and other applications. Hybrid quantum–classical systems are positioned as the emerging architectural pattern where quantum subroutines handle parts of problems benefiting from quantum parallelism while classical components manage data preprocessing and post-processing.
    [codes: C-002, C-016, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-035, C-046, C-047, C-050, C-061, C-064, C-087]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: As a conceptual/review piece, the paper reports no new quantitative experiments but makes a series of performance claims about quantum-enhanced AI in finance: quantum optimization and parallelism purportedly handle high-dimensional datasets and optimization problems more efficiently, enabling real-time processing of data streams and larger solution spaces in portfolio optimization, fraud detection, and credit risk modeling. The authors claim that quantum-enhanced AI models can achieve superior accuracy, efficiency, scalability, more granular credit scoring, improved anomaly/fraud detection with reduced false positives, better default prediction, and more resilient portfolios under stress scenarios, and can support real-time algorithmic trading, scenario analysis, and decision support systems at scales infeasible for classical systems. They further state that cloud-based quantum access enables practical experimentation today, and conclude that AI–quantum synergy can enhance forecasting and optimization and ultimately transform enterprise decision systems and accelerate digital transformation.
    [codes: C-004, C-005, C-014, C-019, C-020, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-034, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-046, C-049, C-051, C-060, C-068, C-069, C-070, C-072, C-073, C-074, C-075, C-076, C-088, C-098, C-099]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The authors repeatedly contrast quantum-enhanced AI with classical approaches, arguing that classical models such as Monte Carlo simulations, traditional ML, Markowitz portfolio optimization, and standard fraud and credit scoring tools are limited by computational resources and struggle to scale with high-dimensional, real-time financial data and complex constraints. In contrast, they claim quantum or hybrid models can explore far larger solution spaces via quantum parallelism, analyze more asset configurations, uncover hidden correlations, capture non-linear interactions more effectively, and thus deliver faster asset allocation, improved fraud detection with fewer false positives, more granular credit scoring, and more accurate credit-risk and portfolio-resilience assessments than purely classical systems.
    [codes: C-003, C-015, C-019, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-034, C-036, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-048, C-049, C-071, C-072, C-074]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper acknowledges extensive limitations and challenges. On the technical side, current quantum hardware is not yet scalable for large real-world financial models; available qubits are too few, noisy, and decohere quickly, requiring long coherence times, high-fidelity qubits, and substantial connectivity that existing devices do not yet provide. Error correction remains an unsolved problem whose overhead exceeds current resources, and NISQ-era noise and gate errors limit the robustness and trainability of complex quantum ML models. The authors also note that portfolio optimization with hundreds or thousands of assets would need an impractically large number of qubits, and that resource intensity and upfront costs can outweigh near-term benefits. Integration with legacy fintech and classical infrastructures is described as complex, compounded by immature quantum-specific regulatory oversight, demanding compliance with stringent financial regulation and data-privacy rules. Security is highlighted as a particular limitation because powerful quantum computers threaten current cryptographic schemes (RSA, ECC), while robust post-quantum and quantum-cryptographic solutions are still emerging. Ethically, the authors warn that quantum AI’s opacity amplifies existing black-box, explainability, and accountability issues, that high-dimensional processing may inadvertently magnify hidden biases and discriminatory patterns, and that tracing decision logic is especially difficult for quantum algorithms. Overall, they stress that scalability, integration, and governance challenges mean quantum supremacy in financial decision-making remains distant and contingent on substantial advances in hardware and algorithms.
    [codes: C-009, C-010, C-041, C-045, C-048, C-052, C-053, C-054, C-055, C-056, C-058, C-059, C-062, C-063, C-065, C-066, C-078, C-081, C-083, C-084, C-091, C-094, C-100]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work is framed around making AI–quantum finance systems practical, robust, and governable. The authors call for advances in quantum hardware (improved coherence, lower error rates, better circuit design), and for quantum algorithms and QML techniques that are more robust, accurate, scalable, and tailored to noisy devices, including improved error-mitigation and error-correction strategies for QML. They highlight emerging hybrid quantum–classical architectures, novel quantum ML methods, and the integration of AI with quantum cryptography to enhance cybersecurity as promising research directions, while noting that achieving any form of quantum supremacy in financial decision-making will require substantial breakthroughs. On the institutional side, they foresee the need for regulatory evolution: methods to certify quantum-enhanced algorithms, the creation of international standards, and frameworks to ensure fairness, accountability, and compliance. They also argue that early adopters could gain strategic advantages and that ongoing digital transformation of financial services could be accelerated as these technologies mature.
    [codes: C-051, C-067, C-076, C-085, C-086, C-087, C-088, C-089, C-090, C-092, C-094, C-095, C-097, C-099]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper presents itself as a conceptual exploration and synthesis that frames the convergence of AI and quantum computing as a paradigm shift in financial risk modeling and enterprise decision systems, emphasizing quantum-enhanced AI as a means to tackle high-dimensional optimization and pattern-recognition problems beyond classical capabilities. Its claimed contribution lies in articulating how hybrid AI–quantum models, spanning portfolio optimization, fraud detection, credit risk, and enterprise decision support, could redefine risk modeling, highlighting both transformative potential and the associated ethical, regulatory, and implementation challenges.
    [codes: C-001, C-013, C-016, C-019, C-020, C-068, C-073, C-096, C-097, C-098]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: The article is explicitly conceptual and review-oriented, with no original datasets, quantitative benchmarks, or implementation details reported; claimed performance gains for quantum-enhanced models are presented at a high level and not supported by reproducible experiments or metrics in this paper. The discussion of AI and quantum methods is largely theoretical—describing capabilities of ML on nonlinear, high-dimensional data, qubits, entanglement, and standard QML models such as QNNs and QSVMs—as well as generic case-study narratives of portfolio optimization, fraud detection, credit scoring, and risk management without specifying data sources, parameter settings, or evaluation protocols. The authors nonetheless emphasize the need for fairness-aware algorithms, accountability frameworks, and algorithm certification, implicitly acknowledging that rigorous methodological and governance standards will be critical for future empirical work.
    [codes: C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-035, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-047, C-050, C-071, C-072, C-079, C-082, C-085]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: Several cross-cutting themes extend beyond the core technical and comparative narrative. The authors stress the importance of ethical and regulatory considerations—transparency, fairness, accountability, and bias mitigation—within AI–quantum financial systems, alongside the potential for quantum models to support continuous compliance monitoring and real-time regulatory reporting. They also emphasize the need for cross-disciplinary collaboration among quantum physicists, AI researchers, financial experts, and cybersecurity professionals to develop practical and secure solutions, and note that quantum cloud services already offer a low-barrier environment for experimentation even before hardware matures fully.
    [codes: C-011, C-012, C-057, C-060, C-075, C-077, C-080, C-082, C-093]

=== PAPER 378c2a73ea46 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames a combined problem of improving financial prediction (market anomaly detection, portfolio optimization, dimensionality reduction for high‑dimensional indicators, and time‑series forecasting) and strengthening transaction security against quantum attacks. It motivates quantum methods by claiming that classical cryptography and ML struggle with high‑dimensional data, complex optimization, and the coming quantum threat to RSA/ECC, and proposes integrating quantum machine learning with quantum‑resistant lattice cryptography for finance. The portfolio problem is explicitly posed as maximizing the Sharpe ratio under risk constraints, while security is posed as protecting financial transactions in a post‑quantum world.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-004, C-005, C-006, C-007, C-008, C-012, C-013, C-029]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors use several gate‑based quantum approaches: QSVM with a quantum kernel defined as the overlap of data‑encoded quantum states to accelerate high‑dimensional classification and capture asset correlations via entanglement; QAOA encodes portfolio optimization as an Ising Hamiltonian over asset qubits to search allocations in superposition while targeting a high Sharpe ratio; QPCA represents the covariance matrix as a density matrix and uses phase‑estimation‑style routines to extract principal components; and QBM is used as a probabilistic time‑series model to capture short‑ and long‑run dependencies in financial data. For security, they specify lattice‑based cryptography grounded in SVP and LWE, detailing an LWE‑style encryption c = As + e + m and highlighting homomorphic properties for secure computation on encrypted financial records. All quantum ML components are evaluated in a hybrid quantum‑classical simulator setup.
    [codes: C-010, C-024, C-025, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-012, C-031, C-033, C-035, C-036, C-038, C-039]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The paper reports that QSVM improves classification accuracy by up to about 20% and reduces training time by around 50%, including 92.3% accuracy on S&P 500 anomaly detection and detection of 95% of anomalies in their summary. QAOA is claimed to improve portfolio optimization with a 10–15% Sharpe ratio uplift and 25% faster optimization, including a reported Sharpe ratio of 1.45 for a 50‑asset portfolio and an example allocation of 45% equities and 30% bonds. QPCA is reported to reduce dimensionality by ~90% while preserving around 95% variance (e.g., 95.2% variance retained on S&P 500 indicators), and QBM is said to increase forecasting accuracy by about 5–10% with 30% faster convergence, achieving roughly 88–88.5% stock‑price prediction accuracy. For security, lattice‑based cryptography is claimed to provide 99.9% security against classical and quantum attacks with roughly 10–15% extra execution time, yet still securing financial transactions with minimal overhead in their examples. The conclusion summarizes that all quantum‑enhanced components yield substantial gains in computing efficiency, predictive accuracy, and security.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-004, C-005, C-006, C-014, C-015, C-018, C-019, C-026, C-032, C-034, C-037, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-046, C-047, C-048, C-049, C-050, C-051, C-052, C-053, C-054, C-055]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: Throughout, the authors compare their quantum approaches to classical baselines, arguing that classical cryptography (e.g., RSA/ECC) and ML models are inadequate for high‑dimensional financial data and complex optimization. In their review and experiments, they state that QSVM and hybrid quantum–classical models outperform classical SVMs by 10–20% in accuracy and halve training time, QAOA improves Sharpe ratios over classical mean‑variance optimizers by about 10–15% and is claimed to reduce optimization time by 25%, QPCA retains more variance than classical PCA for equivalent dimensionality reduction, and QBM outperforms classical RNNs by 8–10% in prediction accuracy with around 30% faster convergence. For security, they emphasize that lattice‑based schemes resist both classical and quantum attacks, unlike legacy RSA/ECC, though at the cost of 10–15% higher encryption/decryption time versus classical schemes.
    [codes: C-007, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-026, C-032, C-034, C-037, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-049]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper acknowledges several technical and organizational limitations. On the quantum side, it notes that current hardware noise and limited qubit coherence can seriously affect the performance and reliability of QSVM, QAOA, QPCA, and QBM, and that scalability to larger datasets remains unresolved because performance may degrade as data size and complexity grow. There is also an explicit admission that lattice‑based cryptography introduces a 10–15% increase in encryption and decryption time relative to classical schemes, and this overhead is reiterated in the comparative analysis. In terms of adoption barriers, the authors highlight a shortage of quantum‑trained professionals in the financial sector and the difficulty of embedding these quantum technologies into existing financial infrastructures, which together constrain near‑term deployment.
    [codes: C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-037, C-045, C-061]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: For future work, the authors propose scaling their quantum‑enhanced methods to larger and more diverse datasets, including global assets, emerging markets, cryptocurrencies, and derivatives. They foresee testing these models on actual quantum hardware as devices mature, and emphasize the need to improve robustness of algorithms against noisy quantum environments. Methodologically, they suggest designing a hybrid QBM‑RNN architecture that combines quantum modeling of complex dependencies with the scalability of classical recurrent networks. On the cryptographic side, they point to developing advanced quantum‑resistant schemes enabling efficient secure multiparty computation and analytics on encrypted financial data.
    [codes: C-056, C-057, C-058, C-059, C-060]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper presents its main contribution as an advanced integrated framework that combines quantum machine‑learning techniques (QSVM, QAOA, QPCA, and QBM) with quantum‑resistant lattice‑based cryptography to jointly address financial predictive modeling and cybersecurity. In its literature review and comparative analysis, it claims that, taken together, these quantum‑enhanced methods significantly outperform prior classical approaches in computational efficiency, predictive accuracy, and security, thereby positioning the proposed architecture as a next‑generation solution for financial systems.
    [codes: C-001, C-009, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-017, C-050]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, all quantum models are evaluated in a hybrid quantum–classical setting using quantum simulators coupled with classical processing, rather than on physical quantum hardware. The authors provide mathematical formulations for QSVM kernels, QAOA Hamiltonians, QPCA density‑matrix representations, QBM energy functions, and LWE‑based lattice encryption, and they illustrate behavior with concrete financial examples such as market anomaly detection cases, sample portfolio allocations, dimensionality‑reduction statistics, and secured transaction scenarios. However, the reliance on simulators, the optimistic assumptions about quantum parallelism (e.g., logarithmic‑time kernel and QPCA operations), and the high‑level descriptions of integration into real financial infrastructure suggest that the results are exploratory and require further validation in realistic production environments.
    [codes: C-024, C-025, C-028, C-029, C-031, C-033, C-035, C-036, C-038, C-039, C-046, C-047, C-048, C-049]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: Several cross‑cutting claims recur, including that quantum parallelism and superposition provide a generic computational advantage for financial bottlenecks, enabling QSVM kernels and QAOA portfolio searches to explore many high‑dimensional configurations at once. The paper also reiterates an overall narrative that each quantum‑enhanced component—prediction, optimization, compression, and encryption—could substantially impact efficiency and security in financial systems.
    [codes: C-009, C-026, C-030, C-032, C-034, C-038, C-050]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to fraud_detection because its strongest contribution is quantum-enhanced anomaly detection, especially the QSVM results on market anomaly detection and the broader claim that quantum models improve classification accuracy and speed. It also includes security-oriented lattice cryptography, which is relevant to protecting fraud-sensitive transaction data, though the paper is not specifically about fraud or AML workflows.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]

  KEY_POINT 3: QPCA is relevant as a preprocessing step for fraud detection because it reduces dimensionality while preserving most variance.

=== PAPER 394c63b275d9 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets credit card fraud detection, framed as a high‑stakes binary classification problem on a large, highly imbalanced transaction dataset, where traditional ML struggles due to data size, imbalance, and structural complexity. This problem is reformulated for a quantum setting by projecting selected transaction features into a qubit-based quantum space and classifying them with a Variational Quantum Classifier (VQC).
    [codes: C-005, C-006, C-011, C-024, C-025, C-014]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The study uses a Variational Quantum Classifier (VQC) as a hybrid quantum–classical model, in which classical feature vectors are amplitude‑encoded into an n‑qubit system and processed by a parameterized quantum circuit. Features are first selected via traditional K‑Best or meta‑heuristic algorithms (PSO, ACO, ASO), then projected into quantum space using a qubit-based mechanism and classified by the VQC; all experiments are executed on quantum simulators running on classical hardware.
    [codes: C-001, C-011, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-024, C-025, C-010, C-026]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Across scenarios, the best-performing configuration is PSO-based feature selection combined with VQC, achieving about 94.54% accuracy with balanced precision (≈93.64%) and recall (≈93.55%), which the authors highlight as providing the highest performance among tested setups. In contrast, the K‑Best+VQC baseline yields high precision (≈99.17%) but low recall (≈47.57%), while ACO+VQC and ASO+VQC give intermediate, more balanced performance but remain below PSO in accuracy and F1; the authors also claim their approach reduces overfitting to produce more balanced and accurate results.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-012, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper primarily compares different feature selection strategies feeding a quantum VQC (traditional K‑Best versus meta‑heuristics PSO, ACO, ASO) rather than directly benchmarking against end‑to‑end classical classifiers, documenting that PSO+VQC substantially improves recall and overall accuracy relative to K‑Best+VQC while maintaining strong precision. The authors argue that by mitigating traditional ML issues such as overfitting and by exploiting meta‑heuristics' ability to discover complex feature relationships, the hybrid QML–meta‑heuristic pipeline can yield more balanced fraud detection performance than traditional feature selection within the same quantum framework, but no explicit runtime or accuracy comparison to purely classical models is reported.
    [codes: C-001, C-006, C-012, C-017, C-013, C-021, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors caution that their findings are obtained entirely on quantum simulators, so the reported results, while promising, may not fully reflect performance in real deployment scenarios. They explicitly note that the simulators are constrained by limited qubit availability and gate depth, which restricts scalability and may differ from the behavior on actual noisy quantum hardware.
    [codes: C-038, C-039, C-010, C-026]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: For future work, the authors recommend validating the proposed quantum models on real quantum hardware and scaling to larger datasets, to assess how the approach behaves beyond simulator constraints and closer to operational fraud-detection settings.
    [codes: C-040, C-038, C-039]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims novelty in integrating quantum machine learning, specifically a VQC, with meta‑heuristic feature selection algorithms such as PSO, ACO, and ASO for credit card fraud detection, stating that, to the best of their knowledge, no prior work has combined QML and meta‑heuristics in this manner.
    [codes: C-001, C-009]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses the large, highly imbalanced European Cardholder credit card dataset, removes duplicate records, and applies advanced class‑imbalance handling via SMOTE‑ENN and Random Under Sampling before constructing a balanced working subset and a 37,500/12,500 train–test split. It provides a comparative analysis of traditional K‑Best versus multiple meta‑heuristic feature selection methods (PSO, ACO, ASO), reports which features each method selects and their subset sizes, and primarily evaluates performance using accuracy alongside precision, recall, and F1; all computations are run on classical hardware with quantum simulators, with features amplitude‑encoded into qubits. The authors also motivate meta‑heuristics as tools for exploring complex feature relationships and possible global optima, and position QML as potentially mitigating overfitting and achieving more balanced results.
    [codes: C-002, C-010, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The introduction situates the work within a broader trend of hybrid quantum–classical methods for fraud detection and emphasizes that quantum computing is positioned as a potentially faster and more capable paradigm for complex, large‑scale problems compared to classical computing, though these are presented as prospective capabilities rather than demonstrated advantages in this study.
    [codes: C-007, C-008]

=== PAPER 468c3327bdef (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames US banking as highly exposed to fraud and financial crime because banks process billions of transactions daily and face increasingly complex, digitally enabled threats including identity theft, card fraud, money laundering, insider abuse, AI-driven attacks, and ransomware (C-002, C-003, C-004, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-029). It argues that traditional fraud detection systems struggle with high false positives and false negatives, slow processing of large transaction volumes, and heightened exposure due to irreversible real-time payment systems and stringent compliance requirements (C-005, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-030). Within this context, quantum computing is positioned as a potential game changer for fraud prevention, real‑time risk analysis, and financial security, but also as a source of new cryptographic risk (C-006, C-008, C-013, C-019, C-031, C-033).
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-004, C-005, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-006, C-008, C-013, C-019, C-031, C-033]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: Methodologically, the paper is conceptual and describes quantum computing in terms of qubits exploiting superposition and entanglement to enable parallel processing and exponential-speed calculations relative to classical bits (C-007, C-012). For fraud prevention, it highlights quantum machine learning and “quantum AI” as key approaches for anomaly detection, complex pattern discovery, and adaptive fraud detection, and suggests hybrid cryptographic schemes combining classical and quantum‑resistant methods, but without specifying concrete algorithms or implementation details (C-015, C-016, C-033, C-036, C-037, C-056, C-058, C-054).
    [codes: C-007, C-012, C-015, C-016, C-033, C-036, C-037, C-056, C-058, C-054]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The paper does not report empirical or quantitative results; instead, it offers claims about expected benefits. The authors assert that quantum computing and quantum AI could increase fraud detection accuracy, reduce false positives, and identify hidden fraudulent patterns and complex relationships in financial data in real time (C-014, C-016, C-036, C-037, C-057, C-058). They further conclude that deploying quantum computing in US banking would be a “game changer” for fraud prevention and cybersecurity, while emphasizing its potential role in supporting risk management and predictive modeling (C-019, C-061).
    [codes: C-014, C-016, C-019, C-036, C-037, C-057, C-058, C-061]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The authors compare their envisioned quantum approaches conceptually to current classical systems, arguing that conventional computing struggles with massive, high‑speed interactive querying and that existing fraud detection models can take days or weeks to process transaction volumes of interest (C-005, C-026). They contend that rule‑based or traditional machine‑learning systems lead to high false positives and missed fraud, particularly as fraudsters mimic legitimate behavior, whereas quantum algorithms and quantum AI are claimed to process massive datasets almost instantaneously, detect hidden correlations, and stay a step ahead of criminals (C-024, C-025, C-036, C-037, C-059).
    [codes: C-005, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-036, C-037, C-059]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper acknowledges multiple limitations and risks. On the technology and implementation side, it highlights the high cost and complexity of quantum infrastructure, including specialized hardware, extreme cooling, scarce expertise, and limited access largely via a few cloud providers (C-009, C-020, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-062). From a security perspective, the authors stress that large‑scale quantum computers threaten current cryptographic schemes such as RSA and ECC via Shor’s algorithm, rendering existing banking encryption vulnerable and introducing quantum‑enabled cyber‑attack risks (C-010, C-017, C-031, C-034, C-045, C-062). Regulatory and compliance challenges are also emphasized, including legal uncertainty around quantum use in banking, data privacy concerns in quantum applications, and the need for new standards and clear guidelines from regulators (C-020, C-030, C-047, C-048, C-050, C-062). Finally, the paper notes that adoption will require significant time, investment, and regulatory adjustment, and that current systems face inherent irreversibility of real‑time payments, making fraud harder to remediate even with improved detection (C-009, C-027, C-028).
    [codes: C-009, C-010, C-017, C-020, C-027, C-028, C-030, C-034, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-047, C-048, C-050, C-062, C-031]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future directions center on strategic preparation and ecosystem development rather than specific algorithms. The authors urge US banks to invest in next‑generation defenses including AI‑based fraud detection, blockchain security, and especially quantum‑resistant and post‑quantum cryptography, with an emphasis on auditing existing cryptographic infrastructure, adopting hybrid classical/PQC schemes, and tracking NIST standardization efforts to stay ahead of "Q Day" (C-032, C-035, C-046, C-051, C-052, C-053, C-054, C-055). They call for workforce development in quantum AI and cryptography, stronger collaboration with tech firms, cybersecurity providers, regulators, and international partners, and the creation of clear regulatory frameworks and guidelines for quantum-secure electronic transactions and cross‑border risk management (C-011, C-018, C-032, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-046, C-049, C-050, C-051, C-053, C-060, C-063).

  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper presents itself as a forward‑looking, US‑banking‑specific discussion of how quantum computing and quantum AI could transform fraud prevention, financial crime detection, and cybersecurity, characterizing quantum computing as a “game changer” and emphasizing both its defensive opportunities and offensive threats (C-013, C-031, C-033, C-056, C-061). === PAPER 4715aa3cb8e4 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper addresses credit card fraud detection under extreme class imbalance, focusing on improving minority-class (fraud) sensitivity rather than overall accuracy, which the authors argue can be misleading because trivial classifiers can reach ~99.8% accuracy by predicting all transactions as normal. They explicitly prioritize recall and PR-AUC as primary metrics to reflect real fraud-detection operational constraints such as changing attacker behavior and tight latency budgets.
    [codes: C-001, C-008, C-009, C-010]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors use a hybrid quantum–classical neural network where a variational quantum circuit (VQC) layer replaces a classical dense layer in an otherwise shared neural architecture, with the quantum layer implemented via a PennyLane TorchLayer that outputs expectation values as classical features. The circuit employs angle (RY) embedding with data re-uploading to expose all 30 features using 10–15 qubits, followed by a custom data-driven priority-entanglement scheme that entangles the most strongly correlated feature pairs and then a strongly entangling layer (SEL) composed of parameterized rotations and a ring of CNOTs to approximate global mixing. Entanglement is constrained by correlation-based priority-pair selection within feature chunks determined by the qubit count, and models are trained with class-weighted binary cross-entropy under a configurable decision threshold tuned toward higher recall.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-004, C-011, C-012, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The hybrid quantum–classical model is reported to achieve consistent gains in recall and PR-AUC over classical baselines under matched parameter budgets, with the best 10-qubit configuration (80 priority pairs, shallow depth) reaching a PR-AUC of about 0.9192 and the best 15-qubit shallow configuration (10 priority pairs) reaching about 0.9229. At 10 qubits, introducing correlation-guided entanglement improves recall and PR-AUC over a no-pair baseline, but increasing depth from one to three or five layers does not improve PR-AUC, while at 15 qubits, very high entanglement budgets increase precision but reduce recall and PR-AUC, and deeper 15-qubit circuits drive recall very high but cause precision to collapse, highlighting a precision–recall trade-off. The authors further note that computation time grows with both circuit depth and coupling budget.
    [codes: C-005, C-006, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-037, C-040]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: Under a design where the quantum layer simply replaces a dense layer in a capacity-matched neural network with shared classical trunk, the authors report that classical baselines such as logistic regression and XGBoost achieve the highest precision, while the classical neural network attains the best recall among purely classical models. Against these baselines, the best-performing hybrid configuration improves PR-AUC by an absolute +0.0042 over logistic regression and increases recall by +0.0468 absolute over the classical neural network, with parameter counts between the classical and hybrid networks differing by less than 1%.
    [codes: C-002, C-011, C-017, C-024, C-025, C-032, C-033, C-035, C-036]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The study is explicitly limited to simulator-based experiments using idealized quantum circuits; the authors acknowledge that hardware noise and connectivity constraints could change the observed behavior and would substantially increase runtimes. They also note that the 15-qubit, 3-layer configuration had to be excluded because training repeatedly collapsed to a degenerate solution, indicating potential instability of deeper circuits, and they highlight that the anonymized PCA-like features of the fraud dataset restrict semantic interpretability of which financial attributes are being coupled by the entanglement scheme.
    [codes: C-041, C-042, C-043]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The authors propose extending their correlation-guided entanglement design to real quantum hardware and systematically studying its robustness under noisy, hardware-constrained conditions as key directions for future work.
    [codes: C-044]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The core claimed contribution is a data-driven priority-entanglement scheme that uses train-only feature correlations to determine which feature pairs to entangle before a strongly entangling block, thereby injecting a correlation-guided inductive bias into a hybrid variational quantum circuit. Combined with data re-uploading to handle all 30 features on 10–15 qubits and a controlled layer-swap comparison design, the authors position this structured, correlation-aware entanglement as the main novelty underlying the reported recall and PR-AUC gains.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-007, C-011, C-012, C-019, C-020]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the paper emphasizes a controlled comparison by matching parameter budgets and using an identical classical feature-extraction trunk for both classical and hybrid models, with near-equal parameter counts and wall-clock time used as the relevant compute metric. The dataset is handled carefully for class imbalance by retaining all 492 frauds and downsampling legitimate transactions, applying class-weighted binary cross-entropy and a lowered decision threshold (default 0.3) to favor recall, and computing the Pearson correlation matrix on the training split only to avoid leakage before reordering features for entanglement mapping. Experimental rigor is further supported by extensive repeated randomized trials (100 runs for 10-qubit and 20 for 15-qubit configurations), explicit documentation of the Intel Xeon simulation hardware, and discussion of how runtime scales with depth and coupling, alongside an interpretation that shallow, structured circuits lie on an efficient frontier where increased depth or entanglement produces diminishing or negative returns.
    [codes: C-002, C-011, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-037, C-038, C-039]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The authors argue that excessive entanglement budgets tend to over-mix and degrade recall and PR-AUC, reinforcing their conclusion that moderate entanglement and shallow depth form an empirical sweet spot for this fraud detection task.
    [codes: C-006, C-039, C-040]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is highly relevant to fraud_detection because it targets extreme class imbalance, prioritizes recall and PR-AUC over accuracy, and evaluates a hybrid quantum-classical model specifically on fraud detection data. Its main value for this silo is showing how correlation-guided entanglement and threshold tuning can improve minority-class fraud capture under realistic detection constraints.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: The paper is explicitly framed around minority-class fraud detection under severe imbalance, where recall and PR-AUC are more meaningful than accuracy.
    [codes: C-001, C-008, C-009, C-010  dim_source: dimension_1]
  KEY_POINT 2: The hybrid model uses train-only correlation analysis and priority entanglement to couple the most dependent features before a strongly entangling block.
    [codes: C-004, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-020  dim_source: dimension_2]
  KEY_POINT 3: The model is trained with imbalance-aware loss and a lowered decision threshold to favor fraud recall.
    [codes: C-021, C-022, C-013  dim_source: dimension_8]
  KEY_POINT 4: The best reported hybrid settings improve recall and PR-AUC over classical baselines, with the strongest gains appearing in shallow, moderately entangled circuits.
    [codes: C-005, C-026, C-027, C-029, C-035, C-036, C-039, C-040  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 5: The study’s limitations matter for fraud_detection deployment because it is simulator-only, and deeper circuits can become unstable or degrade precision.
    [codes: C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044  dim_source: dimension_5]
  PRIMARY_CODES: C-001, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-004, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-013, C-005, C-026, C-027, C-029, C-035, C-036, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044

=== PAPER 4d0406b8b736 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames the problem as escalating fraud and cybersecurity threats in rapidly growing fintech ecosystems, where sparse, imbalanced, high‑dimensional transaction data challenge existing approaches. Traditional rule‑based systems and even conventional ML/deep learning are described as increasingly inadequate due to limited adaptability, computational inefficiency, and difficulty modelling complex structures, motivating a reformulation of fraud detection as a high‑dimensional anomaly-detection and classification problem potentially amenable to quantum machine learning and advanced data science workflows.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-012, C-013, C-019, C-027, C-030, C-031]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors conceptually propose using QML algorithms—primarily Quantum Support Vector Machines, Quantum Principal Component Analysis, and Quantum Neural Networks—exploiting superposition, entanglement, and quantum interference for high‑dimensional classification, feature extraction, and sequence modelling in fraud detection. They discuss quantum data representation via amplitude encoding and QRAM for compact storage and rapid access, and design a hybrid quantum‑classical workflow where classical components handle data preparation and initial screening while quantum components perform computationally intensive anomaly detection and pattern analysis, augmented by quantum cryptographic techniques to secure data pipelines.
    [codes: C-004, C-005, C-007, C-008, C-010, C-011, C-015, C-016, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-033, C-034, C-038, C-040, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-047, C-050]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Reported outcomes are largely conceptual, but the paper claims that quantum‑enhanced fraud detection could improve accuracy, speed, and scalability of transaction pattern analysis and anomaly detection, including identifying rare and complex patterns classical ML may miss. Specific claimed benefits include reduced feature space requirements and lower overfitting risk for QML models, the ability to evaluate hundreds of thousands of transactions in parallel, uncover hidden relationships in high‑dimensional data, support real‑time behaviour‑based monitoring via QNNs, and overall efficiency and accuracy gains in hybrid pipelines. The authors also present case-style narratives asserting reductions in false positives (including a reported 25% reduction in a P2P lending context) and better detection of subtle identities and rapid small‑amount fraud, alongside the assertion that QNNs can outperform classical deep learning in classifying fraudulent vs. legitimate transactions and that hybrid architectures ease integration into existing infrastructures.
    [codes: C-002, C-006, C-018, C-023, C-025, C-028, C-030, C-032, C-035, C-036, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-046, C-048, C-049, C-051, C-052, C-053, C-054, C-055, C-074]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper contrasts QML with classical rule‑based systems and ML approaches such as decision trees, random forests, gradient boosting, neural networks, deep learning, and graph-based methods, arguing that classical models struggle with computational cost, scalability, and modelling of highly complex, high‑dimensional fraud data. In comparison, QML (via QSVM, QPCA, QNNs, and quantum‑enhanced anomaly detection) is claimed to offer accelerated computation, reduced feature space requirements, less overfitting in high dimensions, and the ability to uncover hidden relationships and rare patterns, with hybrid models described as yielding more nuanced fraud identification and significantly reduced false positives relative to purely classical systems. The authors also explicitly claim that QNNs have shown superiority over classical deep learning for fraud classification and that quantum algorithms provide notable speed and efficiency advantages over classical ML in certain fraud scenarios.
    [codes: C-003, C-013, C-020, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-035, C-036, C-038, C-039, C-046, C-049, C-052, C-054, C-055]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge substantial limitations to deploying QML for fraud detection: current quantum hardware is constrained by noise, limited qubit coherence times, and overall immaturity, making practical implementations early‑stage and restricting algorithm complexity. They highlight decoherence and the heavy qubit overhead of quantum error correction, scalability and integration challenges when embedding quantum components into legacy‑heavy fintech infrastructures, and the need for robust integration protocols to ensure seamless data flow between classical and quantum systems. Implementation quality is sensitive to data encoding choices, where improper encoding can degrade performance, and the paper notes that existing financial regulations were not designed with quantum computing in mind, creating compliance and governance gaps. More broadly, they stress that security and privacy considerations, the vulnerability of current public‑key cryptography to quantum attacks, and the difficulty of achieving interoperability with mixed legacy and modern architectures remain critical barriers.
    [codes: C-009, C-037, C-056, C-059, C-060, C-061, C-062, C-065, C-058, C-063]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future directions proposed include developing practical hybrid quantum‑classical fraud detection models tailored to NISQ devices and leveraging both quantum and classical strengths, as well as advancing quantum hardware, software, and cloud access (QPUs via quantum cloud) to democratize QML deployment. The authors advocate research into fully quantum‑native fraud detection frameworks and quantum‑native anomaly detection capable of exploring vast solution spaces, improved quantum data structures such as QRAM for real‑time fraud detection, and combining quantum forecasting algorithms with fraud detection to enable proactive threat identification. They also emphasize the need for post‑quantum cryptography standards, interdisciplinary collaboration across physics, computer science, data science, and finance, and ultimately envision QML becoming a core pillar of financial security as these technical and ecosystem developments mature.
