  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The authors encourage enterprises, particularly in finance, to begin exploring quantum technologies now to build strategic knowledge and intellectual property, even while the technology is still maturing. They suggest developing quantum algorithm–based services that can improve outcomes, speed, and control for complex optimization and analytics problems, and considering deployment models such as colocation data centers to support hybrid quantum–classical architectures when on-premises and cloud resources must be integrated. More broadly, they imply that further work is needed on operational models and architectures that integrate quantum tools into existing financial infrastructure.

  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: The paper does not present original experiments, datasets, or quantitative evaluations; instead it provides high-level methodological guidance for developing quantum-enhanced finance applications. It stresses that algorithms and use cases must be adapted to real-world conditions using authentic data, and recommends a workflow where quantum circuits are first evaluated on robust simulators prior to hardware execution. The authors describe the current software stack as relying on QASM embedded in high-level Python frameworks, and note the necessity of constructing pre- and post-processing procedures to manage inputs, models, and gate parameters.     [codes: C-001, C-002, C-008, C-009, C-024, C-032, C-055, C-011, C-015, C-032, C-036]

=== PAPER 76a87207ec32 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets financial fraud detection as an anomaly detection problem on highly imbalanced, real‑world transaction datasets, formulating it via principal component analysis that measures a point’s distance from the distribution of genuine data and applies a threshold-based anomaly score. This PCA-based anomaly detection (AD‑PCA) framework is positioned as an unsupervised approach suited to fraud datasets dominated by genuine transactions. The authors instantiate this formulation on a real-world bank transfer dataset with about 11×10^6 samples and 20 features.
    [codes: C-003, C-009, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-034]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors design a complete quantum circuit–level implementation of a PCA-based anomaly detection algorithm, AD-QPCA, built around quantum Amplitude Estimation (AE) applied in superposition over samples and features. Classical data are basis-encoded and accessed via simulated QRAM, then converted to amplitude encoding through controlled rotations; the circuit introduces signed-value representation, a Dist gate for signed differences, multi-controlled phase flips for negative contributions, and assumes mean values and intermediate quantities are stored and reused via QRAM, while one subtraction step is handled classically. The implementation is realized and released in Qiskit, with closed-form expressions for qubit counts, circuit depth, gate and T-gate resources derived for each subroutine.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-012, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-031, C-032, C-014]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The paper reports that, at circuit level, the proposed AD-QPCA design achieves logarithmic scaling in depth and T-depth with respect to both the number of training samples and the number of features, which the authors interpret as an exponential speed-up over the classical AD-PCA algorithm under their model of superposition access. They apply their analysis to a real-world fraud detection dataset of approximately 11×10^6 samples and 20 features, deriving asymptotic and closed-form resource estimates; for this instance, they estimate that a fault-tolerant implementation would require on the order of 1.41×10^10 logical qubits and 1.16×10^14 total gates. The authors further claim that the logarithmic-depth quantum implementation exhibits an exponential advantage starting from datasets with roughly 2×10^5 samples and 100 features, and note that the final anomaly-score computation can be performed classically in O(1) time.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-013, C-022, C-023, C-030, C-031, C-034, C-035, C-037]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The quantum method is consistently benchmarked conceptually against the classical AD-PCA algorithm, which the authors cite as having O(md^2) complexity in the number of samples m and features d. They argue that by leveraging AE and QRAM-like superposition access over samples and dimensions, their circuit achieves logarithmic depth in M and d, leading to an exponential asymptotic speed-up in query complexity relative to the classical baseline, with the crossover to quantum advantage projected around datasets of 2×10^5 samples and 100 features or equivalent scale.
    [codes: C-016, C-023, C-036, C-037]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors emphasize that high-level algorithmic formulations alone do not suffice to judge quantum advantage and that detailed circuit-level resource analysis is required, implicitly acknowledging that prior work may have overestimated practicality. They note trade-offs in data encoding: amplitude encoding offers exponential compression but generally demands deep circuits and complicates arithmetic, while basis encoding incurs linear qubit and gate costs; they position their approach as a compromise but do not claim to avoid these trade-offs. A major hardware limitation is that QRAM has not yet been physically realized, so their scheme relies on QRAM-like behavior and a specific simulated Bucket Brigade model. The resource estimates rest on explicit assumptions about tolerated numerical errors being bounded by the discretization error from n-qubit encodings and the complexity of AE, and the authors caution that quantum compilation and fault-tolerant error-correction protocols are expected to increase the practical cost by at least two orders of magnitude beyond their logical-level counts.
    [codes: C-007, C-008, C-019, C-021, C-033, C-038]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: For future work, the authors suggest that improvements in QRAM design and the development of dedicated hardware devices could substantially reduce the resource requirements of their scheme, potentially making it more practical. They also propose extending their AD-QPCA framework to integrate kernel methods as a promising research direction.
    [codes: C-039, C-040]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims novelty in presenting a complete, circuit-level quantum implementation of PCA-based anomaly detection tailored to classical input data, including the introduction of signed-value data representation and new subcircuits such as the Dist gate for sign-magnitude differences and phase-flip mechanisms for negative contributions. Additionally, the authors highlight their closed-form resource analysis for qubits, depth, gate counts, T-depth and T-count for each subroutine, arguing that this detailed accounting supports a theoretical exponential quantum advantage in fraud detection under specified assumptions, and they further contribute by releasing a full Qiskit implementation as open-source code.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-009, C-011, C-012, C-028, C-029, C-013, C-023, C-037, C-014]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the authors stress the importance of resource estimation under near-term hardware constraints and therefore provide detailed, closed-form counts for basic gate types, including multi-controlled, rotational, and arithmetic gates, aggregated into a systematic resource table. They ground their analysis in a real-world, large-scale fraud dataset (≈11×10^6 samples, 20 features) and rely on a specific QRAM simulation model (parallel Bucket Brigade) to approximate realistic access patterns, while also explicitly documenting their error and discretization assumptions. Reproducibility is supported by a public Qiskit implementation of all subroutines, and the hybrid design choice to perform certain arithmetic (a key subtraction step) classically is made explicit in the algorithm description.
    [codes: C-003, C-006, C-008, C-024, C-026, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-014]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper situates its work within broader claims that quantum computing is promising for data-intensive tasks due to potential exponential speed-ups, and remarks that amplitude encoding often favors sign-magnitude representation because negative values can be expressed directly as negative amplitudes, contextualizing their representation choices relative to general quantum machine learning practice.
    [codes: C-005, C-027, C-020]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is directly relevant to fraud_detection because it targets fraud as an anomaly detection problem on a highly imbalanced, real-world transaction dataset and evaluates a quantum PCA-based detector on that setting (C-003, C-015, C-034). Its main value for the silo is the circuit-level quantum implementation and resource analysis for detecting rare anomalous transactions, which is the core operational pattern in fraud and AML screening (C-001, C-002, C-013, C-023).
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: Fraud is framed as highly imbalanced anomaly detection, matching the core fraud-detection setting.
    [codes: C-003, C-015, C-017, C-018, C-034  dim_source: dimension_1]
  KEY_POINT 2: The paper provides a complete quantum circuit implementation of PCA-based anomaly detection, not just a high-level idea.
    [codes: C-001, C-009, C-010, C-012, C-028, C-029  dim_source: dimension_2]
  KEY_POINT 3: The claimed logarithmic-depth scaling is important because fraud screening can involve very large transaction volumes.
    [codes: C-004, C-013, C-023, C-036, C-037  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 4: The resource estimates and hardware caveats are especially relevant for deployment feasibility in fraud monitoring.
    [codes: C-002, C-006, C-021, C-031, C-033, C-035, C-038  dim_source: dimension_5]
  KEY_POINT 5: The real-world dataset and open Qiskit implementation make the work more directly actionable for fraud-detection research.
    [codes: C-003, C-014, C-032, C-034  dim_source: dimension_8]

=== PAPER 76bf72482fcf (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames financial fraud detection as an increasingly complex task due to rapid growth in digital transactions and evolving fraud tactics, operating on large, high-dimensional, and extremely imbalanced transactional datasets. It positions quantum machine learning as a potential alternative or improvement to existing machine learning approaches for this binary fraud/no-fraud classification problem, while recognizing that the practical relevance of such quantum models is still unclear.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-004, C-006, C-008, C-030, C-012]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The review focuses on quantum support vector machines (QSVMs) that use quantum kernels to map classically preprocessed features into high-dimensional Hilbert spaces, and variational quantum classifiers (VQCs) trained via hybrid classical–quantum optimization loops. In the proposed architecture, a dedicated quantum path encodes dimension-reduced classical features into quantum states, feeds them into QSVM or VQC models, and uses shared evaluation metrics with classical models, while noting that quantum preprocessing is constrained by limited qubit availability.
    [codes: C-019, C-020, C-011, C-026, C-024, C-033, C-032, C-027]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Synthesizing peer-reviewed studies on real transactional datasets, the paper reports that classical ensemble models (Random Forests and Gradient Boosting Machines) consistently achieve high AUC and recall in fraud detection, with deep learning sometimes offering slight recall gains at the cost of interpretability and higher computational complexity. For quantum models, the authors note that QSVMs may enhance class separability and can, on reduced feature sets, perform significantly better than some classical classifiers on certain datasets, but that these gains are not robust across datasets; hybrid classical–quantum approaches exhibit the most balanced overall performance profile.
    [codes: C-015, C-016, C-039, C-040, C-021, C-036, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-046]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The core contribution is a detailed comparative study of traditional and quantum machine learning models for financial fraud detection, including comparisons of system architectures and performance trends. The authors describe how classical models—from early statistical methods to decision trees, ensembles, and deep learning—effectively capture non-linear patterns in high-dimensional, imbalanced data and are widely deployed in industry, whereas quantum models (QSVM, VQC) are still experimental and show dataset-dependent advantages only under constrained, low-dimensional feature settings. Overall, the paper concludes that classical machine learning remains more mature and reliable for current fraud detection practice, with quantum and hybrid models offering promising but as yet non-robust improvements.
    [codes: C-002, C-004, C-006, C-007, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-021, C-036, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-046]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper repeatedly emphasizes limitations around quantum machine learning for fraud detection: despite theoretical benefits, the practical relevance of quantum models in real-world financial settings is still unclear. Most empirical QML results cited are obtained on simulators or small-scale hardware, so they may not reflect production conditions, and are further constrained by noise, decoherence, limited qubit counts, and strong sensitivity to feature dimensionality, which necessitates aggressive dimensionality reduction before encoding. The authors also highlight that reported quantum performance improvements are not robust across datasets, indicating lower maturity compared with classical approaches. Methodologically, they note the risk of inflated performance in simulated settings and deliberately restrict their synthesis to studies on real-life transactional datasets to mitigate this issue; they further point out that quantum preprocessing is more limited by hardware, restricting feature usage.
    [codes: C-012, C-022, C-023, C-029, C-030, C-032, C-033, C-041, C-043, C-047]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The authors argue that near-term progress should focus on hybrid classical–quantum systems, which they see as the most practical path for realizing short-term quantum benefits and enabling gradual adoption in financial institutions. They recommend future research on designing noise-resilient quantum algorithms, scalable feature-encoding schemes compatible with limited-qubit hardware, and frameworks to assess regulatory and operational aspects of deploying such systems in finance.
    [codes: C-005, C-045, C-048, C-049, C-050, C-051]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper presents itself as a detailed comparative survey of classical, quantum, and hybrid machine learning models for financial fraud detection, explicitly aiming to compare system architectures and performance trends using empirical findings from peer-reviewed studies on real transactional datasets rather than synthetic experiments. It further highlights hybrid classical–quantum frameworks as an emerging and particularly promising research direction within this domain.
    [codes: C-004, C-006, C-007, C-008, C-028, C-029, C-005, C-044]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the review defines clear research objectives to survey classical and quantum algorithms for fraud detection and to compare their system architectures and performance trends. It bases its comparative analysis on empirical results from peer-reviewed studies using real-life transactional datasets, intentionally avoiding synthetic experimentation to reduce the risk of inflated simulated performance. The paper describes common data characteristics—large volumes, high dimensionality, and extreme class imbalance—and documents standard preprocessing steps such as PCA-based anonymization of sensitive attributes, SMOTE and cost-adjusted loss functions for class imbalance, and dimensionality reduction (PCA or information-gain ranking) before quantum encoding due to qubit constraints. Classical models are typically trained with cross-validation and grid or randomized hyperparameter search, and all models are evaluated with fraud-oriented metrics that emphasize precision, F1-score, and AUC, using a shared evaluation layer in the proposed architecture to ensure comparability.
    [codes: C-006, C-007, C-008, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-018, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-038]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: Several codes provide contextual characterizations of model capabilities that underpin but do not directly extend other dimensions: classical ensembles are noted for their robustness to noisy features and concept drift in fraud detection, while quantum models are said to seek richer feature representations via superposition and entanglement; deep learning is described as learning hierarchical feature representations. These points enrich the conceptual backdrop for the comparative analysis but are not framed as standalone findings.
    [codes: C-011, C-017, C-035]
[SILO OVERLAY]

=== PAPER 775d4b298ecd (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames anomaly detection in consumer electronics—including IoT devices—as a cybersecurity-critical task where irregular patterns can manifest as anything from minor glitches to severe vulnerabilities, motivating robust anomaly detection systems across the sector. Within this broad setting, the authors position quantum machine learning as a candidate technology for building anomaly detection systems (ADS) that can better handle resource demands and adapt to novel anomalies than classical machine learning. A specific finance-relevant instance is credit card fraud detection, treated as an anomaly detection problem within consumer electronics payments infrastructure.
    [codes: C-001, C-005, C-006, C-007, C-008, C-009, C-011]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors present a generic QML workflow for anomaly detection that starts with dimensionality reduction to fit near-term quantum hardware, encodes classical data into quantum states, processes it through a quantum circuit that maps data into a higher-dimensional feature space, and uses classical optimization to train variational parameters before predicting anomalies from measured expectation values, often via parity-based post-processing. They survey and describe several algorithm families: variational quantum circuits and QNNs with angle encoding and parameter-shift optimization; hybrid quantum–classical neural networks where PQC-based quantum layers or PQCs in autoencoders are combined with classical encoders/decoders and convolutional front-ends; quantum kernels implemented via IQP circuits and kernel SVMs; unsupervised methods such as quantum clustering via swap tests and quantum ensemble methods exploiting superposition; and quantum reinforcement learning that uses measurement-induced collapse for action selection. Across case studies, they highlight concrete instantiations such as a hybrid deep network using VQC layers, a hybrid autoencoder with a 4‑qubit PQC latent layer, a QCNN for surveillance anomalies, and a quantum-kernel fraud detector using PCA plus an IQP feature map with data re-uploading.
    [codes: C-002, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-042, C-045, C-049, C-051, C-052, C-060, C-065]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The survey reports that in cited case studies, quantum or hybrid models can reach competitive or strong anomaly detection performance: quantum deep learning–based IoMT security models achieved 92.23–99.34% accuracy; a hybrid deep QML network (QHDNN) obtained best AUC scores of 89.41% on Fashion‑MNIST and 88.24% on MNIST; a hybrid autoencoder with a PQC latent layer yielded 64.1% recall on a gas turbine dataset and recalls of 100%, 72.7%, and 76% on Musk, Arrhythmia, and Satlog benchmarks; a QCNN for surveillance anomalies reported accuracies up to 1.00 in several crime/normal classification scenarios; and a quantum-kernel–based credit card fraud classifier showed a 15% absolute improvement in average precision over a classical kernel, reaching 70% average precision. The authors also state more generally that hybrid quantum–classical neural architectures tend to be more stable than stand-alone PQCs and that quantum models with comparable parameters can sometimes outperform classical networks.
    [codes: C-018, C-022, C-026, C-029, C-030, C-033, C-035, C-037, C-038, C-041, C-044, C-046, C-048, C-050, C-053, C-056, C-057]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: Across the surveyed case studies, the authors consistently report comparisons to classical baselines and claim relative advantages for quantum or hybrid approaches. For IoMT security, a fused semi‑supervised reinforcement learning model with quantum feature extraction is described as being compared against state-of-the-art classical RL, DNN, CNN, and quantum DNN approaches, with quantum deep models achieving up to 99.34% accuracy. The QHDNN hybrid deep network is positioned as an enhancement over purely classical DSVDD-style architectures, with improved AUC performance on image anomalies, and hybrid QNN architectures are noted to behave more stably than stand‑alone PQCs. In power-plant and benchmark anomaly datasets, the hybrid autoencoder is said to significantly increase performance over classical autoencoders (though the survey passage only quotes the quantum metrics). For surveillance anomalies, the J‑QCNN is reported to outperform a classical J‑DCNN in multiple crime/normal classification scenarios. In the finance-relevant credit card fraud case, the authors highlight that a quantum kernel–based classifier achieved a 15% higher average precision than a classical kernel baseline. They further generalize that quantum models with comparable numbers of parameters can perform better than classical networks.
    [codes: C-029, C-030, C-039, C-041, C-042, C-044, C-048, C-050, C-053, C-056, C-057]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper explicitly acknowledges several limitations of both classical and quantum approaches. On the classical side, machine learning–based anomaly detectors are described as resource‑intensive, slow to train, prone to overfitting, and poorly adaptive to novel anomalies emerging during operation. For QML, the authors stress that current quantum hardware can only handle small-dimensional inputs due to limited qubit counts, making dimensionality reduction and subsampling almost always necessary; this restricts direct use of raw, high-dimensional consumer electronics and transaction data. Kernel-based quantum methods inherit a quadratic cost in the number of samples for Gram matrix computation. At the device level, the survey emphasizes that near-term quantum devices suffer from substantial noise and operational uncertainty, with decoherence singled out as a major practical problem. They also note that quantum error correction will be required to reach fault-tolerant machines capable of industrial-scale anomaly detection systems, implying that many envisioned advantages are not yet realizable on current NISQ hardware.
    [codes: C-008, C-009, C-012, C-013, C-054, C-055, C-062, C-063, C-064]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The authors argue that realizing industrial anomaly detection systems based on QML will depend on the advent of fault-tolerant quantum computers, underpinned by effective quantum error correction. They suggest that as error-corrected, large-scale devices become available, QML algorithms—especially those leveraging quantum neural network building blocks—could be deployed in practical ADS across consumer electronics, and they imply that further work is needed to address current hardware noise and decoherence issues.
    [codes: C-059, C-064, C-065]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims novelty primarily as a comprehensive survey: it introduces quantum machine learning and its applications specifically for anomaly detection in consumer electronics, and the authors state that, to their knowledge, no prior work has provided such a focused survey of anomalies in consumer electronic devices. Their contribution includes a generic QML workflow for anomaly detection, a structured overview of supervised, unsupervised, and reinforcement learning–based QML algorithms in this context, and five detailed case studies illustrating recent applications, including a quantum-kernel approach to credit card fraud detection.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-004, C-011, C-012]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the survey emphasizes a generic but concrete QML workflow for anomaly detection, detailing steps such as dimensionality reduction to respect qubit limits, data encoding into quantum states, variational circuit optimization via classical gradient methods (e.g., parameter-shift), and parity-based post-processing for binary anomaly decisions. It systematically categorizes supervised, unsupervised, and reinforcement learning QML algorithms, highlighting typical encoding choices like angle encoding and standard practices such as repeated measurements for gradient estimation. In discussing case studies, the authors describe dataset choices (e.g., MNIST/Fashion‑MNIST, power-plant gas turbine sensors, surveillance video datasets, and credit card fraud data), architecture components (e.g., PQC positions in hybrid networks, IQP circuits with data re-uploading), and evaluation metrics (accuracy, AUC, recall, average precision), although some implementation details—like exact encoding schemes or full classical baselines—remain at a high level, reflecting typical limitations of secondary survey reporting rather than primary reproducible experiments.
    [codes: C-002, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-023, C-027, C-028, C-032, C-034, C-039, C-040, C-042, C-043, C-045, C-047, C-049, C-051, C-052]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The authors characterize anomaly detection as a promising application area for QML because mapping classical data into complex high-dimensional quantum feature spaces can make anomalies more distinguishable, and they note that near-term quantum algorithm development accelerated after the introduction of the Variational Quantum Eigensolver. They also highlight that anomalies in many domains, including consumer electronics, may closely resemble normal data, underscoring the intrinsic difficulty of the task and the appeal of more expressive quantum representations.
    [codes: C-031, C-058, C-060, C-061]

=== PAPER 77f0bbb6e5f3 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets clustering of blockchain transaction data, emphasizing that such data are high-dimensional, temporally entangled, and noisy, which challenges conventional clustering methods in sensitive financial settings like AML, fraud detection, and DeFi monitoring. It specifically frames an interpretability gap for quantum-enhanced clustering, arguing that limited transparency restricts deployment in these financial governance tasks and highlighting interpretability as an under-explored aspect of QML. The authors operationalize the problem as selecting and interpreting an appropriate number of clusters (K=2–6) for Ethereum-based carbon-credit transaction networks.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-010, C-011, C-012, C-019, C-013, C-021]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The study employs two quantum-enhanced feature extraction strategies within the Quantum-Train (QT) paradigm: Random Quantum Features (QF), where classical neural network parameters are derived from randomly initialized, untrained QNNs, and a fully trained QNN that is optimized end-to-end with a SwAV self-supervised loss. Transaction features are preprocessed (including cyclical timestamp encoding, label encoding for categorical attributes, and robust scaling for key numerical variables) before being passed through these quantum-enhanced feature networks and subsequently clustered with K-Means; the SwAV loss is explicitly formulated and used as the training objective for the QNN-based feature generator.
    [codes: C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Quantitatively, the fully trained QNN features achieve very strong internal clustering scores, including a near-perfect Silhouette Score of 0.999777 and extremely low Davies-Bouldin index at K=2, with the authors noting that fully trained QNNs outperform random QF on these metrics overall. Qualitatively, the AI Agent reveals that QNN-driven models frequently produce singleton clusters that isolate high-value or single-sender transactions and remain strongly value-sensitive as K increases, whereas QF tends to produce more diverse, heterogeneous clusters that capture token diversity and network-flow patterns. Combining metric-based evaluation and AI-agent semantics, the analysis converges on K=3 as the preferred configuration, described as balancing clarity and complexity and endorsed as the optimal clustering choice over the examined range.
    [codes: C-006, C-007, C-008, C-034, C-035, C-038, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-046, C-051]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: Classical clustering methods and internal metrics (Silhouette, Davies-Bouldin, Calinski-Harabasz) are used to select K and benchmark clustering quality, but the primary methodological comparison is between quantum feature strategies (QNN vs. QF) rather than between quantum and purely classical feature pipelines. The authors report that fully trained QNNs outperform random QF on these classical metrics, while emphasizing that QF can still be competitive in its best runs. In the qualitative discussion, they contrast QNN’s precision and anomaly-spotlighting behavior with QF’s more holistic representation of ecosystem activity, suggesting complementary roles rather than a strict superiority claim over classical baselines.
    [codes: C-004, C-030, C-006, C-040, C-047, C-048]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors explicitly acknowledge trade-offs and risks in both quantum feature strategies: QNN, while precise and effective at isolating impactful outliers and singleton clusters, risks over-fragmentation and may overlook more subtle transactional patterns in the data. Conversely, QF is said to provide a broader, more holistic view of the transaction landscape, but may over-generalize patterns and thus fail to pinpoint critical anomalies. These are presented as inherent behavioral limitations of each approach, motivating the need for hybrid or ensemble strategies.
    [codes: C-047, C-048, C-049, C-050]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work directions include developing dynamic and adaptive clustering frameworks in which AI Agents iteratively refine clustering parameters as blockchain data streams evolve, extending the methodology to multi-modal blockchain datasets, and investigating ensemble models that combine QNN and QF to improve both interpretability and performance. These plans position the current study as a first step toward more adaptive and integrated quantum–AI systems for blockchain analytics.
    [codes: C-053, C-054, C-055]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims a fourfold contribution centered on interpretable quantum-assisted blockchain analytics: it advances quantum-enhanced feature engineering for challenging, high-dimensional blockchain data; introduces a two-stage hybrid framework that tightly couples internal metric-based evaluation with AI Agent-assisted qualitative interpretation; operationalizes AI Agents not just as passive explainers but as active proposers of cluster adjustments and strategic parameter insights; and positions these elements as collectively pushing the interpretability frontier in quantum-assisted blockchain analysis, particularly for sensitive financial applications.
    [codes: C-003, C-009, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-020, C-037, C-015]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study follows a structured two-stage design: Stage 1 systematically evaluates K from 2 to 6 using K-Means and internal clustering metrics, while Stage 2 leverages an AI Agent based on large language models to perform semantic analyses of cluster outputs. The underlying dataset consists of Ethereum MCO2 carbon-credit transactions focusing on ten major nodes, with a detailed preprocessing pipeline that includes cyclical timestamp encoding, label encoding for multiple categorical fields, and robust scaling of key numerical attributes. On the interpretability side, a pre-processing chain re-encodes hashed identifiers to improve language-model efficiency, and the Clustered Information Base plus multi-level Knowledge Base provide hierarchical and persistent structures for AI-Agent reasoning; the agent is explicitly tasked to answer specific interpretability questions and to interact closely with Stage-1 metrics, aiming to couple numerical rigor with qualitative depth.
    [codes: C-003, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-027, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-036, C-037]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The qualitative analysis identifies recurring cluster archetypes: singleton clusters in QNN outcomes representing extreme or high-impact transaction behaviors, and diverse clusters in QF outputs capturing varied token types and address interactions; these patterns are also discussed at a higher level as QNN’s precision versus QF’s holistic ecosystem view. Additionally, interpretability is framed as an under-explored frontier in QML more broadly, and AI Agents are situated within prior work as tools that can summarize complex model behaviors, providing contextual justification for their use in this setting.
    [codes: C-019, C-020, C-034, C-035, C-047, C-048]

=== PAPER 7a159ec77866 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames quantum computing as an evolutionary computing paradigm aimed at addressing complex, data- and computation-intensive problems in financial services, particularly in banking, insurance, high-frequency trading, and fraud/risk management. It formulates finance problems mainly as constrained and uncertain optimization, targeting three application groups: targeting and prediction (personalized services, fraud detection), trading optimization (portfolio rebalancing, derivatives valuation), and risk profiling (scenario simulations under regulatory pressure).
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-005, C-046, C-053, C-054, C-061, C-067, C-081]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The paper does not specify concrete quantum algorithms but conceptually describes quantum computation using qubits, superposition, and entanglement, emphasizing that quantum algorithms can avoid exhaustive sequential search and tackle combinatorial optimization. It discusses quantum logic gates, entangling gates, and system-level characteristics such as coherence time, connectivity, gate fidelity, and logical (error-corrected) qubits as key elements in realizing useful financial applications, and it notes superconducting and trapped-ion/atom-based qubits as primary hardware approaches.
    [codes: C-002, C-007, C-009, C-011, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-029, C-030, C-032, C-033, C-036, C-037, C-039, C-040, C-043, C-045, C-065]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: As a conceptual review, the paper reports no experimental or quantitative quantum results; instead it presents speculative performance claims. It asserts that entanglement and superposition can yield much higher speed and lower energy use than classical computers, that several computational issues could be solved more efficiently with quantum algorithms, and that as few as ~50 high-quality qubits could run certain algorithms in minutes. In finance, the authors claim that quantum computing could enlarge solution spaces beyond supercomputers, nearly double "power" with each added qubit, improve pattern discovery for targeting and prediction, enhance portfolio diversification and rebalancing, streamline settlement, accelerate and improve risk simulations, and improve investment gains, reduce capital needs, create new investment opportunities, and strengthen fraud, risk, and compliance management.
    [codes: C-010, C-011, C-014, C-018, C-028, C-030, C-037, C-047, C-048, C-049, C-050, C-060, C-065, C-066, C-070, C-071, C-072, C-073, C-074, C-080, C-081]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The authors contrast quantum and classical computing primarily at a conceptual level, arguing that classical machines encode information strictly in 0/1 bits, which restricts their ability and leads to intractability for certain combinatorial problems, whereas qubits can exist in multiple states simultaneously and be entangled. They claim quantum computers process data differently, excel at complex simulations and data analysis, and have solution spaces orders of magnitude larger than even supercomputers, while also emphasizing that everyday office applications may not see speedups. They further contend that some problems may never be solvable by traditional computers regardless of time, highlighting combinatorial growth of variables as a bottleneck that quantum approaches can mitigate by not testing all solutions sequentially.
    [codes: C-008, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-017, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-049]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper acknowledges multiple limitations and caveats. On hardware, it notes that quantum computers are difficult and expensive to manufacture, require ultra-cold environments, and involve fragile quantum states where even minor disturbances cause decoherence and destroy computation; all operations must complete before decoherence, and gate errors accumulate quickly, so low gate fidelity and limited gate depth can render large qubit counts relatively useless. Architecturally, superconducting systems are constrained by physical adjacency of qubits, and identical qubits plus sufficient fidelity (roughly enabling n×n gates for n qubits) are needed for useful computation. Application-wise, the authors explicitly state that quantum computing will not speed up routine applications such as Word or Excel, that real-time behavioral analytics and accurate fraud detection are currently difficult with existing systems (e.g., high false positives, slow onboarding), and that Monte Carlo-based risk models are constrained by error scaling. At the strategic level, they admit that commercial adoption may take several years and explicitly concede that there is no clear timeline for when quantum computing will realize its full potential or whether it will achieve all the proposed financial and real-world functions.
    [codes: C-015, C-016, C-017, C-021, C-022, C-027, C-031, C-034, C-035, C-038, C-041, C-042, C-044, C-056, C-057, C-059, C-063, C-064, C-068, C-082]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The authors situate quantum computing as a longer-term opportunity, suggesting that widespread commercial applications in finance may take several years and that future regulation will likely increase compliance costs, strengthening the case for more powerful risk tools. They highlight the combination of quantum and cloud computing as a way to expand accessible applications and foresee quantum manufacturers using quantum computers themselves to discover improved algorithms and qubit technologies. More broadly, they speculate on future uses in drug discovery, climate-change mitigation, and logistics optimization as areas where further research and development could unfold.
    [codes: C-051, C-069, C-075, C-079, C-076, C-077, C-078]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper positions its contribution as a conceptual exposition of how quantum computing—grounded in superposition, entanglement, and improved hardware metrics—could transform financial services across targeting and prediction, trading optimization, and risk profiling, particularly for high-frequency trading and fraud detection. It emphasizes the idea that quantum computing’s enlarged solution space and combinatorial optimization capabilities can unlock new arbitrage opportunities, enhance personalization, and restructure operational processes in ways classical computing cannot.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-005, C-046, C-047, C-048, C-049, C-050, C-052, C-060, C-065, C-070, C-071, C-072, C-073, C-074, C-080, C-081]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: The article is a high-level conceptual and review-style piece with no implemented quantum algorithms, datasets, or experiments, and thus offers no reproducible methodology or empirical evaluation. Its most technical elements concern system-assessment concepts—such as analyzing qubits alongside system-level qualities (connectivity, coherence, quality), defining coherence time, connectivity, gate fidelity, and error-corrected logical qubits, and arguing that qubit count alone is an inadequate power metric—rather than concrete financial modeling protocols. All reported numbers (e.g., qubit thresholds, fraud false-positive rates, revenue loss estimates) are cited or asserted rather than derived from original experiments presented in the paper.
    [codes: C-028, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-036, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-055, C-057, C-058]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper also situates financial applications within a broader cross-industry narrative for quantum computing, mentioning potential uses in agriculture, manufacturing, health, education, secure data sharing, medical and environmental sensing, battery manufacturing, drug discovery, climate-change mitigation, logistics route optimization, and the general resolution of both computational and real-world issues.
    [codes: C-006, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-075, C-076, C-077, C-078, C-080]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to fraud_detection because it repeatedly frames fraud detection as a core financial use case for quantum computing, especially for faster pattern discovery, classification, and risk/compliance management (C-004, C-057, C-060, C-074, C-081). Its main value for this silo is conceptual: it argues that quantum approaches could help with the current problems of high false positives, slow behavioral analytics, and limited real-time detection capacity, while also noting that the hardware and timeline remain uncertain (C-056, C-057, C-059, C-082).

=== PAPER 7cc9ce4dad7d (type: central) ===

=== PAPER 7f91dcfbca63 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames anomaly detection as selecting data points that deviate significantly from normal behaviour in datasets, a task relevant to domains such as finance, where it is applied to credit card fraud detection with highly imbalanced classes. This problem is cast as a Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization (QUBO) model in which a fixed number k of outliers must be selected, enabling solution by QUBO-oriented solvers and, in principle, quantum annealers.
    [codes: C-007, C-008, C-010, C-017, C-028]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The approach is "quantum-inspired" anomaly detection formulated as a QUBO, rather than run on a quantum processor: each data point corresponds to a binary variable, and the objective combines linear distances to a centroid with quadratic pairwise distances between selected points, weighted by a parameter α. The method uses a Mahalanobis distance metric, enforces an exact number of outliers via a quadratic penalty term with weight A > max(Q), and sparsifies the model by limiting quadratic terms to the k-furthest neighbours; benchmark instances are solved with the classical qbsolv QUBO solver, though the formulation is positioned as suitable for simulated quantum annealing or any QUBO-capable solver.
    [codes: C-001, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-033, C-034]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Using ROC AUC as the evaluation metric, the author reports that the proposed quantum-inspired QUBO anomaly detection algorithm shows improved accuracy compared with classical methods on both random Gaussian data and real-world datasets, including MNIST and a credit card fraud dataset. The paper also finds that limiting quadratic interactions to the k-furthest neighbours improves accuracy and may aid in embedding the model on hardware with constrained connectivity, though the current penalty formulation blocks near-term QPU use.
    [codes: C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The QUBO-based anomaly detector is explicitly benchmarked against five classical anomaly detection algorithms on synthetic Gaussian data, MNIST digit subsets, and a highly imbalanced credit card fraud dataset, with performance compared using ROC AUC. The author claims that the quantum-inspired method achieves improved accuracy over these classical baselines on both random and real-world datasets.
    [codes: C-002, C-010, C-023, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper acknowledges that current NISQ hardware, with number-limited noisy qubits, impacts the practicality of gate-based quantum anomaly detection algorithms. For the proposed QUBO formulation, the outlier selection penalty term induces all-to-all quadratic interactions, making the current model not solvable on near-term, connectivity-limited QPUs, despite sparsification of other quadratic terms. The author therefore explicitly notes that, in its present form, the approach is effectively restricted to classical or simulated quantum solvers rather than real quantum hardware.
    [codes: C-009, C-031, C-033]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: To enable execution on connectivity-limited quantum processors, the author suggests that an alternative method for constraining the number of selected outliers is needed, replacing the current penalty formulation that creates all-to-all couplings. Future work is thus directed at redesigning the selection mechanism so that the QUBO can be embedded on near-term QPUs.
    [codes: C-031, C-032]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The contribution claimed is a new quantum-inspired anomaly detection algorithm formulated as a QUBO that conceptually combines ideas from statistical and density-based anomaly detection methods, with a tunable trade-off between linear and quadratic distance terms and an explicit control over the number of outliers. The author further emphasizes that, compared with standard classical anomaly detectors, this formulation achieves improved accuracy on both synthetic and real-world benchmarks.
    [codes: C-001, C-010, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-029]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the paper situates the QUBO formulation relative to standard classical anomaly detection paradigms—statistical, density-based, and machine-learning-based—describing their underlying assumptions. Experiments are run on three benchmark types (random Gaussian data with varying standard deviations, MNIST digit subset configurations where a minority class is treated as outliers, and a public credit card fraud dataset with 0.172% fraud rate), and all instances are solved with the qbsolv QUBO solver and evaluated via ROC AUC. The algorithm includes a dataset-specific training phase to learn the α weighting parameter, uses Mahalanobis distance, and adopts a sparsification strategy limiting quadratic terms to the k-furthest neighbours, which the author notes benefits both accuracy and potential hardware embedding.
    [codes: C-011, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-018, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-030]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The introduction briefly motivates the work by noting that quantum machine learning combines quantum computing with machine learning, and that quantum computers may offer parallel computation and potential exponential speedups for certain problems, with quantum technologies claimed to have the potential to revolutionize various domains including areas where anomaly detection is used such as finance, healthcare, and cybersecurity. The author also underscores that, despite current hardware constraints, the proposed quantum-inspired anomaly detection algorithm can be applied at scale using simulated quantum annealing, simulated annealing, or any other QUBO solver, without being tied to quantum hardware.
    [codes: C-004, C-005, C-006, C-008, C-033, C-034]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to fraud_detection because it directly evaluates a QUBO-based anomaly detector on a highly imbalanced credit card fraud dataset and reports improved ROC AUC over classical baselines. Its formulation is especially pertinent to fraud settings where rare fraudulent transactions must be isolated from normal activity, while also highlighting current solver/hardware constraints.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: The method is explicitly tested on a credit card fraud dataset with extreme class imbalance.
    [codes: C-028, C-025, C-023  dim_source: dimension_8]
  KEY_POINT 2: The paper claims improved ROC AUC over classical anomaly detection methods on the fraud benchmark.
    [codes: C-002, C-025, C-029  dim_source: dimension_4]
  KEY_POINT 3: The QUBO formulation is designed to select a fixed number of outliers, which matches fraud-style rare anomaly selection.
    [codes: C-017, C-019, C-001  dim_source: dimension_2]
  KEY_POINT 4: The model uses a learned weighting parameter and Mahalanobis distance, with sparsification via k-furthest neighbors.

=== PAPER 80690e1e30a0 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames the problem as escalating real‑time cyber threats against financial institutions—such as DDoS, malicious APIs, bots, and AI‑augmented phishing—that classical, rule‑based cybersecurity mechanisms and static blacklists increasingly fail to handle due to processing power and scalability limits. It argues that this evolving threat landscape demands adaptive, real‑time detection and mitigation and motivates exploring quantum computing as a potentially more scalable response layer for financial IT infrastructures.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-008]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors conduct a theory‑driven review of three quantum (or quantum‑inspired) approaches: (1) continuous‑variable quantum neural networks (CV‑QNNs) for anomaly/fraud detection in financial data, implemented as hybrid quantum‑classical models where classical layers control parameters and an encoding layer converts bits into qumode states with a specified layered gate sequence; (2) the lattice‑based CRYSTALS‑Kyber post‑quantum cryptographic scheme (CPAPKE/CCAKEM) for securing financial communications, with tunable parameter sets and retrofitting into TLS handshakes via replacement of Diffie–Hellman; and (3) quantum‑enhanced Monte Carlo for risk modeling using Quantum Amplitude Estimation (QAE), where probabilities are encoded in qubit amplitudes and a generic QMC circuit structure is outlined and connected to IBM/Qiskit implementations.
    [codes: C-004, C-021, C-020, C-022, C-023, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-024, C-035, C-036, C-038, C-039, C-025, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: For anomaly detection, the paper cites prior work showing a CV‑QNN achieving an ROC‑AUC of 0.945 and reports that, relative to a classical analogue, such a model can reduce false positives by ~15% and detection latency by ~20% in banking intrusion‑detection scenarios. In cryptography, the authors emphasise Kyber’s standardization by NIST and its efficiency via the number‑theoretic transform as key practical outcomes rather than reporting new benchmarks. For quantum‑enhanced Monte Carlo, they restate the theoretical quadratic speed‑up of QAE and reference IBM implementations, highlighting that QMC with QAE can, in principle, reduce sampling requirements compared to classical Monte Carlo.
    [codes: C-031, C-032, C-033, C-037, C-040, C-043, C-011]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper contrasts CV‑QNN‑based anomaly detection with traditional intrusion‑detection systems and a classical analogue (described elsewhere as a CNN), claiming ~15% fewer false positives and ~20% lower detection latency for the quantum‑inspired model. It positions classical computing as facing fundamental scaling limits—such as degraded performance when transistors shrink—and argues that quantum superposition, entanglement, and exponentially scaling storage capacity could offer advantages over classical architectures for processing large‑scale, real‑time financial threat data.
    [codes: C-002, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-032]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors explicitly acknowledge that most of the reviewed quantum algorithms are at an early stage, often existing only as concepts or simulations rather than production‑ready tools, and they note an active debate over whether some supposed quantum advantages could be replicated on classical hardware. They highlight serious hardware limitations: quantum computers’ extreme sensitivity to environmental noise leading to errors and decoherence, the need for hard‑to‑maintain cryogenic temperatures near absolute zero, and the very large qubit overheads required for surface‑code fault tolerance (on the order of 1,000–10,000 physical qubits per logical qubit) along with additional latency and hardware complexity from syndrome‑extraction and repeated stabilizer measurements. More generally, they emphasize high error rates, environmental sensitivity, and integration complexity as major barriers to practical deployment of quantum solutions in financial infrastructures.
    [codes: C-006, C-044, C-045, C-046, C-047, C-048, C-049]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: For future work and adoption, the paper proposes a phased roadmap in which financial institutions run R&D pilots over the next few years and gradually move toward integrating quantum modules, with an aspirational goal of production‑grade quantum Monte Carlo–based risk modules by around 2030. The authors also stress that their current analysis relies on idealized assumptions and that real‑hardware characterization plus end‑to‑end benchmarking of quantum algorithms in realistic financial security environments are essential next steps.
    [codes: C-050, C-051, C-052]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper positions its contribution as a theoretical exploration and synthesis of how three classes of quantum and post‑quantum techniques—CV‑QNNs, Kyber, and quantum‑enhanced Monte Carlo—could be applied to real‑time threat mitigation in financial IT infrastructures. It claims to provide actionable strategic guidance for financial institutions by mapping these algorithms to concrete use cases (anomaly detection, secure communication, and risk modeling) and by assessing their technological readiness and integration paths rather than introducing a new quantum algorithm or reporting large‑scale hardware experiments.
    [codes: C-004, C-007, C-020, C-021, C-026]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the work is primarily a qualitative, theory‑driven literature review that selects and analyses three algorithmic classes—quantum anomaly detection via CV‑QNN, post‑quantum Kyber cryptography, and QMC/QAE‑based risk modeling—rather than a full empirical study. The CV‑QNN part relies on previously published results for ROC‑AUC and appears to be based on simulations or conceptual models, without detailed dataset descriptions or hardware resource reporting. The authors themselves note that many of the considered algorithms are only conceptual or simulation‑based, and they call out the need for real hardware characterization and end‑to‑end benchmarking to validate performance claims in practical financial settings.
    [codes: C-020, C-021, C-022, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-031, C-044, C-052]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: Several background and context codes do not directly alter the methodological or result‑focused dimensions but clarify the conceptual framing: the paper asserts that quantum superposition and entanglement enable improved processing and claims that quantum storage capacity scales exponentially (C‑009, C‑010). It also provides technical background on Kyber’s security assumption, namely the presumed hardness of the module learning‑with‑errors problem in lattices (C‑034).
    [codes: C-009, C-010, C-034]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to fraud_detection because it centers on real-time anomaly detection using CV-QNNs, including a cited fraud-detection use case and performance gains over a classical baseline. === PAPER 8372b98f0875 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets credit card fraud detection as an anomaly-detection problem and develops quantum protocols for identifying fraudulent transactions within highly imbalanced credit card data. The task is framed both as supervised classification and as unsupervised anomaly detection on a subsampled and balanced dataset of 500 transactions including 25 fraud cases.
    [codes: C-001, C-010, C-017, C-027]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors use quantum kernel methods where classical PCA-reduced features are embedded into N-qubit quantum states via a circuit-defined feature map, and similarity is measured through state overlaps κ(x,x′)=|⟨ψ(x)|ψ(x′)⟩|² evaluated as return probabilities. They primarily employ IQP-style circuits with threefold data re-uploading as the quantum feature map, scaling each input feature by a prefactor η=0.1 before encoding, and briefly note an alternative projective kernel based on local Pauli observables.
    [codes: C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Using average precision as the main metric, the authors report that quantum fraud detection can challenge and in some regimes surpass classical protocols as the number of features/qubits increases, particularly when using IQP kernels with re-uploading. In supervised settings, quantum kernels show a clear performance separation from classical RBF kernels at N=20 features and appear less prone to overfitting as dimensionality increases. In unsupervised anomaly detection, IQP-based quantum kernels achieve up to AP≈0.7 at N=20 qubits, corresponding to a reported 15% gain over the classical baseline, and re-uploading is specifically associated with better average precision among quantum variants.
    [codes: C-002, C-004, C-005, C-006, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-022, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The study establishes classical baselines using both supervised (logistic regression and SVC) and unsupervised (one-class SVM) methods evaluated with average precision, observing that supervised classical performance peaks around 5–10 features and degrades with more features while the OC-SVM’s AP gradually increases then saturates around 0.55 at ~15 features. Against these baselines, quantum kernels—especially IQP circuits with re-uploading—are reported to offer slightly higher supervised performance than logistic regression/SVC in the 5–10 feature regime and a clear advantage at 20 features, and in the unsupervised setting to outperform OC-SVM with an AP increase of about 15% at 20 qubits.
    [codes: C-003, C-009, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-022, C-025, C-027, C-028, C-029]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge that quantum-kernel fraud detection is currently better suited to checking highly prioritised transactions rather than en masse screening, due to computational and latency constraints. Training cost is highlighted as large because Gram-matrix construction scales quadratically with the number of samples, and inference requires O(N_d N_s) kernel evaluations, which can be prohibitive at scale unless per-sample testing can be reduced to sub-millisecond times for real-time use. They also note that achieving a strong, practically relevant quantum advantage will likely require going beyond the 20-qubit regime studied here, towards 50–100 qubits and deeper quantum kernels, implying that the present results are at a relatively small scale.
    [codes: C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-040]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work directions include developing tailored strategies for constructing Gram matrices using randomized or sparse approaches instead of brute-force full evaluation, in order to reduce quadratic scaling in sample size. The authors also emphasize the need to optimize the number of measurement shots so that kernel estimates are near-deterministic while keeping Nshots as small as possible, potentially exploiting structure in the kernel.
    [codes: C-038, C-039]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper positions its main contribution as developing and empirically studying quantum kernel-based protocols for anomaly (fraud) detection on credit card data, with a focus on unsupervised quantum machine learning. It further claims that IQP-based quantum kernels with data re-uploading can challenge and surpass well-tuned classical baselines in terms of average precision as feature and qubit counts increase, providing an early indication of potential quantum advantage in this fraud-detection setting.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-004, C-005, C-022, C-025, C-027, C-028, C-029]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses a heavily subsampled and artificially balanced version of a public credit card fraud dataset (500 samples with 25 frauds), applies standard scaling followed by PCA to retain only the first N principal components, and consistently evaluates performance via the threshold-free average precision metric, explicitly preferring AP over F1 for imbalanced anomaly detection. Classical baselines are trained with hyperparameters tuned by random search using cross-validation AP, and the same PCA+scaling pipeline feeds both classical and quantum models, with quantum encoding implemented via a feature map that scales each feature by η=0.1 before loading into the circuit.
    [codes: C-002, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-018, C-023, C-024]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The authors connect model performance improvements to business value by noting that even a few-percent increase in average precision for fraud detection can translate into multi-million savings for financial institutions. They also discuss hardware-specific timing estimates, suggesting that superconducting circuits may enable microsecond-scale circuit evaluations, trapped ions offer slower but high-fidelity operations at roughly 100 µs per sequence (about 10 kHz), and photonic systems could in principle achieve the fastest training times, though such photonic platforms are not yet fully mature for deterministic gates.
    [codes: C-030, C-035, C-036, C-037]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is directly about credit card fraud detection framed as anomaly detection, making it highly relevant to fraud_detection. Its main value for the silo is the comparison of quantum kernels against classical baselines on imbalanced fraud data, with results and limitations that speak to detection quality, scalability, and real-time screening constraints.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: The paper’s core task is credit card fraud detection as anomaly detection, evaluated with average precision on a fraud-imbalanced dataset.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-010, C-009  dim_source: dimension_1]
  KEY_POINT 2: Quantum kernels with IQP circuits and threefold re-uploading are the main method used for fraud detection.
    [codes: C-018, C-019, C-020, C-023, C-024, C-022  dim_source: dimension_2]

    [codes: C-004, C-005, C-006, C-025, C-027, C-028, C-029  dim_source: dimension_3]

    [codes: C-003, C-012, C-013, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-025, C-027  dim_source: dimension_4]

=== PAPER 85e9109e6bb0 (type: central) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets credit card fraud detection on a highly imbalanced Kaggle benchmark dataset, where fraudulent transactions constitute less than 0.1% of the data and 38 features describe each transaction. The fraud-detection task is framed as a supervised binary classification problem under extreme class imbalance, with fraud as the minority class.
    [codes: C-002, C-020, C-021, C-022]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors use CVQBoost, a continuous-variable extension of the QBoost boosting algorithm, tailored to run as a continuous quadratic solver on Quantum Computing Inc.’s Dirac-3 entropy quantum computing (EQC) hardware. EQC is described as a dissipative optimization paradigm that finds minima of a quadratic Hamiltonian in a native continuum encoding, allowing continuous, positive weights with a sum constraint rather than binary QUBO variables.
    [codes: C-001, C-009, C-010, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: On the Kaggle fraud benchmark, CVQBoost on Dirac-3 is reported to maintain competitive AUC accuracy relative to XGBoost, with performance improving as training data are more strongly balanced and even surpassing XGBoost under ADASYN-based balancing. The authors claim CVQBoost achieves superior speed and power efficiency and a significant training-time advantage, with Dirac-3 runtimes remaining nearly constant as training samples grow, leading to a runtime crossover around 20,000 samples and consistently shorter runtimes than XGBoost as feature counts increase. On large synthetic datasets up to tens of millions of samples and hundreds of features, they report that CVQBoost scales linearly in feature dimension while XGBoost exhibits quadratic growth, concluding that CVQBoost on Dirac-3 is well-suited for large-scale classification tasks.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-006, C-011, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-034, C-036, C-039, C-040, C-042]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: XGBoost is used as the main classical baseline, with the authors benchmarking CVQBoost against it in terms of AUC and runtime. They find that at low class-balance ratios (minority-to-majority 0.01–0.02), XGBoost achieves higher AUC, while CVQBoost catches up and can surpass XGBoost as the training data become more balanced, especially under ADASYN. For runtime, single-core and multi-core XGBoost show sharply increasing training times with more data and features, with multi-core performance exhibiting diminishing returns and an apparent optimum around eight cores; GPU-based XGBoost is faster on smaller datasets, but the authors report that CVQBoost shows slightly better scalability than GPU XGBoost as data size grows. In solver-level comparisons, other classical quadratic solvers are faster for small Hamiltonians but scale poorly for larger instances relative to Dirac-3.
    [codes: C-002, C-011, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-036, C-039]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors explicitly note that although boosting methods like CVQBoost can, in principle, aid explainability by inspecting highly weighted weak classifiers, they do not investigate explainability in this work, focusing instead on performance. They also acknowledge a hardware limitation of Dirac-3: a known operational performance limit at 23 dB dynamic range, and they attribute an observed slight energy gap in favor of other solvers at larger problem sizes to Hamiltonians whose dynamic ranges exceed this 23 dB limit.
    [codes: C-019, C-038, C-041]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The paper suggests extending CVQBoost and Dirac-3-based approaches beyond financial fraud detection to scientific application domains such as computational bioscience and chemistry.
    [codes: C-043]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The core claimed contribution is the introduction of CVQBoost, a novel boosting-style classifier that extends the QBoost quadratic formulation to a continuous-variable setting naturally implementable on Dirac optical EQC hardware. The method leverages EQC’s dissipative continuous quadratic solving and continuous weight encoding to avoid binary QUBO mapping, and the authors position this as enabling superior speed and power efficiency for large-scale classification tasks while retaining competitive accuracy.
    [codes: C-001, C-009, C-010, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-006, C-011]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Empirically, the study focuses on the public Kaggle credit card fraud dataset with 38 features and extreme class imbalance, employing several standard class-balancing techniques and reporting AUC as the primary metric. CVQBoost is instantiated with logistic regression weak classifiers, despite the authors having tested alternative weak learner types, and these weak learners are trained on one or two features each. The authors motivate boosting partly via interpretability but explicitly set explainability aside in their evaluation, concentrating instead on performance and scalability, and they extend their empirical analysis with synthetic datasets from 1M to 70M samples and up to 900 features to probe large-scale behavior.
    [codes: C-007, C-009, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-035]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper links the interpretability of boosting methods like CVQBoost to financial regulatory concerns, arguing that AI systems that provide decisions without explanations could create legal difficulties for financial institutions.
    [codes: C-008]

=== PAPER 85fbbb9f2a42 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames its work around the open question of whether quantum machine learning offers a clear advantage over classical machine learning, noting that such advantages have not yet been extensively or clearly demonstrated, especially on noisy, few-qubit NISQ hardware and with large, imbalanced datasets relevant for business contexts such as credit risk and fraud detection. It formulates the problem as developing QML workflows that circumvent qubit-number constraints while handling big, imbalanced financial datasets by focusing on input preprocessing and dimensionality reduction choices for binary classification tasks. The authors explicitly target realistic business-like settings where datasets may be imbalanced and have either small or large numbers of features.
    [codes: C-001, C-004, C-005, C-006, C-008, C-009, C-010]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The study employs two NISQ-oriented quantum machine learning methods: a quantum support vector classifier (QSVC) based on a quantum feature map, and a variational quantum algorithm (VQA) / variational quantum classifier. Classical data are first reduced in dimension and then encoded on only two qubits using either a Qiskit ZZFeatureMap for QSVC or angle encoding in PennyLane for the VQA, explicitly to demonstrate usability under the small-qubit constraint typical of business-relevant NISQ devices. Quantum encoding is a central design choice, and the experiments are run purely on noiseless simulators (Qiskit Aer and PennyLane) to allow fast iteration, with noise explicitly subtracted from simulations.
    [codes: C-004, C-005, C-017, C-018, C-015, C-016]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The core empirical finding is that incorporating Linear Discriminant Analysis (LDA) in the preprocessing stage improves the performance of quantum classifiers, particularly VQA, and in some cases enables them to outperform baseline classical classifiers on the evaluated metrics. On the UCI credit card default dataset, LDA emerges as the best dimensionality reduction method for both QSVC and VQA, with the VQA+LDA configuration achieving very high balanced accuracy, while some other quantum configurations such as QSVC with SKPP fail to separate classes at all. On the fraud detection dataset, LDA again provides the best preprocessing for both QSVC and VQA, and the quantum algorithms offer a small performance advantage over an LDA-based classical approach; moreover, four quantum configurations reportedly exceed the best baseline precision metric.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-007, C-022, C-023, C-021, C-025, C-027, C-028, C-029]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The authors benchmark their quantum models against several classical classifiers (including logistic regression, KNN, decision trees, Naive Bayes, and SVM) and report that, on the UCI credit card default dataset, these classical baselines struggle to separate the minority class, with the best reported precision and F1-scores remaining relatively low. In this regime, VQA with LDA is claimed to outperform the classical baselines in balanced accuracy and overall performance metrics. For the fraud detection dataset, classical baselines perform comparatively well, yet the quantum algorithms with LDA preprocessing are said to provide a small advantage over the classical LDA-based approach, and specifically, four quantum algorithms surpass the best classical precision value. The authors further propose a broadened notion of quantum advantage that need not require beating all classical models outright, but may instead be interpreted as extracting information differently or more effectively from the data.
    [codes: C-003, C-019, C-020, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-027, C-028, C-030]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper explicitly acknowledges several methodological limitations that temper its quantum-advantage claims. First, all experiments are run on simulators with noise subtracted, so hardware noise and realistic NISQ error characteristics are not evaluated. Second, the classical machine learning baselines are not specifically tuned to the datasets; default parameters are used throughout, and logistic regression fails to converge on the fraud dataset under these defaults, which may understate classical performance. Third, on the quantum side, the parameters of the quantum feature map are fixed (e.g., number of repetitions and feature dimension) and not tuned, and the exploration of different ansatz structures for VQA is left for future work, meaning the reported quantum results may not reflect optimally configured circuits. These caveats collectively limit the strength and generality of the reported quantum-classical performance comparisons.
    [codes: C-015, C-016, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The authors highlight multiple avenues for future research. They propose a deeper investigation into why LDA appears to outperform PCA in their supervised quantum classification setting, with the aim of understanding how LDA may provide a better data representation for qubit encoding. They also plan to study the role of PCA in unsupervised tasks, where its strengths may differ. On the quantum side, exploring alternative ansatz designs for the VQA is flagged as an interesting direction to assess the sensitivity of results to circuit structure. Finally, they call for extending their benchmarking approach to additional datasets with higher feature counts and to other domains, in order to build a more general and robust benchmark of QML preprocessing strategies.
    [codes: C-031, C-032, C-037, C-038]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper positions its main contribution as a "preprocessing perspective" on quantum machine learning, arguing that appropriate classical dimensionality reduction—specifically LDA—can unlock performance advantages for NISQ-era quantum classifiers under severe qubit constraints. It claims novelty in demonstrating, across two business-relevant financial datasets with class imbalance and differing feature dimensionality, that LDA-based preprocessing both improves classical encoding into two-qubit quantum circuits and can enable QSVC and VQA models to match or outperform classical baselines on certain metrics, thereby reframing quantum advantage as partly a question of how classical data are represented before quantum encoding.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-005, C-006, C-007, C-008, C-022, C-027, C-029, C-030]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study focuses on binary classification using two financial datasets chosen to emulate realistic business scenarios with imbalanced labels and varying feature dimensionality, including the UCI Default of Credit Card Clients (22% defaulters) and a high-dimensional fraud-detection dataset with 114 features and class imbalance. Standard dimensionality reduction techniques—PCA, LDA, SVD, and SKPP—are compared, with PCA noted as the predominant tool in prior QML work and LDA characterized as a supervised method exploiting class labels to maximize separability. Model evaluation uses 10-fold cross-validation and reports multiple metrics (precision, recall, F1, MCC, balanced accuracy). Experiments are conducted exclusively on idealized simulators with noise removed to accelerate iteration. Only two qubits are used throughout to illustrate a workflow compatible with current NISQ limitations in a business context, and quantum encoding relies on specific, fixed choices of Qiskit ZZFeatureMap and angle encoding in PennyLane rather than a broad hyperparameter search.
    [codes: C-008, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-012, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: None; all codes are incorporated into dimensions 1–8.
    [codes: —]
[SILO OVERLAY]
