=== PAPER 86891f250a78 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames its objective as surveying supervised and unsupervised quantum machine learning techniques that have actually been executed on quantum hardware, with a specific emphasis on real‑world applications in high‑energy physics, finance, and healthcare rather than toy problems. It focuses on how data (classical vs quantum) are defined and encoded into quantum feature spaces, highlighting efficient data loading and representation as central challenges for making these applications practical.
    [codes: C-001, C-004, C-005, C-010, C-013, C-019, C-020]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The survey centers on two main QML frameworks—quantum kernel methods and variational quantum algorithms—detailing their conceptual distinction and implementation via parameterized quantum circuits and kernel similarity measures. It systematically reviews encoding strategies (basis, amplitude, angle, data re‑uploading, Hamiltonian encodings), circuit structures (feature map vs model circuit separation in VQCs, Swap‑test classifier), and optimization and gradient techniques (SPSA, quantum gradient methods, parameter‑shift, quantum natural gradient and QN‑SPSA, shot‑frugal optimizers, local cost functions) as the key levers for making these methods workable on near‑term devices.
    [codes: C-003, C-007, C-008, C-019, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-057, C-061, C-070]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Across domains, the authors report that amplitude encoding can in principle offer exponential memory savings and that randomized measurement strategies can improve the scaling of quantum kernel estimation. They highlight observed robustness and faster convergence of certain optimizers (SPSA under noise and quantum natural gradient methods, including QN‑SPSA) and cite results such as exponential speedup claims for qPCA, high‑fidelity quantum GAN state replication, and tensor‑network approaches whose qubit requirements scale only logarithmically or are input‑size independent. Application‑wise, they note that high‑energy‑physics studies largely pursue gains in small‑data regimes, finance feature‑selection with QN‑SPSA converged on 20‑qubit hardware, quantum‑data‑driven models appear more promising than those on classical data, and some healthcare image‑classification experiments on real hardware achieve accuracies comparable to classical baselines, though vulnerabilities and hardware‑induced performance gaps are also observed.
    [codes: C-023, C-031, C-036, C-039, C-041, C-042, C-044, C-047, C-048, C-051, C-052, C-054, C-066]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The survey repeatedly benchmarks QML performance against classical ML, using classical models as baselines and noting that in many cases current quantum methods only match but do not exceed classical performance. In high‑energy physics and healthcare, the authors report comparable AUC‑ROC or accuracy to standard classical classifiers, with specific examples where more expressive encodings yield no improvement on real hardware relative to simpler encodings or classical baselines. They also emphasize that there may exist efficient classical algorithms that achieve performance comparable to quantum approaches, underscoring that convincing empirical evidence of a clear quantum advantage over classical learning remains limited so far.
    [codes: C-002, C-048, C-052, C-062, C-063]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors catalogue extensive limitations: hardware constraints such as limited qubit connectivity, noise, short coherence times, and state‑preparation and measurement errors, which in turn cause long running times and instability between simulator and hardware performance. They emphasize data‑related bottlenecks including the difficulty of efficiently encoding classical data (data‑loading problem), amplitude encoding’s potentially exponential gate cost, quadratic scaling and noise in quantum kernel estimation, and the lack of efficient, standardized data loaders or QRAM. On the algorithmic side, they highlight barren plateaus in variational training, the need for careful ansatz design, inefficiency of backpropagation for PQCs, and scaling issues for methods like qPCA on larger datasets. They also note that more complex encodings can amplify hardware noise without improving performance, that QGANs risk mode collapse, that quantum hardware’s noise and coherence constraints limit attainable circuit depth, and that full quantum error correction introduces overhead that can erode speedups. Further, they stress security and privacy gaps—including vulnerability of QML classifiers to adversarial examples, lack of built‑in privacy mechanisms, and insufficient exploration of differential privacy—as well as meta‑level limitations such as the lack of standardized benchmarks and quantum datasets, limited empirical evidence of quantum advantage, possible parity with unknown classical algorithms, and poor transparency around hyperparameter choices, all of which hinder interpretability, reproducibility, and progress.
    [codes: C-006, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-020, C-024, C-029, C-030, C-035, C-041, C-043, C-048, C-049, C-050, C-053, C-055, C-056, C-058, C-060, C-062, C-063, C-068, C-071, C-072, C-075]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: For future work, the authors call for developing and testing quantum gradient and hybrid optimization methods (e.g., quantum gradient techniques, QN‑SPSA), designing shot‑frugal optimizers and local cost functions to mitigate barren plateaus and measurement overheads, and advancing quantum autoencoder‑based error mitigation or correction. They argue that standardized benchmarks and easily preparable quantum datasets are needed to assess QML fairly, alongside systematic methods for choosing optimal encodings and ansätze that scale. They also highlight research gaps in privacy‑preserving QML (including differential privacy), in explainable quantum ML (XQML), and in distributed or ensemble‑style schemes such as quantum federated learning and quantum boosting to better utilize limited near‑term hardware. QRAM and improved quantum data preparation are identified as longer‑term directions for addressing data‑loading and storage challenges.
    [codes: C-037, C-057, C-059, C-061, C-064, C-065, C-067, C-069, C-070, C-073, C-074, C-076]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The authors position the paper as an application‑oriented survey that synthesizes the current state of supervised and unsupervised QML techniques actually run on near‑term quantum hardware, with an explicit focus on real‑world domains—high‑energy physics, finance, and healthcare—rather than quantum chemistry or purely theoretical proposals. Their contribution lies in organizing these hardware‑level studies by application domain, explicating core frameworks (quantum kernels vs variational algorithms), encodings, and optimization strategies, and drawing cross‑cutting conclusions about limitations, open questions on quantum advantage, and needed benchmarks and transparency practices.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-004, C-005, C-006, C-007, C-012, C-062, C-064, C-075]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the survey organizes prior work into structured tables by domain (high‑energy physics, finance, healthcare) and emphasizes AUC‑ROC as the main performance metric across applications, reinforcing comparability. It distinguishes clearly between quantum and classical data and between supervised and unsupervised learning setups, and it underscores that hyperparameters and ansatz choices critically affect scalability yet are often under‑reported, limiting transparency and reproducibility. The authors also note that many studies rely on small datasets or limited‑data regimes, and that gaps in standardized benchmarks and quantum datasets currently impede rigorous, fair evaluation of QML against classical baselines.
    [codes: C-012, C-013, C-015, C-016, C-046, C-047, C-064, C-065, C-070, C-075]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper includes foundational background on classical ML training paradigms, basic qubit state representation, and universal gate sets, plus conceptual motivation that quantum encodings can exploit exponentially large Hilbert spaces for stronger representational power than classical feature spaces. It also discusses the general role of backpropagation in deep learning as context for contrasting its reduced efficiency in parameterized quantum circuits.
    [codes: C-014, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-071]

=== PAPER 8d08019fb608 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper is framed as an extensive survey of how quantum machine learning (QML) can be applied to core quantitative finance problems, including portfolio optimization, market prediction and trading, option pricing, risk measurement (VaR/CVaR), credit risk, and fraud detection as anomaly detection. It motivates these applications by arguing that quantum properties such as superposition and entanglement could improve on traditional financial modelling and ML methods. The authors also carefully define central financial tasks and metrics (e.g., portfolio optimization, Sharpe ratio, VaR/CVaR, credit rating categories, and the treatment of fraud detection as a special case of anomaly detection) to ground the surveyed QML work in standard finance problem formulations.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-005, C-006, C-010, C-012, C-013, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: As a survey, the paper covers several families of quantum and quantum‑hybrid methods rather than implementing one specific algorithm. It explains basic gate‑model concepts (qubits, superposition, unitary gates) and positions QML within the NISQ era, emphasizing hybrid quantum–classical algorithms where parametrized quantum circuits (QNNs) are trained via classical optimizers. The authors describe key building blocks such as QAOA for combinatorial optimization, QAE for amplitude estimation, HHL for linear systems, and quantum PCA for eigenvalue problems, and then map these to finance use cases (e.g., QAOA and VQE for portfolio optimization, QAE and qGAN-based loading for derivative pricing, quantum kernels and QSVMs for classification and anomaly detection, and quantum GANs and kernel methods for fraud and credit risk). They also highlight architectures such as feature‑map plus variational layers in QNNs and multiple quantum kernel learning schemes used in risk and fraud contexts.
    [codes: C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-046, C-049, C-050, C-053, C-061, C-062, C-063, C-064, C-070, C-074, C-075, C-083]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The authors compile reported results across several financial subdomains. For portfolio optimization, cited work using quantum annealers and variational algorithms reports that optimized reverse annealing can be more than 100× faster than forward annealing, that gate‑model rebalancing on small portfolios is feasible on NISQ devices and can find portfolios within 5% of optimal adjusted returns, and that VQE can yield solutions close to exact ones under suitable hyperparameters. In market prediction and trading, they report studies where quantum deep hedging policies based on QNNs significantly outperform Black–Scholes hedges, quantum‑assisted feature selection improves predictive accuracy with lower dimensionality, quantum Elman networks achieve similar prediction quality with much smaller hidden/context layers, and hybrid deep QNNs demonstrate high forecasting accuracy across regression and extrapolation tasks, including for churn prediction and stock prediction. For pricing, the survey cites evidence that qPCA reduces noisy factors in interest‑rate models, qGAN‑based loading is gate‑efficient, QAE-based methods achieve quadratic or near-quadratic speedups for option pricing, shallow circuits can accurately price European and Asian options, unary encodings may be advantageous on near‑term devices, and several quantum approaches (including on IBM Q hardware) demonstrate quadratic or exponential speedups in steps or dimensionality for Monte Carlo and PDE‑based pricing. In risk management and fraud/credit analytics, the authors report that QAE-based methods offer near‑quadratic speedups for VaR and CVaR estimation, quantum feature selection on real credit risk data can match or surpass state‑of‑the‑art classical methods, quantum kernels can improve average precision as system size grows, QSVMs can deliver lower false positives and higher precision in fraud detection, QBoost offers interpretability benefits over classical boosting, quantum GNNs achieve AUC around 0.85 in graph‑based fraud detection, and quantum GAN and kernel setups show competitive or superior performance on anomaly detection benchmarks.
    [codes: C-046, C-047, C-048, C-049, C-050, C-051, C-052, C-053, C-054, C-055, C-056, C-057, C-058, C-059, C-061, C-062, C-063, C-064, C-065, C-066, C-067, C-068, C-069, C-070, C-071, C-072, C-076, C-079, C-081, C-084, C-085]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: Across the surveyed studies, quantum methods are usually benchmarked against classical baselines. For optimization, the authors describe QA and VQE approaches that come close to or match the performance of existing classical solvers on problems of comparable size, and note that VQE can approximate exact solutions under good hyperparameter choices. In trading and hedging, QNN-based deep hedging strategies reportedly significantly outperform the Black–Scholes hedge, and quantum‑assisted feature selection and QENN architectures match or exceed classical models with fewer parameters. In risk and fraud analysis, QAE‑based VaR/CVaR estimation is claimed to surpass classical Monte Carlo in efficiency, quantum feature selection for credit risk can compete with or surpass state‑of‑the‑art classical methods, quantum kernels can outperform standard approaches in average precision as problem size grows, and QSVMs are reported to have lower false positives and higher precision than both classical and other quantum baselines. Some comparisons are negative or neutral: QSVMs in certain stock prediction experiments do not show clear advantages over classical SVMs, and in a blockchain phishing study no performance improvement is observed when increasing the number of qubits, which tempers expectations of uniform quantum superiority.
    [codes: C-048, C-049, C-050, C-051, C-052, C-053, C-054, C-055, C-056, C-057, C-059, C-060, C-063, C-066, C-067, C-068, C-069, C-071, C-072, C-076, C-079, C-081, C-084, C-085]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper explicitly acknowledges that current quantum hardware and algorithms face substantial limitations that constrain practical impact. At a general level, the authors stress that existing NISQ devices and simulators are still far from demonstrating robust quantum advantage, being restricted by limited qubit counts, noise, and decoherence. They note that scalability, hardware limits, and algorithmic complexity remain overarching challenges. In specific applications, they report that current hardware issues hindered QAOA-based FX reserves management to the point that only a few demonstrations succeeded even for a five-variable problem, and that available QPUs were not sufficiently stable or error‑tolerant for fraud detection at realistic scales. They also highlight more nuanced limitations in QML experiments: QSVMs show no clear performance advantage over classical SVMs in some stock prediction tasks, increased qubit counts did not yield better performance in a blockchain phishing detection setting, and certain speedup claims are theoretical or emulator-based rather than demonstrated on large‑scale, noisy hardware. The discussion of Black–Scholes assumptions and the curse of dimensionality in classical models further contextualizes where quantum methods are intended to help, while implicitly acknowledging that both classical and quantum approaches can be limited by modelling assumptions and high dimensional data.
    [codes: C-014, C-020, C-036, C-037, C-060, C-077, C-078, C-080, C-086]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: For future work, the authors argue that overcoming hardware scalability, noise, and algorithmic complexity will be crucial to realize the potential of QML in finance, explicitly calling for continued research on more scalable algorithms and more capable devices. They also point to specific methodological avenues, such as further exploring unary encodings for option pricing which may offer practical advantages on near‑term hardware, and investigating how quantum noise and other QML properties might be harnessed to improve fairness in financial decision systems. The conclusion frames the field as a bidirectional exchange where financial problems can inspire new quantum methods, implicitly encouraging future research at this interdisciplinary interface.
    [codes: C-065, C-082, C-087, C-088]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper positions itself as an extensive, structured overview of quantum machine learning applications in quantitative finance, spanning portfolio optimization, trading, pricing, risk management, and fraud/anomaly detection. Its contribution lies in synthesizing technical background in finance and ML (e.g., definitions of portfolio optimization, VaR/CVaR, fraud as anomaly detection, feature selection/extraction, and ML evaluation metrics) with the building blocks of quantum and hybrid QML algorithms, then mapping these systematically onto financial use cases. The authors also emphasize the emerging, NISQ‑era perspective on QML and highlight a two‑way relationship where quantitative finance not only benefits from but also inspires the development of quantum methods, which they present as a distinctive framing of the field’s current state.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-019, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-031, C-038, C-043, C-088]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the survey dedicates space to explaining standard ML workflows and concepts (training pipeline, curse of dimensionality, feature selection and extraction, supervised/unsupervised/RL paradigms, neural network structures, and evaluation metrics like ROC/AUC), providing readers with the conceptual tools to assess QML studies. In the quantum risk and credit‑risk sections, the authors note papers that explicitly examine scalability and expected runtime, describe precise classifier and metric choices (e.g., logistic regression with log‑loss, average precision, false positives, interpretability), and report experiments on real‑world datasets, including credit risk and fraud detection data. They also mention works that test quantum kernels both in simulation and on real hardware, and a QML fairness verification framework that explores the impact of quantum noise, which signals attention to robustness and ethical aspects beyond raw accuracy. However, because this is a survey, it generally relays other authors’ methodological setups rather than enforcing a unified experimental protocol, and many reported results are drawn from small‑scale or idealized simulations, as suggested by repeated emphasis on NISQ limitations and hardware instability.
    [codes: C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-073, C-075, C-082, C-083]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: Several codes provide core technical or definitional background but are not central to the survey’s comparative or evaluative claims. These include detailed formulations of mean–variance portfolio optimization and constrained/binary variants, the identification of algorithmic trading as quantitative, ML‑driven automation, and specific formal results about quantum subroutines (e.g., QAE, HHL, quantum PCA) that underlie many of the reviewed applications. They contribute to the paper’s pedagogical role rather than to specific findings or limitations.
    [codes: C-007, C-008, C-009, C-011, C-034, C-035, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-044, C-045]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to fraud_detection because it surveys quantum machine learning methods for anomaly detection, including explicit fraud-detection studies and closely related credit-risk/graph-based classification tasks. It also reports comparative results, practical limitations, and hardware constraints that directly affect whether quantum approaches can be used for real fraud screening or AML workflows.

  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The article frames quantum computing in financial services as both an opportunity for improved analytics (especially risk analysis, fraud detection, portfolio/risk optimisation, CRM personalisation, HFT, and Monte Carlo-based derivative pricing) and a systemic cyber‑security threat to existing cryptographic infrastructures and the wider digital economy. It highlights risk analysis as the most promising near‑term application, while simultaneously stressing that current public‑key cryptography, crypto‑asset wallets, and blockchains are vulnerable to future quantum attacks that could undermine trust in financial institutions and digital services.
    [codes: C-003, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-027, C-028, C-035]

It also discusses defensive cryptographic methods, including post‑quantum or ‘quantum‑safe’ public‑key schemes based on mathematically hard problems and the adaptation of existing standards like MIKEY‑SAKKE, as well as existing use of quantum cryptography to protect links to off‑site backups. For risk evaluation, it notes the development of algorithms that use quantum capabilities to analyse IT infrastructure states and support near real‑time what‑if analysis, although detailed algorithmic specifications are not provided.
    [codes: C-007, C-008, C-014, C-025, C-026, C-035, C-036]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The article reports several claimed benefits: industry forecasts of rapid quantum market growth overall and especially in financial services; practical deployment of IBM’s Q System One (20‑qubit) as a commercial platform; and exploratory projects by major banks via IBM’s Q Network. It cites external work suggesting that quantum systems could dramatically speed Monte Carlo algorithms for derivative pricing and could enable near real‑time detection of portfolio risk, enhanced fraud assessment, improved CRM personalisation, and further acceleration and AI‑enhancement of high‑frequency trading. On the security side, it notes that some banks already use quantum cryptography to secure backup links and that quantum‑enabled risk‑analysis algorithms aim to provide near real‑time what‑if scenario analysis for infrastructure risk.
    [codes: C-004, C-005, C-006, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-035]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: Comparisons with classical methods are primarily qualitative or presented via cited external work: the article relays claims that quantum systems could dramatically speed Monte Carlo derivative pricing relative to classical simulations, and that quantum computing will further accelerate existing ultra‑fast, algorithmic HFT infrastructure, potentially in combination with AI and predictive analytics. It also notes that quantum‑enabled risk‑analysis tools are expected to deliver near real‑time scenario analysis beyond current capabilities, but the article does not provide detailed benchmarks or head‑to‑head experimental results.
    [codes: C-015, C-020, C-022]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: Several limitations and caveats are emphasised. The adoption timeline for commercially mainstream quantum computing is highly uncertain, with estimates ranging from a few years to at least a decade, and some experts judging timelines like 2025 for large‑scale, general‑purpose systems as ambitious. Present‑day controlled qubit counts are in the tens rather than the thousands or millions required to make cyber‑threat scenarios practical, leading some practitioners to warn that quantum cyber‑risk should not be over‑estimated for now. Technical barriers such as bandwidth, error correction, and environmental requirements are singled out as current obstacles. The article also notes that existing encryption standards and crypto‑asset wallets remain vulnerable to future quantum attacks, underscoring that current systems are not yet quantum‑safe.
    [codes: C-001, C-018, C-027, C-029, C-030, C-032]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The article points toward multiple future directions: developing quantum‑resistant blockchains that account for asymmetric access to large‑scale quantum resources; evolving public‑key standards like MIKEY‑SAKKE to be quantum‑safe; and expanding quantum‑computing‑as‑a‑service delivery models while addressing how such services might be restricted from criminal misuse. It also implies continued work on quantum‑safe cryptographic schemes based on hard mathematical problems and broader integration of quantum considerations into financial institutions’ digital transformation agendas.
    [codes: C-017, C-025, C-026, C-028, C-031, C-036]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: As an industry‑oriented overview, the article’s contribution lies in synthesising emerging views on how quantum computing may impact financial services, highlighting risk analysis as the most promising current application while juxtaposing prospective business gains with systemic cyber‑security threats. It brings together forecasts of market growth, early hardware deployments, exploratory banking projects, and ongoing standardisation and post‑quantum cryptography efforts to characterise financial services both as a likely first mover in adopting quantum technologies and as a sector acutely exposed to their security implications.
    [codes: C-002, C-004, C-005, C-006, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-016, C-021, C-023, C-024, C-033, C-034, C-035]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: The piece reports that many bank‑led quantum initiatives are explicitly described as exploratory, involving proofs of concept, quantum risk assessments, and early deployment of quantum‑resistant algorithms in selected systems rather than large‑scale production roll‑outs. It notes that cloud‑based access via networks like IBM Q supports experimentation with simulators and hardware, and that standards bodies such as ASC X9 have created study groups to systematically review quantum risk for financial services. However, the article does not provide detailed experimental protocols, datasets, or reproducibility artefacts; most claims are high‑level and often based on external reports and vendor or expert statements.
    [codes: C-008, C-010, C-033, C-034]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The article underlines that the greatest impact on financial services may come from adversaries obtaining quantum capabilities, rather than from banks’ own deployments, especially given the sector’s ongoing digital transformation that increases its attack surface. It warns that quantum computers pose a systemic threat to public‑key cryptography underpinning most technology platforms and that successful attacks could erode confidence and trust in the financial system and wider digital economy.
    [codes: C-003, C-016, C-017, C-023, C-024]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper matters for fraud_detection because it highlights quantum computing as a potential accelerator for detecting fraudulent payments and other anomalous activity, while also warning that quantum capabilities could weaken the cryptographic protections that fraud systems rely on. === PAPER 8fec8d839e47 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets financial cybersecurity and risk management, arguing that traditional models like VaR and existing classical AI struggle with high-speed, complex financial data and centralized vulnerabilities, and thus proposes a new approach to detect fraud and evolving threat patterns in real time. It formulates this as a financial fraud/risk classification and threat detection problem embedded in a secure, decentralized infrastructure that must also satisfy regulatory explainability requirements.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-004, C-007, C-008, C-009, C-012]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The core quantum method is the Quantum-Enhanced Risk Classifier (QERC), a hybrid QML algorithm combining QSVMs and variational quantum circuits with amplitude encoding of financial data into a high-dimensional Hilbert space to capture complex non-linear threat patterns. QERC is implemented on IBM Qiskit Aer simulators using COBYLA optimization, with a design that uses O(log2 d) qubits (e.g., 5 qubits for 23-dimensional FICO features) and relies on Pauli-expectation measurements for classification, embedded within a broader architecture that includes a blockchain-based Decentralized Trust Network and an Explainable Quantum AI module that adapts SHAP to quantum outputs.
    [codes: C-002, C-010, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-018, C-020, C-022, C-017]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: In simulation, the authors report that Q-DTN achieves around 97% fraud detection accuracy and 0.95 F1-score with roughly 10 seconds of training time, with convergence reduced from 2,500 to 50 iterations and sub-linear O(N^0.8) training-time scaling from 1,000 up to 1,000,000 samples. They also claim zero data exposure due to off-chain storage and zk-SNARK-protected on-chain hashes, and report application-specific results such as 96% accuracy for credit scoring and a 98% detection rate for DeFi fraud, alongside a qubit-scaling advantage of O(log2 d), exemplified by using 5 qubits for 23-dimensional FICO data.
    [codes: C-005, C-006, C-019, C-027, C-028, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper claims that Q-DTN improves fraud detection accuracy by about 5% over classical baselines such as XGBoost and neural networks, with approximately 98% less computational overhead and a 50× speedup in training time (e.g., 10 seconds versus several hundred seconds for classical models). In the credit scoring case study, the authors state a 4% accuracy improvement over XGBoost, and they also indicate that QERC converges in roughly 50 iterations compared with around 2,500 iterations for classical methods.
    [codes: C-006, C-019, C-028, C-032, C-034]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge several deployment and scalability constraints: current NISQ hardware would degrade the idealized performance by roughly 15–25%, feature encoding is practically limited to about 50 dimensions, and zk-SNARK-based privacy verification introduces latency that may be problematic in high-frequency financial scenarios.
    [codes: C-036, C-037, C-038]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: For future work, the paper points to leveraging fault-tolerant quantum computers to overcome present NISQ-related performance and dimensionality constraints, and proposes extending the framework with quantum neural networks and federated learning, as well as deploying it on fault-tolerant hardware once available.
    [codes: C-039, C-040]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The authors position Q-DTN as a novel, holistic framework that synergistically integrates a new hybrid QML classifier (QERC), a blockchain-based Decentralized Trust Network, and an Explainable Quantum AI module plus real-time dashboard to address gaps they identify in existing quantum–blockchain hybrids—namely lack of real-time adaptability to financial risk patterns, absence of explainable mechanisms for compliance, and insufficient integrated cybersecurity focus.
    [codes: C-001, C-009, C-012, C-013, C-016, C-017, C-024]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the work uses three heterogeneous datasets (FICO HELOC, Ethereum DeFi transactions, and IoT climate finance records) with normalization to [0,1] and differential privacy (ε = 0.1) for anonymization, and evaluates QERC under a stated 5-fold cross-validation protocol on IBM Qiskit Aer simulators with specified hardware resources. The blockchain component is tested on a 10-node Hyperledger Fabric network with zk-SNARKs, and classical baselines (XGBoost, neural networks, standalone QSVM) are reported as run on the same hardware with cross-validation, supporting at least some degree of comparability and reproducibility, further aided by a described real-time risk dashboard for system-level monitoring.
    [codes: C-013, C-020, C-021, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-029, C-030, C-031]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper also cites prior studies reporting that quantum SVMs can outperform classical models on complex financial fraud tasks, and briefly discusses broader fraud detection performance as background context rather than as new empirical results.
    [codes: C-011]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is highly relevant to fraud_detection because it proposes a real-time quantum classifier for detecting fraudulent and non-linear threat patterns, and it reports direct fraud-specific results on DeFi fraud and credit-scoring-related fraud risk. Its added value for this silo is the combination of detection performance, explainability, and privacy-preserving sharing, all of which are central to operational fraud monitoring.

    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-012, C-015  dim_source: dimension_1]

  KEY_POINT 3: The strongest fraud-specific empirical result is the reported 98% detection rate for DeFi fraud, with XQAI highlighting transaction amount as a key predictor.

  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets financial fraud detection and associated risk assessment, arguing that existing methods suffer from high false positives, slow processing, and limited adaptability to complex and evolving fraud patterns, and that more strong, scalable, and economical systems are required. Experiments are conducted in a hybrid computing environment using a quantum simulator that integrates quantum and classical resources.

=== PAPER 924cd91fefd6 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper addresses credit card fraud detection as a major and growing concern for the financial industry, emphasizing rising global losses and regional fraud cases and the associated economic and reputational harms. It frames the task as highly imbalanced binary classification of credit card transactions, where only a tiny fraction of records are fraudulent, requiring scalable and effective detection methods. This motivates exploring quantum machine learning for credit card fraud detection to better handle large-scale, imbalanced transactional data.
    [codes: C-001, C-005, C-006, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-027]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors propose a hybrid quantum–classical fraud detection architecture that encodes normalized transaction features into quantum states, processed by a variational quantum circuit (VQC), whose measurement outputs are then classified by a classical neural network. They discuss amplitude encoding, which is qubit-efficient in log2(N) features, and angle encoding, which scales linearly in qubits, and describe a VQC that leverages superposition and entanglement, trained via a classical optimizer in a hybrid loop. The overall architecture integrates quantum and classical components, with the VQC as the core quantum element and a threshold-based decision rule producing binary fraud labels.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-014, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-034, C-036, C-037]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: On the imbalanced credit card fraud dataset, the proposed QML hybrid achieved a recall of 0.89 and precision of 0.82, indicating it captured most fraudulent transactions while keeping false positives relatively low. The authors report an F1-score of 0.85 and an AUC-ROC of 0.91, which they interpret as balanced performance and strong discriminative power. They cautiously suggest that the VQC’s ability to explore high-dimensional feature spaces through superposition and entanglement likely contributed to these favorable metrics, and conclude that the framework demonstrates promising results and effectively handles the imbalanced dataset.
    [codes: C-004, C-031, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-047, C-048]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper does not report a direct empirical comparison between its hybrid QML model and specific classical baselines on the same dataset. Instead, it motivates QML by claiming that quantum methods can potentially outperform classical algorithms in feature extraction and pattern recognition, and by citing related work where QSVM, QNN, and QLR achieved high accuracies in cybersecurity settings, as well as quantum-enhanced approaches like Quantum-SMOTE and QAE-FD that improved classical performance metrics. It also notes prior studies where VQC-based models improved recall and hybrid ensembles enhanced fraud detection under current hardware constraints.
    [codes: C-006, C-007, C-012, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors explicitly state that their experiments are conducted on a quantum simulator due to current quantum hardware limitations, indicating that real-device behavior and performance are not yet validated. They further acknowledge challenges including noise in quantum simulations, limitations on scalability, and the computational cost of training hybrid quantum–classical models. In related work, they also note that the current limitations of quantum hardware necessitate hybrid approaches rather than fully quantum solutions.
    [codes: C-021, C-035, C-046]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work is oriented toward deploying the proposed QML framework on real quantum hardware as devices mature, to validate and assess performance beyond simulation. The authors argue that as hardware advances, the approach could offer significant speedups, and they highlight the need for research on noise mitigation techniques. They also propose integrating explainable AI methods to improve interpretability of the fraud detection decisions produced by the hybrid model.
    [codes: C-049, C-050, C-051, C-052]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims novelty in proposing and implementing a hybrid quantum–classical architecture for credit card fraud detection that combines a variational quantum circuit with a classical neural network, specifically tailored to handle highly imbalanced transactional data. It positions the integration of quantum encoding of transaction features, VQC-based feature processing leveraging superposition and entanglement, and imbalance-aware classical classification as a promising QML framework that demonstrates effective performance on a standard credit card fraud dataset. The authors also emphasize that this hybrid approach addresses scalability and computational concerns associated with large-scale fraud detection while remaining compatible with current quantum hardware constraints.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-006, C-014, C-015, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-033, C-037, C-039, C-040, C-047, C-048]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses the well-known European credit card fraud dataset with 284,807 transactions and 492 fraudulent cases (0.172%), represented by PCA-transformed features and highlighting severe class imbalance. Transactional features are normalized and encoded into quantum states via amplitude or angle encoding, and the classical neural network incorporates techniques such as weighted loss functions and oversampling to address the rarity of fraudulent transactions. Experiments are implemented in Python using Qiskit on a quantum simulator, with a 70:30 train–test split and standard classification metrics—accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score, and AUC-ROC—carefully defined and used for evaluation. However, only simulator-based results are reported, and there is no mention of code release, multiple random seeds, or repeated runs, which constrains reproducibility and robustness assessment.
    [codes: C-023, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-033, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper provides additional contextual and background framing: it discusses foundational statistical methods for fraud detection as effective but limited, and notes that AI-based approaches automate pattern recognition and enhance detection capabilities. It also explains that quantum computing methods exploit superposition and entanglement and may excel at addressing imbalanced datasets and uncovering subtle correlations, positioning the proposed QML approach within a broader taxonomy of statistical, AI, and quantum methods for credit card fraud detection.
    [codes: C-010, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-018]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is directly relevant to fraud_detection because it targets credit card fraud detection with a hybrid quantum-classical model designed for highly imbalanced transaction data. Its main value for the silo is the combination of imbalance-aware modeling, fraud-focused evaluation metrics, and a reported high-recall result that is especially important for catching fraudulent transactions while limiting false positives.

=== PAPER 9352e26d9232 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper tackles detection of fraudulent housing/property insurance claims as a supervised binary classification task, motivated by the complexity of fraud patterns that challenge traditional detection methods. It frames this problem for quantum computation by applying quantum support vector machines (QSVM) with quantum feature maps and kernels to classify claims based on selected fraud-related features.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-019, C-031, C-037, C-038, C-039]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors use a QSVM approach, described as an extension of classical SVM, grounded in quantum superposition and entanglement phenomena. They implement QSVM on IBM Quantum Experience cloud hardware, with a pipeline that includes state preparation/encoding, quantum circuit initialization and execution, quantum kernel estimation via transition amplitudes, and use of the resulting quantum kernel within a classical SVM. Several quantum feature maps are discussed, notably the Pauli, Z, and ZZ feature maps, with the ZZFeatureMap highlighted as a short-depth, classically hard-to-replicate circuit suitable for near-term devices.
    [codes: C-005, C-006, C-007, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-022, C-024, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The study reports comparative fraud-detection accuracies for QSVM using a ZZFeatureMap under different entanglement settings, with accuracies around 91–93%. The authors also assert, based on their experimentation, that QSVM could potentially provide computation speedup, although this is presented as a tentative observation rather than a demonstrated result.
    [codes: C-034, C-040, C-042, C-043]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: QSVM performance is explicitly compared to classical SVM on the same insurance fraud dataset. Classical SVM with several kernels (linear, RBF, polynomial, sigmoid) achieves high accuracies, with reported values around 98–99%, while QSVM with ZZFeatureMap attains lower accuracies of about 91–93%. The authors nonetheless suggest that QSVM may offer computational speed benefits even though its predictive accuracy is currently inferior.
    [codes: C-004, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper acknowledges several limitations. Conceptually, the authors state that quantum computing does not provide speedup for all types of problems, and that quantum techniques have not yet been mathematically proven to provide a quantum advantage in this application. Practically, they highlight that state preparation is nontrivial and that the choice of encoding directly affects algorithmic complexity, and they note that dimensionality reduction via PCA is crucial because the number of available qubits may be limited on current hardware.
    [codes: C-011, C-013, C-014, C-027, C-044]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The authors suggest that future advances in quantum hardware and the discovery of new quantum approaches could make quantum machine learning, including QSVM, a strong alternative to classical machine learning techniques for tasks such as insurance fraud detection.
    [codes: C-045]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper positions itself as implementing and exploring QSVM-based quantum machine learning techniques for detecting complex patterns in housing insurance claim fraud, including detailed consideration of quantum state preparation, different encoding and feature-map choices, and a full implementation pipeline on real IBM cloud-based quantum hardware, benchmarked directly against classical SVM. It also emphasizes the use of specific, domain-informed fraud features within a QML framework.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-007, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-022, C-024, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the paper specifies a multi-step QSVM implementation pipeline: data scaling and normalization, PCA-based dimensionality reduction motivated by limited qubit counts, kernel generation and estimation, and execution of the quantum circuit on cloud-accessible IBM devices. It provides some transparency about the fraud-related feature set (e.g., credit and claims history, time gaps around loss and policy dates, and contact with authorities), and explains how quantum kernels are integrated into classical SVM classifiers. However, the available codes do not indicate detailed reporting of dataset size, splits, or robustness analyses.
    [codes: C-007, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-021, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper includes general expository material on machine learning and quantum computing: it categorizes types of machine learning; explains qubits, superposition, and the exponential state space of n-qubit systems; and notes that quantum computers are particularly effective for certain optimization and simulation problems. It also describes how SVM uses high-dimensional mappings and the kernel trick to reduce computational complexity, and mentions the potential use of Grover’s algorithm to yield quadratic speedup within SVMs.
    [codes: C-008, C-009, C-010, C-012, C-020, C-023]

=== PAPER 946a6e1b2725 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames a broad computational problem: classical machine learning is increasingly constrained by scalability and algorithmic efficiency limits, motivating exploration of quantum artificial intelligence (QAI) to overcome these bottlenecks and enhance learning performance in complex, high-dimensional problem spaces, including but not limited to finance. It positions quantum computing as a potential paradigm shift that leverages superposition, entanglement, and large quantum state spaces to address these issues.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-015, C-016, C-035]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: As a conceptual review, the paper surveys multiple quantum approaches for AI rather than implementing a specific method. It describes quantum support vector machines based on quantum-enhanced kernel estimation and quantum kernel methods for computing inner products in Hilbert space, quantum neural networks that integrate quantum circuits with classical training, and variational quantum algorithms (VQAs) as a key near-term framework using a hybrid quantum–classical optimization loop. It also covers quantum PCA for dimensionality reduction and emphasizes hybrid quantum–classical architectures as the preferred design in the NISQ era.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-019, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The paper does not present empirical or quantitative results; instead, it conceptually argues that QAI approaches could accelerate optimization, improve generalization in high-dimensional spaces, and provide potential speedups in tasks such as dimensionality reduction and classification. Reported performance advantages are framed as prospective or theoretical rather than as experimentally validated findings of this study.
    [codes: C-011, C-016, C-026, C-028, C-030, C-036, C-047]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: Comparisons to classical methods are high-level and conceptual: the authors contrast classical ML’s scalability and efficiency limitations with quantum computing’s exponential state space and non-classical correlations, suggesting that QAI may overcome classical bottlenecks and provide better classification boundaries or speedups in tasks like PCA. Hybrid quantum–classical approaches are presented as a pragmatic way to combine strengths of both paradigms, but no empirical benchmarking against specific classical baselines is reported.
    [codes: C-002, C-008, C-011, C-015, C-016, C-025, C-026, C-030, C-031, C-043, C-048]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper acknowledges extensive limitations across hardware, algorithms, and evaluation. On hardware, current NISQ devices suffer from limited qubit counts, noise, decoherence, short coherence times, and high gate error rates, making deep circuits impractical. On the algorithmic side, QAI faces issues of algorithmic stability, notably barren plateaus in variational quantum algorithms that hinder training, as well as challenges around data encoding/quantum feature mapping and readout. The authors stress that classical ML scalability constraints motivate QAI but also highlight that QAI itself is nascent, lacks standardized benchmarks for quantum advantage, generalizability, and robustness to noise, and must contend with broader ethical concerns around bias, fairness, security, and accountability. More generally, they describe implementation barriers such as circuit depth, hardware limitations, and data encoding as key obstacles to practical QML deployment.
    [codes: C-006, C-008, C-015, C-023, C-032, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-043, C-044]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work is framed around advancing QAI’s theoretical foundations, hardware, and hybrid system design. The authors call for development of noise-resilient quantum circuits, exploration of novel architectures such as topological qubits, and problem-specific quantum kernels and ansätze, alongside a deeper theory of quantum generalization and learning bounds. They emphasize the need for scalable, fault-tolerant hardware, efficient hybrid quantum–classical algorithms and interfaces, and standardized benchmarks for evaluating quantum learning models. They also highlight research directions in applications—such as accelerating scientific discovery and sector-specific modeling in domains including finance—that could showcase QAI’s transformative potential.
    [codes: C-007, C-028, C-031, C-038, C-042, C-045, C-048]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper positions itself as a comprehensive review of foundational concepts in classical ML and quantum computing, combined with an in-depth discussion of emerging QAI algorithms and their applications across sectors like healthcare, finance, cybersecurity, and logistics. It claims to synthesize key challenges and research priorities, framing QAI as a transformative yet nascent paradigm at the intersection of quantum computing and machine learning with the potential to solve previously intractable problems.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-004, C-005, C-006, C-007, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-045, C-046, C-047]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, this is a conceptual review article rather than an empirical study, so it does not report datasets, experiments, or reproducible implementations. The authors systematically outline classical ML paradigms and workflows as a baseline, then detail foundational quantum computation concepts and gate-based models, followed by descriptions of QML architectures and hybrid variational optimization loops. They explicitly note the absence of standardized QML benchmarks for assessing quantum advantage, generalizability, and robustness, which itself is a methodological limitation for the field’s empirical rigor.
    [codes: C-003, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-024, C-029, C-031, C-043]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: Several codes describe general background and contextual claims about quantum computation and QAI that support the narrative but do not map directly to the other dimensions. These include explanations of core quantum principles (superposition, entanglement, interference, probabilistic measurement), generic sectoral opportunity statements for logistics, climate, smart grids, and scientific computing, and high-level affirmations of QAI’s transformative potential beyond specific methods or limitations.
    [codes: C-017, C-018, C-020, C-021, C-023, C-033, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-046]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to fraud_detection because it frames quantum AI as a way to improve anomaly detection and classification in high-dimensional, data-heavy settings, while explicitly naming cybersecurity applications and real-time threat detection. However, it is a conceptual review rather than an empirical fraud model, so its value is mainly in outlining candidate quantum methods and the practical barriers to deployment.
    [silo_relevance_score: medium]

=== PAPER 95a7b64bceac (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames financial decision support as increasingly challenging for classical methods due to high-dimensional, large-scale market and risk data, and the computational burden of techniques like VaR and Monte Carlo that impede real-time risk assessment. It argues that classical models such as Black–Scholes and Markowitz rely on simplifying assumptions (efficient markets, normal returns) that often fail in practice, motivating the use of quantum computing’s parallelism and superposition to accelerate and improve market forecasting, portfolio optimization, risk analysis, and fraud detection.
    [codes: C-001, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-014]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors propose a Quantum Powered Financial Intelligence (QPFI) system that integrates Quantum Machine Learning (QML), Quantum Optimization, and Quantum Monte Carlo simulations for financial decision support. QML components include Quantum Neural Networks, Quantum Variational Classifiers, Quantum Support Vector Machines, and QGANs for tasks such as market forecasting, credit scoring, and fraud/anomaly detection; optimization is handled with QAOA and VQE formulated on QUBO-style portfolio problems; and Quantum Monte Carlo is used for accelerating derivative pricing, stress testing, and VaR. The system is implemented as a hybrid workflow with classical preprocessing and feature extraction feeding quantum models, executed on NISQ devices via cloud-based platforms such as IBM Q, Google Sycamore, and D-Wave Leap.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-004, C-005, C-006, C-012, C-018, C-020, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Within the QPFI framework, the authors report that QNNs and QSVMs achieve an average market prediction accuracy of 93.5% and are described as predicting market movements with superior accuracy compared to classical baselines. For portfolio optimization, they claim reduced portfolio variance, higher expected returns than Markowitz and Black–Litterman approaches, and a 22.3% improvement in the Sharpe ratio. Quantum Monte Carlo simulations are reported to yield a 35.7% reduction in computational time for derivative pricing, stress testing, and VaR calculation, while maintaining high VaR accuracy. For fraud detection, QGAN-based models are said to reduce false alarm rates by 41.2% relative to traditional ML and to reach 97.2% precision in identifying fraudulent transactions.
    [codes: C-023, C-024, C-026, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-013, C-019]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper consistently positions its quantum approaches as outperforming classical methods: QPFI’s QNN and QSVM models are said to exceed the accuracy of LSTM and ARIMA time-series models for market prediction; quantum portfolio optimization is reported to deliver lower variance, higher expected returns, and better Sharpe ratios than Markowitz mean–variance and Black–Litterman models; and Quantum Monte Carlo is claimed to compute VaR and related risk measures significantly faster than traditional Monte Carlo. For fraud and credit tasks, the authors state that QML methods such as QSVMs and QGANs achieve better classification accuracy, improved credit risk estimation, and reduced false positives compared to traditional machine learning techniques, though concrete classical baselines for these tasks are only partially detailed.
    [codes: C-019, C-024, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-037, C-038]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors explicitly acknowledge that the practical deployment of their quantum approaches is constrained by current hardware limitations, specifically the need to enhance quantum hardware stability and, by implication, cope with NISQ-era noise and scale limitations. They also highlight that regulatory considerations and the lack of practical, real-world quantum trials in banking and trading institutions remain open issues, indicating that their reported benefits have yet to be validated under production-grade financial and regulatory conditions.
    [codes: C-008, C-032, C-040]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work directions identified by the authors include improving quantum hardware stability, addressing regulatory aspects surrounding quantum technologies in finance, and conducting practical quantum trials within banks and trading firms to validate QPFI components in real-world settings. They also broadly imply continued advancement of hybrid quantum–classical architectures and experimentation as quantum hardware matures.
    [codes: C-008, C-040, C-007, C-032]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims novelty in introducing an integrated Quantum Powered Financial Intelligence (QPFI) framework that unifies QML, QAOA/VQE-based optimization, and Quantum Monte Carlo for market prediction, portfolio management, real-time risk estimation, and fraud detection within a single decision-support system. Its stated contributions are the construction of a quantum-empowered market prediction model, risk-aware portfolio optimization via QAOA, accelerated Monte Carlo simulations for real-time risk measurement, and the application of quantum methods—particularly QGANs—for fraud detection, all positioned as advances over traditional financial modeling approaches.
    [codes: C-002, C-014, C-022, C-023, C-025, C-026, C-027]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the work is structured as a hybrid system in which financial data such as market trends, stock prices, and transaction records are first preprocessed and subjected to feature extraction using classical computing before being passed to quantum models for forecasting, risk analysis, and fraud detection on NISQ devices. The authors emphasize cloud-based quantum resources (IBM Q, Google Sycamore, D-Wave Leap) for scalability, but the codes indicate limited reporting of dataset specifications, encoding details, or hardware parameters, and there is no mention of public code release, suggesting that experimental reproducibility and transparency around datasets and implementation details are limited in the current description.
    [codes: C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper notes growing industry interest in applying quantum algorithms to derivatives pricing and risk analysis, reflecting a broader trend toward integrating quantum computing into financial services. It also reiterates the foundational role of classical models such as Black–Scholes and Markowitz as baselines that motivate moving toward quantum-enhanced methods.
    [codes: C-015, C-021]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to fraud_detection because it explicitly proposes quantum methods for detecting fraudulent transactions and concealed anomalies, with reported gains in precision and false-alarm reduction.     [silo_relevance_score: high]

=== PAPER 95ecf8c43fd1 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper tackles financial fraud detection, motivated by the large financial and reputational risks and high false-positive rates faced by institutions, and formulates it as a binary classification task over financial transactions to distinguish suspicious from non-suspicious activity. It positions this as a testbed to comparatively assess classical and quantum-hybrid machine learning models for fraud detection in an AML context.
    [codes: C-001, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-012, C-015]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The study evaluates three quantum-hybrid approaches: a Quantum Support Vector Machine (QSVM) that uses a quantum circuit to compute a kernel matrix, a Variational Quantum Classifier (VQC) built from a quantum feature map plus a trainable parametrised quantum circuit optimized classically, and a Hybrid Quantum Neural Network (HQNN) that embeds a variational quantum circuit as a hidden layer within a classical deep neural network. All quantum components are implemented via PennyLane in a way that is compatible with execution on real quantum hardware, even though only simulators are used in the experiments.
    [codes: C-004, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-036]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The main empirical finding is that classical tree-based models, especially Random Forest, achieve the strongest performance, reaching about 97.3% accuracy and an F-measure around 86.9%, and are reported as the best overall method on the fraud detection task. Among the quantum models, QSVM is the most promising, delivering relatively high precision (~77%) and a low false-positive rate (~1.36%), but with reduced recall and substantial computational overhead, while the other quantum-hybrid models perform markedly worse. Overall, the authors conclude that classical ensemble methods remain the preferred option for reliable fraud detection in this setting.
    [codes: C-006, C-007, C-037, C-041, C-045]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper performs a direct comparison between four classical baselines—Logistic Regression, Decision Tree, Random Forest, and XGBoost—and the three quantum-hybrid models, finding that classical tree ensembles, especially Random Forest, significantly outperform all quantum counterparts on accuracy, F-measure, and recall, while maintaining competitive false-positive rates. The discussion attributes this advantage partly to the richness of the engineered behavioural features, which classical ensemble models can exploit effectively, and it emphasizes a deployment philosophy in which robust classical models serve as the backbone, with quantum components treated as optional enhancements.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-004, C-022, C-006, C-037, C-038, C-029, C-045]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge several limitations: the experiments use only simulated quantum backends and do not run on real quantum hardware, although the implementation is hardware-compatible. They explicitly state that current quantum models underperform, citing algorithmic immaturity, difficulties in training variational circuits, and substantial computational overhead. They further note that the synthetic IBM AML dataset, even with complex behavioural features, may lack the specific structure needed to reveal any quantum advantage. More broadly, they conclude that quantum AI is not yet mature enough to outperform traditional algorithms in this practical, high-stakes fraud detection domain.
    [codes: C-035, C-039, C-040, C-046]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work directions include designing more tailored variational ansätze and adaptive feature maps to improve quantum model expressiveness without incurring training instabilities, exploring hybrid temporal architectures that combine temporal feature extraction (e.g., LSTMs) with variational quantum circuits, and using quantum autoencoders or metaheuristic feature selection to compress or select informative features. The authors also position the FD4QC architecture as a roadmap for gradually integrating more capable quantum components into financial security systems as the technology matures.
    [codes: C-042, C-043, C-044, C-047]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims two main contributions: a comprehensive behavioural feature engineering framework that transforms raw transaction logs into rich behavioural, temporal, contextual, and relational features for fraud detection, and the design of the FD4QC service, an API-driven, classical-first/quantum-enhanced architecture intended to integrate advanced fraud detection models—including future quantum ones—into operational environments. It also presents a comparative analysis of classical and quantum-hybrid ML models on a binary financial fraud classification task, with particular emphasis on how domain-specific engineered features interact with both classical ensembles and quantum-hybrid models.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-013, C-014, C-021, C-029, C-047]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses the synthetic IBM Transactions for AML dataset and applies an extensive behavioural feature engineering pipeline that includes statistical account-role features, temporal dynamics, contextual and categorical attributes, behavioural change indicators, and pairwise relationship metrics such as a novel Pair_Equilibrium measure. The data is reduced and undersampled while maintaining a 9:1 non-suspicious to suspicious class ratio, and performance is evaluated on a holdout test set using accuracy, F-measure, precision, recall, and false-positive rate. The FD4QC architecture is designed as an API-driven, classical-first system with automatic rerouting to classical surrogates if quantum backends fail and explicit flags indicating whether predictions come from classical or quantum engines, supporting operational robustness and auditability. Although only simulators are used, the implementation is made compatible with real devices through the PennyLane interface.
    [codes: C-002, C-013, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: Beyond methods and results, the authors situate their work in the broader context of escalating fraud losses and characterize quantum machine learning as a nascent but intriguing alternative worth exploring, while emphasizing that classical ensemble methods combined with domain-specific behavioural features remain the preferred choice today.
    [codes: C-009, C-011, C-045]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is highly relevant to fraud_detection because it directly studies binary fraud/AML transaction classification, compares classical and quantum-hybrid detectors, and emphasizes operational concerns like false positives, auditability, and fallback behavior. Its strongest fraud-detection value is the behavioural feature engineering pipeline and the finding that classical ensembles currently outperform quantum models on this task.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: Directly targets fraud/AML transaction classification with a classical-vs-quantum benchmark.
    [codes: C-001, C-008, C-012, C-015  dim_source: dimension_1]

    [codes: C-002, C-013, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021  dim_source: dimension_7]

    [codes: C-003, C-006, C-037, C-038, C-045  dim_source: dimension_3]

=== PAPER 993b4e035b55 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames a broad need for scalable pattern recognition in high-dimensional, noisy data that increasingly arises in domains including financial analytics, where the curse of dimensionality, sparsity, and optimization complexity limit classical machine learning. It positions pattern recognition in such settings as a central challenge that motivates exploring alternative computational paradigms, with finance mentioned specifically in the context of real‑world noisy data such as financial anomaly detection.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-007, C-008, C-016, C-017, C-022, C-025]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors conceptually examine hybrid quantum–classical machine learning architectures that embed quantum subroutines inside classical workflows. Key quantum elements include quantum feature mapping that embeds classical data into high-dimensional Hilbert spaces, variational quantum circuits, and quantum probabilistic sampling, while classical components handle preprocessing, parameter optimization, orchestration, and pairings with neural networks or SVMs. These hybrid designs aim to exploit quantum superposition and entanglement for richer feature representations and search, without fully replacing classical infrastructures.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-010, C-011, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-018, C-020, C-023, C-031, C-032]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: As a conceptual and synthetic work, the paper does not report quantitative experiments but argues that quantum feature mappings can, in principle, render complex patterns more separable and improve model expressiveness when combined with classical classifiers. It further claims that hybrid schemes leveraging quantum sampling with classical error correction may enable adaptive systems that maintain performance under uncertainty, and more broadly suggests that hybrid quantum–classical systems may function as a complementary paradigm that expands the analytical reach and resilience of machine learning in complex data ecosystems.
    [codes: C-005, C-006, C-019, C-020, C-023, C-024, C-034]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper contrasts hybrid architectures with purely classical machine learning by emphasizing classical limitations in high-dimensional settings, such as the curse of dimensionality, overfitting, and exponential computational cost. It argues that quantum-enhanced feature representations, when fed into classical models like neural networks or SVMs, may improve expressiveness without requiring fully quantum systems, and repeatedly stresses that hybrid models are not intended as replacements for classical methods but as integrative frameworks that complement and extend classical analytical capabilities.
    [codes: C-002, C-008, C-011, C-014, C-020, C-032, C-033]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors explicitly acknowledge multiple limitations and open challenges. They highlight constraints of current NISQ hardware, including qubit coherence, gate fidelity, and scalability, which restrict practical deployment. They also note unresolved issues in designing efficient quantum circuits, integrating classical optimization loops, and achieving interpretability of quantum‑enhanced models. In addition, they point to unanswered questions about scalability, hardware compatibility, and cost–benefit trade‑offs that must be resolved before widespread adoption is feasible.
    [codes: C-012, C-026, C-027, C-028]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: For future progress, the paper stresses the need for interdisciplinary collaboration among computer scientists, physicists, and domain experts to tackle the technical and theoretical challenges of hybrid quantum–classical systems and to mature these architectures toward practical applications in complex domains such as finance.
    [codes: C-029]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims as its contribution a conceptual examination of hybrid quantum–classical machine learning architectures as scalable solutions for pattern recognition in high-dimensional, noisy environments, synthesizing recent advances in quantum machine learning and analyzing theoretical foundations, design principles, and emerging application domains. It aims to clarify the complementary roles of quantum and classical computation, positioning hybrid systems as integrative, complementary paradigms that may bridge theoretical quantum advantage with practical machine learning applications and enhance analytical reach and resilience.
    [codes: C-003, C-005, C-006, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: The work is characterized as a synthesis and conceptual analysis rather than an empirical study, indicating that it surveys and analyzes theoretical foundations, architectural principles, and applications instead of providing new datasets, implementations, or quantitative benchmarks. As such, issues of experimental rigor, reproducibility, and dataset quality are not directly addressed.
    [codes: C-005, C-031]

=== PAPER 9f6ab6f7c9d8 (type: central) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper addresses increasingly complex financial transaction fraud and risk assessment, arguing that traditional rule-based systems cannot adapt to evolving fraud patterns, suffer from high false alarms, and struggle with severe class imbalance and millisecond-level decision requirements. It frames the problem as designing an adaptive, high-precision yet scalable detection framework that can handle skewed data and balance precision–scalability trade-offs in operational financial settings.
    [codes: C-001, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The quantum component consists of quantum neural networks (QNNs) that process engineered transaction features encoded into quantum states via quantum feature maps to output a risk probability Pq, and quantum autoencoders (QAEs) that compress and reconstruct inputs to detect anomalies via reconstruction error thresholding. These quantum models are integrated within a broader hybrid framework that also includes classical iXGBoost and k-means, with the quantum parts currently implemented at an experimental level rather than for production deployment.
    [codes: C-007, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-051]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The TransGuard-QNet framework reports very high fraud detection performance, with overall accuracy around 99.09%, precision about 99.51%, recall about 98.51%, and F1-score about 98.59%. It further reports the lowest false positive rate (0.0182) and false negative rate (0.00974) among compared models, as well as strong validation behavior where validation accuracy stabilizes around 97% with minimal overfitting indicated by stable validation loss.
    [codes: C-009, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-046, C-047]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The authors conduct comparative experiments against individual baseline models—iXGBoost, QNN, QAE, and k-means clustering—and claim that the integrated TransGuard-QNet achieves superior accuracy and anomaly detection capability. They further assert that their approach outperforms existing methods not only in fraud detection quality but also in operational efficiency and resource utilization.
    [codes: C-041, C-042, C-043, C-010]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper explicitly acknowledges that, despite promising results, quantum models remain largely experimental and are not ready for large-scale commercial deployment in financial systems. This limitation is attributed to current quantum hardware constraints, high implementation costs, and the lack of mature development frameworks, which collectively restrict actual utilization of the quantum components in existing financial infrastructures.
    [codes: C-050, C-051]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work directions include extending the framework to incorporate real-time data streams, aiming to enhance adaptability to evolving fraud patterns, and integrating explainable AI techniques to provide greater transparency and interpretability of risk predictions produced by the hybrid quantum-classical system.
    [codes: C-052, C-054]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The authors claim as their main contribution a pioneering hybrid quantum machine learning framework, TransGuard-QNet, that integrates supervised (iXGBoost and QNN) and unsupervised (QAE and k-means) models with linear programming-based optimization. They further highlight the framework’s dynamic risk scoring with adaptive thresholding and LP-driven intelligent resource allocation as novel aspects, alongside reported gains in fraud detection performance and operational efficiency compared to existing approaches.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-006, C-007, C-008, C-016, C-017, C-010]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study employs an extensive preprocessing pipeline—including mean imputation for missing values, min–max scaling, low-pass noise filtering, log transformation for skewed data, and dual-stage outlier handling via Isolation Forest and Z-score thresholding—before feature engineering across mathematical, behavioral, statistical, temporal, frequency, and correlation dimensions. Supervised risk prediction combines an improved XGBoost variant with a regularized objective tailored to imbalanced data and a QNN risk estimator, while unsupervised detection uses QAEs and k-means; their outputs are aggregated with LP-optimized nonnegative weights that minimize error between a composite score and labeled risk levels. Experiments are run on the Kaggle Financial_Risk dataset using an 80:20 train–test split with a suite of metrics and training/validation curves, and the iXGBoost component is described as parallel and distributed computing-enabled to support scalability and efficiency, though explicit quantum resource details and code availability are not reported.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-005, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-006, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-046, C-047, C-048, C-049]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: None; all verified codes map to the specified reporting dimensions.
