=== PAPER a2b17048118b (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets the growing financial problem of insurance claim fraud, described as causing substantial and increasing monetary losses, and frames it as a binary classification task where each transaction is labeled fraudulent or non‑fraudulent based on engineered claim features. The authors emphasise that fraud detection in insurance requires meticulous examination of claims data and that advanced analytics and machine learning are needed to improve pattern detection and reduce fraud. They position this classification problem as suitable for exploration with quantum machine learning techniques, particularly to handle complex interactions in financial transaction data.
    [codes: C-001, C-011, C-013, C-027, C-006]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The study employs a Quantum Support Vector Machine (QSVM) classifier described as a novel quantum machine‑learning technique for improving classification tasks, using a quantum kernel estimated directly on a quantum computer. Classical fraud features are embedded via a parametric quantum circuit Uφ(θ) acting on n qubits, with a low‑depth ZZ Feature Map chosen as the quantum feature map to exploit entanglement and superposition and enhance expressive power while remaining implementable on near‑term devices. The authors note that QSVM leverages quantum parallelism to process and analyse financial data, and they conceptually separate training (kernel estimation and support‑vector identification) from testing within this quantum‑kernel framework.
    [codes: C-005, C-008, C-026, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-039]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: On the home‑insurance fraud dataset with engineered features, all models achieve high accuracy: KNN 97.3%, classical SVM 99.0%, and QSVM 98.3%. The authors state that the engineered claim‑based features provide valuable discriminative information for the classification, and argue that the QSVM improves feature‑space analysis within their combined quantum‑classical fraud‑detection approach. They describe QSVM performance as promising despite its slightly lower accuracy than the best classical baseline, and further claim that, due to the way the kernel is estimated, the required computational resources for QSVM do not exhibit exponential scaling with problem size as in some classical methods.
    [codes: C-031, C-007, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-038]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The experimental design explicitly compares the QSVM against classical machine‑learning methods—KNN and SVM—as well as against a domain‑expert, rule‑based benchmark model, all trained on the same insurance claims dataset. Reported accuracies show that the classical SVM slightly outperforms QSVM (99.0% vs 98.3%), with KNN somewhat lower at 97.3%, leading the authors to characterise QSVM as competitive but not superior in this setting. They additionally position QSVM as enhancing feature‑space analysis relative to classical techniques and, citing prior work, mention potential runtime advantages for quantum SVMs, though no empirical speedup is demonstrated in their own results.
    [codes: C-004, C-005, C-022, C-023, C-025, C-007, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-014]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge that the predictions of QSVM and classical models can diverge on specific data points, implying potential sensitivity or disagreement in edge cases. They also clarify that their reported quantum results were obtained using simulations on a quantum computer rather than extensive execution on actual noisy hardware, which limits claims about real‑device performance. In addition, they note that personally identifiable information has been scrubbed from the dataset, which reflects necessary privacy handling but may also constrain the richness of available features.
    [codes: C-043, C-044, C-029]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work is explicitly directed toward extending the current QSVM and quantum feature‑selection approach from simulation to implementation on actual quantum hardware, with the implied aim of validating performance under realistic device noise and resource constraints.
    [codes: C-045]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims methodological novelty by introducing a feature‑selection mechanism that can be paired with arbitrary redundancy and importance measures and by combining quantum feature‑selection methods with traditional feature‑selection techniques to evaluate a quantum fraud‑detection classifier. It further presents and explores hybrid quantum/classical machine‑learning ensembles, comparing their performance to purely classical and fully quantum methods within the insurance‑fraud domain, and characterises QSVM itself as a novel classification technique in this application context.
    [codes: C-003, C-009, C-010, C-026]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study runs an experiment on a real home‑insurance claims dataset comprising actual claims payment transactions with scrubbed personally identifiable information and 15 engineered, weighted parameters derived from domain knowledge to capture fraud indicators. The fraud‑detection task is formulated as a binary classifier, and the authors apply feature selection to reduce input dimensionality, using correlation heatmaps for exploratory data analysis, and then feed the selected features into rule‑based, classical (KNN, SVM, XGBoost) and quantum QSVM models for comparison. They emphasise that selecting an appropriate quantum feature map is dataset‑dependent and choose a low‑depth ZZ Feature Map to remain compatible with near‑term devices, aligning with a broader literature trend that uses dimensionality reduction to minimise quantum resource needs and demonstrating awareness of implementation constraints.
    [codes: C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-035, C-016]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The authors situate their work within the broader quantum‑machine‑learning literature, citing results that quantum kernels and QSVMs have shown improved performance or speedups in other domains such as anomaly detection and financial fraud, and noting that dimensionality reduction is commonly used to reduce quantum resource requirements. They also argue more generally that the intrinsic capabilities of quantum systems to simulate complex interactions make them well‑suited to the intricacies of financial transactions, and that heuristic, rule‑based approaches have historically dominated fraud detection but may benefit from being augmented or replaced by advanced analytics and quantum‑enhanced methods.
    [codes: C-006, C-012, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021]
[SILO OVERLAY]

Its main value for the silo is as an example of quantum/classical benchmarking for fraud detection rather than a demonstrated hardware advantage.
    [silo_relevance_score: medium]

    [codes: C-001, C-011, C-013, C-027  dim_source: dimension_1]

=== PAPER a5b68f8e6d90 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames financial fraud as deceptive behavior for gain and focuses specifically on credit fraud, motivating the need for accurate and fast detection systems. It formulates fraud detection as a binary classification problem on the BankSim credit-fraud dataset, treating anomaly detection as integral to identifying fraudulent vs non-fraudulent transactions. The task is set up for quantum machine learning as supervised classification of balanced fraud/non-fraud samples using selected features such as age, gender, category, and amount.
    [codes: C-006, C-007, C-008, C-014, C-028, C-030, C-037, C-038]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The study conducts a comparative analysis of four quantum machine learning models for fraud detection: a Quantum Support Vector Classifier (QSVC), a Variational Quantum Classifier (VQC), an Estimator Quantum Neural Network (EQNN), and a Sampler Quantum Neural Network (SQNN), all implemented in Qiskit and executed on the Aer QasmSimulator. Data are encoded into quantum states using three feature maps—ZZFeatureMap (second-order Pauli-Z evolution), PauliFeatureMap (Pauli expansion), and ZFeatureMap (first-order Pauli-Z evolution)—with QSVC relying critically on the choice of kernel, VQC and EQNN implemented as hybrid variational architectures optimized via COBYLA with up to 200 iterations, and SQNN using a quantum sampler to draw states from complex distributions.
    [codes: C-001, C-013, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-042, C-043]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Across the four QML models tested on the BankSim subset, QSVC with the ZFeatureMap is reported as the top performer, achieving F1 scores of 0.98 for both fraud and non-fraud classes and 0.98 accuracy. VQC attains an F1 score of about 0.90 and a best loss of 0.5 with the ZFeatureMap, but shows higher losses (0.95) with PauliFeatureMap and ZZFeatureMap; EQNN reaches an accuracy of 0.78 and an F1 score around 0.78 with ZFeatureMap, while SQNN performs worst with an accuracy of about 0.58 and F1 scores around 0.57–0.64. Exploratory analysis on the underlying data indicates that fraudulent transactions are typically of higher monetary amount, more common among individuals aged 26–35 and females, and concentrated in certain merchant categories such as “sports & toys” and “health,” with “amount” identified as the most influential feature.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-004, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-036, C-044, C-045, C-047, C-048, C-049, C-051, C-054, C-055]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The authors position QML as potentially more efficient than classical machine learning by enabling new algorithms and quantum parallelisation that could handle larger, real-time datasets, and they note prior work where quantum fraud detection was found superior to classical approaches. However, in this study they do not report explicit experimental comparisons against classical baselines (e.g., classical SVM or neural networks), nor provide runtime or complexity benchmarks—so the relative performance vs classical methods remains speculative and supported only by cited literature.
    [codes: C-009, C-010, C-011, C-012, C-016]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge several limitations. At a high level, they note the need for more efficient quantum algorithms and for larger, more complex datasets to fully realize quantum fraud detection, implying that current results are constrained by algorithmic maturity and data scale. Methodologically, they report optimization challenges for EQNN, where the training process struggled to reach an optimal solution and reduced its accuracy. For SQNN, they explicitly state that its lower accuracy may stem from inherent limitations in handling continuous and high-dimensional data like their BankSim-derived feature space, and they also indicate that SQNN is better suited to combinatorial optimization and constraint problems rather than this classification task. More generally, they suggest that the choice of quantum circuits and data encoding substantially affects EQNN and SQNN performance, indicating sensitivity to circuit design. Finally, all experiments are conducted on a small, balanced subset (200 records) of a much larger, highly imbalanced synthetic dataset, which implicitly limits the realism and scalability of the reported performance.
    [codes: C-005, C-050, C-052, C-053, C-057, C-029, C-030, C-038]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: For future work, the authors point to refining the VQC training process as a promising avenue to further improve its performance beyond the reported F1 score of 0.90, and more broadly imply the need to develop more efficient quantum algorithms and to test on larger, more complex datasets as the field and hardware mature.
    [codes: C-005, C-056]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper’s contribution is framed as a comparative study of four different QML models—QSVC, VQC, EQNN, and SQNN—on a credit fraud detection task using the BankSim dataset, systematically examining how different quantum feature maps and hybrid architectures impact fraud classification performance. They also emphasize how feature selection and quantum data encoding choices (e.g., ZFeatureMap vs Pauli-based maps) critically affect model accuracy, particularly highlighting the strong performance of QSVC with a simple ZFeatureMap.
    [codes: C-001, C-013, C-017, C-019, C-022, C-024, C-044, C-054, C-057]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study carefully preprocesses the synthetic BankSim dataset by cleaning age values with regular expressions, label-encoding categorical variables (e.g., category and gender), and performing PCA-based dimensionality reduction plus domain reasoning to select four informative features—age, gender, category, and amount—for modeling. From the original 594,643-record, heavily imbalanced dataset (7,200 fraud vs 587,443 non-fraud), they construct a balanced subset of 200 records (100 fraud, 100 non-fraud), then apply a train–test split and evaluate using standard metrics such as precision, recall, F1, loss, and accuracy. All quantum models are implemented in Qiskit using the Aer QasmSimulator backend, with VQC, EQNN, and SQNN trained via the COBYLA optimizer capped at 200 iterations. The authors explicitly discuss how kernel choice and feature maps influence QSVC performance, and they present exploratory descriptive statistics and demographics to justify feature importance, but they do not provide code or classical baselines, and they simulate ideal (noise-free) quantum hardware, which affects external validity and reproducibility.
    [codes: C-017, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-046, C-047, C-048, C-049, C-051]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The authors reiterate that fraud detection is closely related to anomaly detection and that the QSVC model differs conceptually from classical machine learning models by not employing a standard loss function, instead relying on its quantum kernel structure for classification. They also underscore that quantum approaches may, in the post-NISQ era, enable faster analysis of large-scale, real-time transactional data via parallelisation, though these points are positioned as broader context rather than empirically validated in this work.
    [codes: C-010, C-011, C-012, C-014, C-046]

=== PAPER a7b3e49448d1 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames its problem as the need for a comprehensive and systematic review of how quantum computing can address key financial tasks such as financial modeling and optimization, risk analysis, cryptography, and machine learning, including their current limitations and prospects in the financial industry. It highlights classical bottlenecks such as high‑dimensional portfolio optimization challenges and broader industry constraints, motivating investigation into quantum approaches. The review is guided by questions about emerging quantum trends in finance, hurdles to adoption, and potential breakthroughs.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-004, C-005, C-018]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: As a conceptual review, the paper surveys a range of quantum methods rather than implementing a single algorithm. It discusses foundational aspects of qubits, superposition, entanglement, and unitary quantum gates, then maps specific finance-relevant algorithms: Shor’s algorithm for factoring and cryptosystem breaking, Grover’s search for risk and fraud detection, QAOA for combinatorial portfolio optimization via QUBO reformulation, and VQE for eigenvalue-based option pricing and market modeling. It further covers quantum amplitude estimation as the mechanism behind quantum Monte Carlo speedups in risk management, and quantum machine learning models such as QSVM and QBMs, along with prospective quantum reinforcement learning for trading.
    [codes: C-007, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-020, C-022, C-021, C-028, C-030]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The paper does not report original empirical or quantitative results, but summarizes claimed advantages from the literature. It notes that QAOA-based optimization can streamline exploration of portfolio scenarios by leveraging superposition and entanglement, and that quantum Monte Carlo with amplitude estimation theoretically reduces sample complexity from O(1/λ²) to O(1/λ) for risk management tasks. It also reports claims that quantum machine learning models such as QSVMs and QBMs can improve handling of financial data compared to classical machine learning, while emphasizing that many of these advantages are still prospective rather than demonstrated at production scale.
    [codes: C-019, C-021, C-022, C-028, C-034]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: Classical approaches are used as a baseline mainly at the conceptual level. The authors contrast quantum portfolio optimization with classical methods like Markowitz’s mean–variance model, arguing that the latter struggles with high-dimensional datasets and computational complexity, whereas QAOA-based approaches may explore many scenarios in parallel. They also note that quantum Monte Carlo offers a quadratic sample-complexity improvement over classical Monte Carlo and that quantum machine learning is claimed to handle financial data better than classical models, while simultaneously acknowledging that not all financial problems benefit from quantum acceleration.
    [codes: C-018, C-019, C-021, C-028, C-034]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The review underscores substantial limitations to practical quantum finance. On the hardware side, current devices are NISQ-era machines with high error rates and short coherence times, too few qubits to tackle real-world financial problems, and heavy overhead from error correction where many physical qubits are needed for a single logical qubit, all of which hamper scalability. Algorithmically, the authors note that not all financial problems exhibit proven quantum speedups and that hybrid quantum–classical approaches introduce additional overhead due to frequent switching between resources, reducing overall efficiency. From an adoption standpoint, they highlight difficulties integrating quantum solutions with legacy systems, including the need for new architectures, quantum programming expertise, and hardware investment, and they flag that regulatory and legal frameworks for quantum-driven financial models and quantum-induced security threats are still immature.
    [codes: C-003, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The paper outlines several future directions. Technically, it calls for more resilient, scalable quantum hardware and improved error correction, along with better quantum heuristics and hybrid algorithms that integrate classical and quantum resources more efficiently and new algorithms tailored specifically to financial problems. It presents a staged timeline: in the short term (0–5 years), focusing on hybrid algorithms and advanced error correction; in the mid-term (5–10 years), achieving higher qubit counts and lower error rates to enable fault-tolerant quantum computing for select financial use cases; and in the long term (10+ years), envisioning fully scalable quantum computers that could enable real-time Monte Carlo simulations, complex derivatives modeling, and quantum cryptography. On the security side, the authors emphasize ongoing development of post-quantum cryptography (including lattice- and hash-based schemes) and quantum-resistant blockchains, and explicitly pose open research questions around improving scalability and error correction for finance, and how the financial sector should prepare for post-quantum cryptography to mitigate Shor-related risks. They also point to prospective applications such as quantum reinforcement learning for enhanced algorithmic trading.
    [codes: C-024, C-025, C-027, C-030, C-038, C-039, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-046, C-047, C-048]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The authors position their contribution as a comprehensive and systematic review of the convergence of quantum computing and finance, explicitly motivated by the perceived absence of such a consolidated assessment of current applications, limitations, and future prospects. They also frame the paper around clearly stated guiding questions about emerging quantum trends in financial services, adoption hurdles, and potential breakthroughs, but do not claim novel algorithms, implementations, or empirical benchmarks.
    [codes: C-001, C-004, C-005]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the paper is a conceptual, narrative review without reported quantitative experiments, datasets, or reproducible implementations. It outlines the structure of the survey—starting with basics of quantum computing in finance and then covering applications, challenges, and prospects—but does not describe systematic review protocols, search strategies, or evaluation metrics. As such, its rigor is primarily in synthesis rather than in experimental validation.
    [codes: C-002, C-006]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: Several additional observations fall outside the other dimensions. The paper surveys quantum hardware platforms relevant to finance—superconducting qubits (used by firms such as IBM and Google), trapped ions with high fidelity and long coherence times, and photonic systems with anticipated scalability and error-correction advantages. It also discusses cross-cutting security implications: Shor’s algorithm threatens RSA and ECC-based financial cryptosystems, and current blockchains depending on asymmetric cryptography are deemed susceptible to quantum attacks, motivating quantum-safe alternatives. Finally, it notes growing industry–academia collaborations, with major banks such as JPMorgan Chase, Goldman Sachs, and HSBC investing in quantum research with quantum labs and universities.
    [codes: C-010, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-023, C-026, C-027, C-040, C-041]

Data is classically preprocessed, then encoded via amplitude, basis, or angle encoding into quantum feature maps and circuits that are optimized under NISQ constraints using shallow, hardware-aware compilation, error mitigation, and parameter-shift gradient estimation within a quantum–classical training loop.

=== PAPER b17e753ba135 (type: central) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper tackles bank transaction fraud detection by viewing it as a community detection task on a transaction graph, where accounts are nodes and transactions are edges, motivated by the need to maintain financial security in big-data environments. Fraud detection is framed as uncovering hidden community structures—particularly organized fraud communities—via modularity maximization on this graph using a QUBO formulation of community detection. The approach is explicitly positioned as an unsupervised method that identifies high‑risk communities rather than individual or occasional frauds.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-014, C-019, C-020, C-029, C-032, C-033, C-048, C-049]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors formulate modularity-maximizing community detection as a QUBO problem with binary assignment variables and a penalty term enforcing one community per node, then exploit the mathematical equivalence between QUBO and Ising models to map it onto an Ising Hamiltonian. This Ising problem is solved on a Coherent Ising Machine (CIM), a room‑temperature photonic quantum optimization platform composed of optical and electrical components, which searches for the ground state corresponding to the optimal community structure. They implement this by encoding the QUBO matrix into an FPGA with 8‑bit and 14‑bit precision and running the CIM to partition a 308‑node denoised transaction graph into four communities.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-013, C-017, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-034, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-050]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: On the denoised 308‑node transaction graph, the CIM‑based QUBO solver successfully partitions the network into four communities and, in both 8‑bit and 14‑bit encodings, is reported to find the optimal community structure. The detected structure includes a clearly identifiable high‑risk community with a fraud probability around 14.4%—more than double the overall fraud rate of about 6.17%—and this community contains nearly 70% of all fraudulent accounts, which the authors argue demonstrates substantial business value. Quantitatively, the CIM is found to reach high modularity solutions more frequently (e.g., the 8‑bit CIM produces the highest modularity roughly one‑third of the time) and achieves sub‑millisecond to a few‑millisecond time‑to‑solution, with the conclusions restating that CIM + QUBO completes detection in ~1 ms and has a >10% likelihood of producing the optimal solution.
    [codes: C-005, C-032, C-040, C-041, C-043, C-045, C-047, C-048, C-049, C-055, C-056, C-057]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The CIM approach is systematically compared with classical Louvain and simulated annealing (SA) algorithms run 100 times each on the same graph using specified settings and a standard laptop CPU. The authors report that CIM computes faster than both Louvain and SA, with time‑to‑solution at least one order of magnitude lower than Louvain (~1 ms vs. ~10–35 ms) and orders of magnitude faster than SA (~10^2–10^3×), while also achieving better or comparable community quality in terms of modularity and having a higher likelihood of reaching the optimal modularity configuration. Louvain is noted to reach the best modularity only once in 100 trials, while SA is much slower and never achieves the optimal modularity, whereas the best partitions from all methods are almost identical when they do reach that optimum and yield similar high‑risk communities in terms of fraud concentration and recall.
    [codes: C-015, C-016, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-041, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-046, C-047, C-048, C-049, C-055, C-056]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper acknowledges several limitations and boundary conditions. At the hardware level, the QUBO matrix encoding on the CIM’s FPGA has limited precision, necessitating experiments with 8‑bit and 14‑bit encodings and revealing sensitivity to noise and precision errors, especially at higher bit depth. From a modeling standpoint, the QUBO formulation requires a number of binary variables (and thus qubits) equal to the product of nodes and communities, so qubit requirements grow rapidly with the number of communities even for fixed node count, posing a scalability constraint. On the application side, the authors stress that their method is tailored to uncovering organized fraud networks and may not be suitable when most fraudulent accounts act individually, and they explicitly leave individual or occasional fraud cases to rule‑based or AI‑based screens. They also note that if fraud is predominantly non‑networked, transaction networks may be ill-suited to QUBO‑based community detection via quantum computing.
    [codes: C-011, C-030, C-033, C-038, C-042, C-050, C-051, C-052]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: For deployment at realistic banking scales, the authors suggest applying community detection selectively to relevant subgraphs instead of entire transaction networks, thereby mitigating qubit-scaling challenges and focusing on suspicious regions. They also express the expectation that, as hardware matures, quantum community detection for anti-fraud could be practically implemented in commercial banks’ operations in the near future, implying further work on integration and scaling.
    [codes: C-053, C-054]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The study positions itself as introducing a novel fraud detection method that leverages quantum computing—specifically CIM‑based QUBO community detection—for transaction networks, directly addressing hardware challenges that prior quantum fraud‑detection work had not considered. It claims to extend the application scope of Coherent Ising Machines beyond previously explored areas such as federated learning, incremental learning, and quantum annealing, and emphasizes the business value of clearly identified high‑risk communities in a real banking anti‑fraud context.
    [codes: C-001, C-012, C-013, C-041, C-057, C-058]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the paper carefully defines the graph modeling, modularity objective, constraints, and QUBO/Ising transformations, including binary assignment variables and penalty terms, which supports reproducibility of the formulation. The dataset is a proprietary transaction graph from a commercial bank, with explicit description of sampling (initial 3934 accounts with ~5% fraud) and denoising steps that reduce it to 308 accounts with 19 fraudulent ones; preprocessing emphasizes rule‑based high‑risk identification and removal of low‑risk and isolated nodes to focus on organized fraud. Experimental protocols are described for the CIM hardware (optical and electrical parts, FPGA encoding precision), and classical baselines (Louvain and SA) are bench‑marked on specified CPU hardware and run 100 times each, with metrics such as modularity, time‑to‑solution, and best‑modularity success frequency reported. However, reliance on a denoised, targeted subgraph and proprietary data limits direct reproducibility across institutions, and no code availability is mentioned in the extracted material.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-017, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-039, C-040, C-045, C-047]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper also frames quantum computing more broadly as a rapidly growing paradigm capable of tackling challenging computational problems, contrasted with the practical hardware difficulties of gate‑based and adiabatic quantum computing, and uses this as part of the motivation for exploring CIMs as a practical optimization platform for financial applications.
    [codes: C-010, C-011, C-018]

=== PAPER b253d747bcc9 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets credit card fraud detection in financial transactions, framing it as an anomaly (outlier) detection task in a heavily imbalanced dataset dominated by genuine samples. Fraud detection is explicitly cast as identifying anomalous transactions within a Kaggle credit card dataset described by 28 anonymized features and a very small minority of fraud cases.
    [codes: C-001, C-009, C-010, C-011]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors employ quantum kernel methods built from quantum feature maps that embed classical transaction data into N‑qubit states, focusing on instantaneous quantum polynomial (IQP) circuits with data re-uploading. Data is angle-encoded into a quantum state |φ(x)⟩ on an N‑qubit register using IQP-style circuits, and kernels are defined via state overlaps κ(x,x′)=|⟨ψ(x)|ψ(x′)⟩|², estimated through return-probability measurements; in experiments they concentrate on IQP circuits re-uploaded three times with features scaled by a factor η=0.1.
    [codes: C-005, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: On the subsampled credit card dataset, quantum kernels based on IQP circuits with re-uploading achieve higher average precision than classical baselines as the feature/qubit count grows, with a reported quantum–classical separation of about 15% AP at 20 qubits. The quantum anomaly detection setup (unsupervised) attains average precision around 0.7 with IQP kernels, while the best classical OC‑SVM baseline saturates around AP≈0.55; in supervised settings, quantum kernels also show improved AP and appear less prone to overfitting at higher feature dimensions.
    [codes: C-006, C-007, C-008, C-015, C-021, C-023, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-049]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: Classical benchmarks include supervised logistic regression and RBF-kernel SVC, as well as an unsupervised OC‑SVM with RBF kernel, all trained and tuned using standard ML practices. The authors report that SVC slightly outperforms logistic regression for medium feature counts, but both supervised methods deteriorate in average precision at higher dimensionality due to overfitting, while the OC‑SVM AP increases with feature count before saturating around 15 features at AP≈0.55. In contrast, quantum kernels—especially IQP circuits with re‑uploading—exhibit improved AP and ultimately outperform classical RBF kernels at larger feature spaces, with a quantum–classical AP separation of roughly 15% at 20 qubits/features.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-004, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-031, C-033, C-034, C-049]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors emphasize that the extreme class imbalance in the original dataset makes accuracy a misleading metric and motivates the use of average precision instead. They acknowledge significant scalability limitations of kernel-based quantum fraud detection: full Gram matrix estimation scales quadratically in the number of samples, making training computationally expensive for large datasets (~100,000 samples), and real-time detection may be infeasible if per-test latency is high. Reliable kernel estimation requires at least 10³ measurement shots, which further inflates training times; for large datasets, forming full Gram matrices on near-term hardware is described as extremely costly, with brute-force evaluations on slow platforms such as trapped ions becoming practically infeasible (e.g., 30-year estimates). They also note that photonic systems, despite high potential clock rates, currently lack established deterministic optical gate implementations, posing another significant practical barrier.
    [codes: C-012, C-037, C-039, C-042, C-043, C-046, C-048]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: For practical deployment, the authors argue that future work must clarify operational requirements by defining realistic timescales and precision targets for both training and real-time inference, then matching these to available and anticipated quantum hardware resources. They suggest exploring randomized protocols and adaptive detection schemes to infer only parts of the Gram matrix, thereby reducing the O(Ns²) training cost, and they state that demonstrating a strong quantum advantage in fraud detection will require scaling beyond the 20‑qubit simulations to approximately 50–100 qubits.
    [codes: C-036, C-044, C-050]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The contribution is framed as developing quantum anomaly detection protocols for credit card fraud detection using quantum kernels, and systematically comparing them against classical supervised and unsupervised baselines under realistic, highly imbalanced data conditions. The paper claims that quantum kernels, particularly IQP circuits with data re-uploading, can challenge and eventually outperform classical RBF-based protocols as feature dimensionality increases, offering up to a 15% gain in average precision—an improvement the authors argue could have nontrivial industrial impact.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-005, C-006, C-007, C-008, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-049]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses a public Kaggle credit card fraud dataset with 28 anonymized features and a strongly imbalanced class distribution, but subsamples 500 transactions with 25 fraud cases for the main near-term simulations. Preprocessing includes feature-wise standard scaling, PCA retaining only the first N principal components, and exploratory t‑SNE visualization, with the authors noting that t‑SNE shows only partial nominal–fraud separation. Models are evaluated primarily via average precision due to imbalance, and classical baselines undergo hyperparameter tuning by random search with cross-validation AP as the objective; similar PCA-scaled features (with an additional η=0.1 factor for quantum encoding) are then used to construct both classical and quantum kernels, supporting a reasonably controlled comparison.
    [codes: C-003, C-010, C-011, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-024, C-030]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The authors discuss the broader feasibility of deploying quantum fraud detection across different hardware platforms: superconducting circuits, which can support microsecond-scale circuit evaluations and MHz repetition rates; trapped-ion systems, which trade higher gate fidelity for slower clock rates; and photonic systems, which promise the fastest hypothetical clock rates but lack mature deterministic gate technologies. They also stress that even modest percentage improvements in average precision for fraud detection could translate into multi-million-dollar savings in an industrial setting.
    [codes: C-035, C-041, C-043, C-045, C-047]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is highly relevant to fraud_detection because it directly studies credit card fraud as an anomaly detection problem and evaluates quantum kernels against classical baselines on a highly imbalanced transaction dataset. Its main value for this silo is the evidence that quantum feature maps, especially IQP re-uploading, can improve average precision for rare-fraud identification while also exposing the practical scaling limits that matter for deployment.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: The paper is explicitly about credit card fraud detection framed as anomaly detection in a highly imbalanced transaction dataset.
    [codes: C-001, C-009, C-010, C-011  dim_source: dimension_1]
  KEY_POINT 2: Average precision is used as the main metric because accuracy is misleading under extreme class imbalance.
    [codes: C-003, C-012  dim_source: dimension_5]
  KEY_POINT 3: Quantum kernels with IQP re-uploading are the strongest reported method, improving AP as feature/qubit count grows and reaching about a 15% separation at 20 qubits.
    [codes: C-005, C-007, C-008, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-033, C-034, C-049  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 4: Classical supervised and unsupervised baselines degrade or saturate at higher dimensionality, while quantum kernels appear less prone to overfitting.
    [codes: C-002, C-004, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-023, C-032  dim_source: dimension_4]
  KEY_POINT 5: The paper also highlights deployment bottlenecks: full Gram-matrix training, shot requirements, and real-time latency can make large-scale fraud detection impractical without further scaling.
    [codes: C-036, C-037, C-039, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-046, C-050  dim_source: dimension_5]

=== PAPER b4634f8d9776 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets credit card fraud detection, arguing that manual verification is impractical and motivates fully automated machine‑learning-based systems to detect rare fraudulent transactions in highly imbalanced datasets. It frames two intertwined challenges: improving fraud detection accuracy with fewer training samples to reduce model complexity and computational cost, and mitigating majority‑class bias caused by extreme class imbalance. The authors propose to re-formulate this classification problem for quantum machine learning by using quantum feature mappings and quantum kernels within a QSVM framework.
    [codes: C-001, C-006, C-007, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-018]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The study employs a hybrid quantum–classical QSVM pipeline combined with a Quantum-SMOTE oversampling scheme. For preprocessing, Quantum‑SMOTE uses amplitude encoding to compress high‑dimensional minority‑class data into a logarithmic number of qubits, then applies quantum swap tests, rotation operations, low‑depth circuits, hyperparameter‑controlled generation, and K‑means clustering of minority samples to synthesize balanced training data. For classification, the QSVM maps selected classical features into a 2^n‑dimensional Hilbert space via amplitude or angle encoding and a parametrized feature map, defines a quantum kernel as the inner product between feature states, estimates kernel entries via swap tests on a quantum device, constructs a kernel matrix over all training pairs, and then delegates dual optimization and prediction to a classical SVM solver using support vectors and quantum kernel evaluations.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-004, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-031, C-033]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Empirically, the combined Quantum-SMOTE + QSVM approach yields high performance on the imbalanced credit card fraud dataset: using a 10‑qubit QSVM with Quantum‑SMOTE, the authors report 98.8% accuracy, F1‑score of 0.962, recall of 0.945, and AUC of 0.992, which they describe as approaching a perfect classifier. They state that the QSVM achieves the best overall performance among tested configurations and that its performance advantage increases with the number of qubits, with accuracy gains widening from small differences at 4 qubits up to a 3.7 percentage‑point gap at 10 qubits. The authors further emphasize that high recall and F1 scores indicate strong ability to correctly identify minority (fraud) cases.
    [codes: C-005, C-028, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper compares its quantum pipeline against two classical baselines: traditional undersampling for handling imbalance and classical SVMs for classification. Quantum‑SMOTE reportedly outperforms traditional undersampling by 6.4 percentage points in accuracy (98.8% vs. 92.4%) and also yields higher F1‑score, recall, and AUC. For classification, classical SVMs are trained on matched feature dimensions (4, 8, and 10 features) and evaluated on the same test sets; the authors report that QSVM performance surpasses classical SVM and that the performance gap in accuracy grows with qubit/feature count, reaching a 3.7 percentage‑point advantage for the 10‑qubit configuration.
    [codes: C-031, C-032, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge that current Noisy Intermediate‑Scale Quantum (NISQ) hardware severely limits the number of available qubits, which directly constrains the expressive power and scalability of their QSVM and Quantum‑SMOTE circuits. They also explicitly state that applications of quantum machine learning in financial domains remain in an exploratory phase, implying that their results are preliminary and that broader practical deployment has not yet been demonstrated.
    [codes: C-040, C-042]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work directions identified by the authors include optimizing quantum circuit design, developing more efficient quantum feature mapping techniques, and moving from simulations to implementations on real quantum hardware to better assess practical performance and robustness in financial applications.
    [codes: C-041]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The contribution is framed as introducing a QSVM‑based fraud detection pipeline that leverages quantum feature mapping and quantum kernel methods to enhance accuracy while reducing computational complexity, together with a Quantum‑SMOTE procedure that uses quantum swap tests, rotation, amplitude encoding, and low‑depth circuits to generate high‑quality synthetic minority samples and compress high‑dimensional financial data into few qubits. The authors present this combination as a way to address severe class imbalance and sample‑efficiency challenges in credit card fraud detection using near‑term quantum resources.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-010, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-018]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses a well‑known public benchmark—the highly imbalanced Kaggle credit card fraud dataset with 284,807 transactions and 492 fraud cases, where only numerical PCA‑derived features are available—thus providing clear dataset context. The authors apply KBest feature selection to choose the top 10 features and run experiments with 4, 8, and 10 feature dimensions aligned to qubit counts, and they describe constructing two training regimes: a Quantum‑SMOTE‑balanced set and an undersampled control subset (first 15,000 samples, including 293 frauds). Reporting includes multiple evaluation metrics (accuracy, F1, recall, AUC) to characterize performance, particularly on the minority class. However, all experiments are conducted in simulation rather than on physical hardware, and while hyperparameters for Quantum‑SMOTE are listed conceptually, specific tuned values and full training protocols are not detailed in the coded excerpts.
    [codes: C-007, C-008, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-035, C-036, C-039]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper emphasizes that quantum kernel methods are intended to reduce computational complexity while maintaining classification performance and that low‑depth circuits are chosen to mitigate quantum noise and decoherence, reflecting a design focus on near‑term feasibility in the NISQ era.
    [codes: C-012, C-014, C-040]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is directly relevant to fraud_detection because it targets credit card fraud detection on a severely imbalanced dataset and evaluates a quantum pipeline designed to improve minority-class identification. Its main value for this silo is the combination of imbalance handling (Quantum-SMOTE) and classification (QSVM), with reported gains in recall, F1, and AUC that are especially important for catching rare fraud cases.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: Quantum-SMOTE is used to address extreme class imbalance in fraud data before classification.
    [codes: C-002, C-007, C-008, C-031, C-035, C-036  dim_source: dimension_2]
  KEY_POINT 2: QSVM is presented as the fraud classifier, using quantum feature mapping and quantum kernels to improve separability and reduce complexity.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-004, C-012, C-018, C-023, C-025, C-026, C-027  dim_source: dimension_2]
  KEY_POINT 3: The reported metrics emphasize minority-class detection performance, which is crucial for fraud detection.
    [codes: C-005, C-035, C-036, C-038, C-039  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 4: The study uses the Kaggle credit card fraud benchmark with only 492 fraud cases out of 284,807 transactions and evaluates multiple qubit/feature settings.

=== PAPER ba8b763050ae (type: central) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets credit card fraud detection, motivated by the rising prevalence of fraudulent card transactions and the difficulty of identifying fraud that is deliberately made to resemble legitimate activity. It frames the task as a binary classification problem on a highly imbalanced European credit card transaction dataset, and positions quantum machine learning—particularly hybrid quantum–classical models—as a potential way to improve the efficiency and effectiveness of fraud detection over existing protections that cannot fully prevent fraud.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-007, C-008, C-009, C-016, C-033, C-037, C-047, C-053]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors implement hybrid quantum–classical neural networks for fraud detection, where a quantum layer (realized as a PennyLane QNode) is sandwiched between two conventional neural network layers. PennyLane is used to provide cross‑platform differentiable quantum scripting and to bridge classical ML libraries with quantum simulators; the QNode is converted into a KerasLayer, with requirements such as an argument named 'inputs'. The work conceptually situates itself within quantum machine learning, highlighting the use of superposition and entanglement, and also discusses QSVM, which maps data into a high‑dimensional feature space via quantum kernels, though the primary implemented model is the hybrid QNN.
    [codes: C-002, C-005, C-035, C-036, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-053, C-061]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The paper reports that the hybrid quantum neural network achieves an accuracy of 99.99% on the fraud detection task and is said to provide high accuracy through effective hyperparameter tuning and the use of quantum layers. Across classical baselines, reported metrics include decision trees (accuracy 99.95%, F1 83.01), KNN (accuracy 99.966%, F1 88), logistic regression (accuracy 99.93%, F1 73.75), SVM (accuracy 99.95%, F1 83.91), random forest (accuracy 99.94%, F1 80.55), XGBoost (accuracy 99.964%, F1 87.24), and a classical neural network with 99.95% accuracy. The authors also state that the quantum hybrid model exhibits quicker accuracy convergence than conventional models for equivalent data points, batches, and epochs, and that the quantum models train more effectively for equivalent prediction performance; they further report detection rates of 90% for random forest, 85% for SVM, and 92% for the quantum neural network.
    [codes: C-004, C-054, C-055, C-056, C-057, C-058, C-059, C-060, C-062, C-063, C-064, C-065, C-066, C-070]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The study explicitly compares three families of models: classical ML algorithms, a conventional neural network, and a hybrid quantum–classical neural network. Within classical methods, KNN is reported to excel in precision, F1 score, and recall relative to other classical baselines, while random forest and XGBoost are said to improve precision. The authors claim that, for equivalent prediction performance, quantum models train more effectively and that the hybrid neural network shows faster accuracy convergence than conventional neural networks under matched training conditions, while also achieving higher reported detection rates (92% for QNN vs. 90% random forest and 85% SVM).
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-038, C-039, C-060, C-062, C-063, C-065, C-066, C-070]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors emphasize several limitations of current quantum computing for fraud detection. On the hardware side, noisy devices constrain circuit depth and the loss of entanglement leads to information loss; there is also a shortage of high‑quality usable qubits, and even IBM’s achievement of 100+ qubits is described as insufficient for sophisticated algorithms that may require thousands or millions of qubits. Methodologically, the paper notes that there are no unified, efficient methods for encoding transaction data into quantum states, which is a major barrier for practical QNN applications, and that it is challenging to demonstrate quantum advantage due to noisy hardware and limited qubit counts. In their own experiments, the hybrid quantum model could only be trained on 10% of the dataset due to lengthy computation times and excessive processing times are explicitly acknowledged, even though the algorithm appears to function properly.
    [codes: C-010, C-011, C-012, C-014, C-015, C-017, C-030, C-067, C-068, C-069]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: For future work, the authors propose improving performance by integrating ensemble machine learning algorithms with quantum machine learning models, effectively extending their hybrid approach to include ensemble‑based classical components alongside quantum layers.
    [codes: C-071]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper positions its main contribution as proposing and empirically evaluating a hybrid quantum–classical neural network for credit card fraud detection on a real, highly imbalanced transaction dataset, implemented via PennyLane and compared against multiple classical ML and neural network baselines. It also presents a structured four‑layer system architecture and claims that applying hybrid QML to this fraud detection problem shows significant promise, especially in terms of training efficiency and convergence behavior.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-016, C-031, C-032, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-061, C-062, C-064, C-066, C-070]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the work uses a public Kaggle dataset of European cardholder transactions with a severe class imbalance (492 frauds out of 284,807 transactions) and organizes the pipeline into a four‑layer architecture comprising data collection, feature extraction, training/testing, and result comparison. Data preprocessing includes cleaning (handling missing values via mean, median, or mode), min–max scaling, one‑hot encoding, and explicit handling of data imbalance, with PCA used for feature extraction and dimensionality reduction and t‑SNE for visualization. The authors motivate their choice of classical algorithms (SVM for high‑dimensional spaces, random forest and XGBoost to reduce overfitting and handle sparse/missing data) and run a broad set of baselines. They note that quantum implementations rely on PennyLane’s integration with classical deep‑learning frameworks and that only 10% of the dataset was used for hybrid model training due to computational cost, which constrains the robustness and scalability of the reported quantum results.
    [codes: C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-046, C-047, C-048, C-049, C-050, C-051, C-052, C-061, C-067, C-068, C-069]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper situates its work within broader QML and fraud detection literature, citing prior studies that demonstrate improved quantum classifier performance via LDA and VQAs, streaming fraud detection with feedback mechanisms, hybrid quantum neural networks and topological data analysis for fraud, SMOTE oversampling, GA‑based feature selection, and general claims that hybrid QNNs can enhance precision and generalization with less processing power. It also mentions a real‑world financial application where CaixaBank used PennyLane for QML risk scoring, and characterizes QML as a rapidly expanding field with growing but still uncertain prospects for outperforming classical machine learning.
    [codes: C-013, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029]

=== PAPER ba8e571f1e45 (type: central) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets financial fraud detection, motivated by the rapid growth of fraud losses, and frames it as a highly imbalanced binary classification problem where classical models are described as unsatisfactory, particularly for correctly identifying the minority (fraud) class. The study aims to analyze the potential of quantum classifiers, specifically Variational Quantum Circuits (VQCs), for this fraud-detection setting. The dataset consists of real bank transactions from Intesa Sanpaolo, with only about 2% of samples labeled as fraud, underscoring the imbalance challenge.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-004, C-022, C-028]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors employ quantum machine learning based on Variational Quantum Circuits (VQCs) with angle encoding, arguing that quantum models can exploit higher-dimensional computational spaces through superposition and entanglement to express more complex models. They focus on angle encoding due to its perceived promise for near-term applications and acceptable qubit scaling on current NISQ devices, and evaluate both Basic Entangler Layer and Strongly Entangling Layer ansatz circuits, with data re-uploading treated as an explicit hyperparameter choice. Measurement is performed via Pauli-Z expectation values, chosen for their straightforward implementation, direct interpretation, and zero additional quantum-operation overhead.
    [codes: C-008, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: On reduced, imbalanced fraud-detection datasets, the quantum models are reported to outperform classical baselines on average in terms of precision and recall, and in particular to achieve better recall, which is emphasized as crucial for detecting the minority fraud class. The results suggest that more complex encoding mechanisms tend to yield the best-performing quantum models, and that, on the test set, quantum models often represent the best compromise between precision and recall relative to the classical alternatives. The authors characterize these findings as preliminary but encouraging evidence of quantum models’ potential in such highly unbalanced classification tasks.
    [codes: C-006, C-013, C-031, C-035, C-036, C-007]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The study benchmarks the VQC-based quantum classifiers against several classical models, including Logistic Regression, XGBoost, Support Vector Machines, Random Forests, and Neural Networks. Classical baselines are reported to have very low precision–recall performance, especially when fewer features are available, with Random Forest showing signs of overfitting due to a substantial drop from training to validation performance. Among classical approaches, logistic regression is identified as the most promising baseline for this task, yet quantum models are still claimed to offer a better overall precision–recall trade-off in most cases.
    [codes: C-012, C-013, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-036]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors explicitly describe their work as preliminary and caution that the results, while encouraging, only prove the potential of quantum models in this context rather than any definitive advantage. Due to the exponential growth of simulation complexity with the number of qubits, they are forced to reduce both the number of transactions and the number of features via PCA, which constrains the realism and scale of the experiments. They also assess execution on real IBM devices and find that current hardware noise significantly degrades performance, leading them to conclude that existing quantum devices are not yet ready for deployment in real application scenarios. Additionally, they note that accuracy is an inappropriate evaluation metric in such highly unbalanced datasets, which implicitly limits comparability with prior work that might rely on this metric.
    [codes: C-007, C-009, C-010, C-021, C-038]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The paper proposes several avenues for future research: exploring alternative dimensionality-reduction techniques beyond PCA, investigating other data-encoding mechanisms such as amplitude encoding and additional ansatz circuits, and evaluating other quantum circuit model solutions like Quantum Support Vector Machines and hybrid quantum–classical models. They also suggest studying machine-learning procedures where quantum computation is used in the parameter-optimization phase via suitable QUBO formulations.
    [codes: C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The authors position their contribution as an analysis of the potential of VQC-based quantum classifiers for real-world financial fraud detection on highly imbalanced data, using a proprietary bank transaction dataset and comparing numerous encoding and ansatz configurations against standard classical baselines. They emphasize that quantum models, operating in intrinsically higher-dimensional spaces, can realize more complex classifiers that in their experiments provide superior recall and generally better precision–recall trade-offs than the evaluated classical models, albeit in a preliminary and resource-constrained setting.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-004, C-008, C-011, C-013, C-035, C-036, C-007]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses a large real-world dataset of 500,000 bank transactions with 15 features, reduced via PCA to smaller datasets with 2, 4, 6, and 8 principal components, where eight components retain over 99% of the variance. Because simulation cost scales exponentially with qubit count, the authors downsample transactions and features and then systematically train and evaluate 400 quantum models per reduced dataset to select the best-performing configuration using a validation set. They optimize decision thresholds for the highly imbalanced setting, arguing that accuracy is inappropriate and instead prioritizing recall and the precision–recall curve over ROC, with final model assessment performed on a held-out test set using precision and recall. Training is standardized across models (10 epochs, mini-batches of size 10, Adam optimizer with binary cross-entropy loss), and the study also includes an assessment of real-device noise using Hellinger fidelity and Kullback–Leibler divergence to compare ideal simulator and hardware outcome distributions.
    [codes: C-004, C-005, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-014, C-020, C-021, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-039]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: None.
    [codes: —]

=== PAPER bcfb2bcd887f (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper addresses financial fraud detection under conditions of rapidly increasing transaction volumes, sophisticated attack vectors, and strict real-time processing requirements, emphasizing the difficulty of capturing temporal patterns ranging from microsecond attacks to long-term behavioral manipulation campaigns and the exponential complexity of analyzing network-level temporal correlations. This motivates formulating fraud detection as a temporal correlation analysis problem over transaction streams and associated networks that may benefit from quantum processing.
    [codes: C-001, C-006, C-007]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The proposed method, Adaptive Quantum Entanglement Networks (AQEN), uses adaptive entanglement mechanisms that dynamically adjust quantum correlations between qubits based on transaction patterns, formalized through a time-dependent entanglement tensor and an adaptive learning update rule. The authors define a quantum temporal state space and a Quantum Temporal Fraud Operator to capture temporal correlations, then instantiate this in an algorithmic pipeline (Algorithm 1) comprising temporal feature encoding, adaptive entangling operations, and measurement-based fraud probability estimation, implemented via a Quantum Fraud Correlation Circuit (QFCC) operating on 8 qubits in a hybrid encoding-processing layout and targeting practical deployment on near-term NISQ devices.
    [codes: C-002, C-005, C-013, C-014, C-017, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Empirically, AQEN is reported to achieve 94.7% accuracy, 81.2% precision, and 79.8% recall on a synthetic fraud dataset, with an F1 score of 0.805 and ROC AUC of 0.951, and these improvements are described as highly statistically significant (p < 0.001) according to paired t-tests across 20 runs. The authors also claim a theoretical detection probability advantage for quantum detectors using the Quantum Temporal Fraud Operator compared to classical temporal methods, provide sample-complexity and computational-complexity bounds favoring AQEN, and prove convergence guarantees for both the adaptive entanglement update rule and the overall adaptive learning mechanism (including exponential-rate convergence and almost sure convergence to an optimal entanglement configuration). The paper further reports that AQEN training converges stably around epoch 80 without overfitting, ablation studies quantify performance gains from temporal encoding and other components, and AQEN is said to deliver substantial computational efficiency, with faster training and inference and lower memory usage, while maintaining robust performance under realistic quantum noise levels.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-015, C-016, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-044]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The study compares AQEN against several classical fraud detection baselines—logistic regression with engineered features, random forests with temporal feature engineering, SVMs with RBF kernels, and gradient boosting (XGBoost)—all tuned via systematic hyperparameter optimization with stratified cross-validation to ensure fairness. AQEN is reported as best overall in terms of accuracy, precision, recall, F1, and AUC, with a ROC AUC increase from 0.923 for XGBoost to 0.951 for AQEN, interpreted as a 28% reduction in ranking errors, and the performance differences are stated to be highly statistically significant (p < 0.001). The authors also claim substantial computational advantages over classical temporal analysis, both in terms of theoretical training and inference complexity and in empirical resource efficiency, including 6.7× faster training, 7.4× faster inference, and 7.1× lower memory usage than XGBoost, with all timing measurements collected on the same dedicated workstation under controlled benchmarking conditions.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-020, C-030, C-033, C-034, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-042, C-043]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge that their theoretical guarantees for AQEN depend on specific structural assumptions, namely that fraud patterns exhibit structured temporal correlations and that quantum hardware noise remains bounded. They further caution that in more challenging or adversarial conditions—such as adversarially obfuscated fraud patterns, scenarios where quantum encodings must scale linearly with the feature dimension, or in the presence of highly correlated hardware noise—the practical performance and efficiency advantages of AQEN may disappear.
    [codes: C-045, C-046]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work directions include developing quantum ensemble methods that combine multiple AQEN instances to enhance robustness and accuracy via quantum voting and error averaging, and exploring multi-modal quantum integration that fuses diverse data sources—such as transaction records, communication patterns, and behavioral biometrics—through quantum data fusion techniques for richer fraud detection signals.
    [codes: C-047, C-048]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims several key contributions: a comprehensive mathematical theory for quantum temporal correlation analysis in fraud detection, including a formal quantum temporal state space and a Quantum Temporal Fraud Operator; novel quantum circuit architectures specifically optimized for temporal fraud analysis, instantiated as the QFCC; and an adaptive entanglement mechanism with a corresponding learning rule that dynamically tunes quantum correlations to evolving fraud patterns, for which the authors provide convergence guarantees and position as the core innovation of AQEN.
    [codes: C-002, C-009, C-010, C-013, C-014, C-017, C-018, C-027, C-028]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the paper makes its assumptions explicit, including structured temporal correlations in fraud patterns (polynomial of degree up to 3 with minimum correlation strength) and quantified NISQ hardware error rates, and then builds a formal quantum temporal state-space and operators under these assumptions. The dataset is a synthetic temporal financial fraud dataset of 5,000 transactions with an 8% fraud rate and engineered temporal and network features, with preprocessing tailored for quantum encoding while aiming to preserve discriminative information; the authors also state that classical feature engineering is kept in parity with the quantum pipeline. Classical baselines undergo systematic hyperparameter optimization via stratified cross-validation, and both classical and quantum models are benchmarked on the same dedicated workstation with controlled conditions; AQEN is implemented in a high-fidelity quantum simulation environment with noise models calibrated to current NISQ devices. The study reports detailed statistical evaluation, including paired t-tests and AUC-based analysis, training dynamics (e.g., convergence around epoch 80 without overfitting), ablation studies to quantify the contribution of components such as temporal encoding, and a computational complexity analysis contrasting quantum and classical training and inference costs.
    [codes: C-006, C-007, C-008, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-037, C-038, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: Quantum computing is motivated as suitable for this problem because quantum superposition is claimed to enable simultaneous analysis of exponentially many transaction patterns, and AQEN is explicitly framed as having practical deployment potential on near-term quantum devices, with additional claims that it maintains robust performance under realistic NISQ noise conditions.
    [codes: C-005, C-008, C-044]
[SILO OVERLAY]

=== PAPER c368d34620bf (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper addresses automatic detection of credit card fraud arising from misuse of stolen, lost, or counterfeited cards, noting that most systems detect fraud only after it occurs and that severe class imbalance makes detection difficult. The authors frame supervised credit card fraud (CCF) discovery as building machine-learning models on current transactional expense data where each transaction is checked against a list of known frauds, under operational constraints such as rapid response time, price sensitivity, and feature preprocessing. They further position this within the broader trend of using machine learning for fraud detection in financial institutions, which are encouraged to implement automatic schemes.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-010, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-014]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors propose a quantum machine learning (QML) detection framework implemented as an SVM augmented with quantum annealing solvers, where SVM kernel functions are formulated as quadratic unconstrained binary optimization (QUBO) problems to be solved by quantum annealers. This QML-based fraud detection scheme is explicitly compared against a set of twelve classical machine learning algorithms, with the QUBO-based SVM (SVM-QUBO) forming the core quantum-enhanced component of the system.
    [codes: C-004, C-028, C-029, C-037]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: On the Israeli bank loan dataset, the quantum-augmented SVM (SVM-QUBO) is reported to outperform all compared machine learning algorithms in both speed and overall correctness, with the authors concluding that QML is the quickest algorithm available in this setting. They find that feature selection substantially improves detection time on both datasets and markedly boosts QML accuracy, turning it from being inferior to most classical methods without feature selection to outperforming 9 of 12 with feature selection. However, they also report that for the Israeli credit card transaction dataset, which is highly imbalanced with a 23.8% fraud rate, QML is not the ideal solution in terms of cost or performance.
    [codes: C-006, C-007, C-008, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The study explicitly compares the performance of the QML-based SVM-QUBO system against twelve classical supervised machine learning approaches commonly used for fraud detection, including methods such as random forests and neural networks referenced in related work. Results indicate that while standard machine learning methodologies remain valuable—particularly for non-time-series data—the quantum-augmented SVM outperforms all classical baselines in both speed and accuracy on the time-series loan dataset, though it is not superior on the more heavily imbalanced credit card dataset.
    [codes: C-005, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-029, C-030, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-037]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge that QML is not the ideal solution for the highly imbalanced credit card transaction dataset in terms of both cost and performance, suggesting limited applicability across all fraud-detection scenarios. More broadly, they highlight the difficulty of converting real-world problems into the QUBO format required by current quantum computing hardware and the high expense of such techniques, emphasizing that it is therefore critical to carefully identify the most important application areas for QML rather than applying it indiscriminately.
    [codes: C-034, C-036]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The conclusion suggests that conventional machine learning will remain the most cost-effective and efficient choice for non-time-series financial data until quantum computing matures significantly, implying that future work should track hardware and algorithmic advances to reassess this balance. The authors also state that their study contributes by outlining future QML research directions, pointing to a research agenda on where and how quantum methods should be applied in fraud detection and related financial time-series problems.
    [codes: C-035, C-038]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims novelty in developing and evaluating a QML-based fraud detection framework that integrates SVMs with quantum annealing via a QUBO formulation of the kernel, and in systematically comparing this SVM-QUBO approach against twelve classical machine learning algorithms on two financial datasets. It further positions its contribution as shedding light on how to choose techniques for different dataset types while balancing speed, accuracy, and price, and as helping to identify promising areas and future directions for applying QML in imbalanced time-series financial data.
    [codes: C-004, C-005, C-008, C-009, C-028, C-029, C-037, C-038]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study frames supervised CCF detection using transactional data where each transaction is checked against known frauds, and applies a suite of supervised ML approaches including SVMs, with multiple classical architectures such as random forests, neural networks, Naive Bayes, Bayesian belief networks, and CNNs discussed in related work. The system architecture includes testing time-series data for stationarity using ADF and KPSS, applying basic detrending and power, square-root, or logarithmic transforms for non-stationary data, and then using dimension reduction and LASSO-based feature selection to eliminate noisy variables and improve prediction models; feature selection is explicitly evaluated for its impact on detection time and accuracy. The authors also emphasize trade-offs between rapid response time, price sensitivity, and feature preprocessing as key operational constraints in financial fraud detection.
    [codes: C-012, C-013, C-014, C-016, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-007, C-029]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper includes general background statements that machine learning is a branch of AI that makes predictions from historical data patterns and that previous credit card fraud detection studies have primarily relied on neural networks—with only a small minority exploring deep neural nets—and random forests, and notes that CNNs can be trained with relatively little data preprocessing.
    [codes: C-015, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-021]
