=== PAPER c408224c2f59 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets automation of two key financial decision tasks—credit card fraud detection and loan eligibility prediction—both of which have become more challenging due to growing transaction volumes and digital financial activity. The authors frame these as supervised binary classification problems where accurate prediction is hindered by scalability, overfitting, high computational cost in classical ML models, and persistent feature selection challenges in DNN-based financial models. They motivate a quantum approach as a way to process high-dimensional financial data more efficiently while improving optimization and prediction quality.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-007, C-009, C-010, C-011]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The proposed method is a Quantum Feature Deep Neural Network (QFDNN) that combines a UU†-based feature encoding block with a variational quantum circuit (VQC). The UU† module encodes PCA-reduced financial features into quantum states by computing inner products between centroid and test states and extracting similarity from the amplitude of the |0⟩⊗n computational basis state using multiple-qubit amplitude encoding with controlled and anti-controlled U gates; this scheme is iteratively extended to higher qubits and is claimed to encode 2^N−1 features using only N qubits with logarithmic qubit scaling. The variational part consists of parameterized Ry and RZ layers (with entangling gates implied) optimized with the COBYLA optimizer using a loss defined as 1 − accuracy, and the overall architecture is explicitly evaluated under six Kraus-operator-based noise channels (bit-flip, bit-phase flip, phase flip, depolarizing, amplitude damping, phase damping).
    [codes: C-003, C-013, C-014, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: On the credit card fraud (CCF) dataset, QFDNN reportedly achieves an accuracy of 0.822 with near-perfect precision of 0.9524, and an ablation study shows accuracies of 0.726 for the UU† block alone and 0.786 for the variational block alone. On the loan prediction (LP) dataset, QFDNN attains a peak accuracy of 0.744 with an F1 score of 0.8247, which the authors describe as indicating a robust balance of precision and recall. The model is also tested against six noise models: it is most sensitive to bit-flip and bit-phase flip errors (accuracy dropping to around 0.366–0.372), shows near-constant accuracy under phase-flip noise, and exhibits moderate degradation under amplitude damping and depolarizing noise. The authors further claim that, compared with prior quantum models, QFDNN achieves similar tasks with fewer qubits, highlighting resource efficiency.
    [codes: C-004, C-005, C-016, C-017, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The authors benchmark QFDNN against several standard classical ML models, including logistic regression, SVM, ANN, random forest, KNN, and gradient boosting. On the CCF dataset, these classical models achieve nearly perfect scores of 1.000 across all metrics, substantially outperforming QFDNN’s reported accuracy of 0.822, albeit with QFDNN showing high precision. On the LP dataset, classical models again achieve higher accuracies (e.g., around 0.85 for LR and SVM) and F1 scores than QFDNN’s accuracy of 0.744, although the paper emphasizes QFDNN’s relatively strong balance between precision and recall. The comparison is framed primarily around accuracy and related metrics rather than runtime, and the authors additionally stress that their quantum model uses fewer qubits than other quantum baselines rather than outperforming classical models.
    [codes: C-002, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-039, C-040, C-044]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper acknowledges that the chosen loss function (1 − accuracy) is effectively non-differentiable in practice, which motivates the use of the derivative-free COBYLA optimizer and implies limitations for gradient-based training strategies. The authors also explicitly state that the training process "takes comparatively more time," indicating a practical performance limitation despite the resource-efficient encoding claims. Additionally, they note that existing quantum models are vulnerable to noise, motivating their design; although they claim improved robustness, the significant performance drops observed under certain noise types further underscore that noise remains a challenge.
    [codes: C-012, C-045, C-046]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work directions include exploring alternative optimizers such as Adam or gradient descent in conjunction with different, more suitable cost functions to overcome current optimization and training-time limitations. The authors also propose extending QFDNN to broader financial tasks, explicitly mentioning portfolio optimization and risk assessment, and suggest hardware-specific optimizations to better align the method with real quantum devices.
    [codes: C-047, C-048]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The authors claim to introduce a novel, resource-efficient, multi-feature and noise-resilient Quantum Feature Deep Neural Network (QFDNN) tailored for financial applications such as fraud detection and loan prediction. Central to their novelty claim are the UU†-based feature encoding combined with a variational quantum circuit that together aim to reduce overfitting and computational overhead, an exponential feature compression scheme that purportedly encodes 2^N−1 features using only N qubits with logarithmic qubit requirements, and empirical demonstrations of robustness under six different noise models. They further position QFDNN as more qubit-efficient than existing quantum models while still achieving competitive accuracies on two financial datasets.
    [codes: C-003, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-017, C-023, C-044]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses two public financial datasets (credit card fraud and loan prediction) that are preprocessed via principal component analysis to reduce dimensionality to seven features and feature scaling to ensure consistency; for the fraud dataset the 'Amount' and 'Time' features are dropped before PCA. Class imbalance is addressed using SMOTE oversampling, and both datasets are truncated to smaller, simulator-compatible sample sizes. The experimental setup trains QFDNN for 10 iterations, which the authors state is sufficient for stable convergence, and includes ablation studies that separately evaluate the UU† and variational components. Noise is modeled rigorously using Kraus operators across six standard channels. However, all experiments are conducted on a simulator (no hardware details or code availability are provided), and classical baselines are reported but without runtime comparisons.
    [codes: C-016, C-018, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-038]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper situates its work within the broader context of "social financial technology," stating an intention to enhance trust and security in financial systems by leveraging advanced automation, and it emphasizes that deep neural networks are already powerful tools for complex financial analysis, with quantum computing presented as a way to build on these capabilities. It also reiterates general claims that quantum models like quantum deep neural networks can offer faster optimization and more accurate predictions for high-dimensional financial data.
    [codes: C-006, C-008, C-010, C-011]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is directly relevant to fraud_detection because it evaluates a model on a credit card fraud dataset and frames the task as binary classification under class imbalance and noise. Its main value for this silo is the combination of fraud-specific preprocessing, fraud-dataset results, and explicit robustness testing under multiple noise conditions.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]

    [codes: C-036, C-037, C-038  dim_source: dimension_3]

=== PAPER c43a47ce0c1a (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames online banking and credit card fraud detection as a highly imbalanced binary classification problem where traditional rule-based, statistical, and machine-learning systems struggle with evolving fraud tactics, data scale, and complexity. It motivates exploring a hybrid quantum–classical model to better capture complex patterns for near real-time fraud detection in financial transactions.
    [codes: C-011, C-016, C-020, C-021, C-023, C-024, C-041, C-012, C-015]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors use a hybrid quantum–classical pipeline where preprocessed transaction data are first passed through a classical neural network to obtain a reduced feature vector, which is then angle-encoded into a quantum circuit using U3 gates, Hadamard gates for superposition, and Controlled‑Z gates for entanglement. The resulting quantum state vector or density-matrix-based representation is measured and fed into a classical neural network for final sigmoid-based binary classification, with class imbalance handled by SMOTE and RandomOverSampler and model hyperparameters tuned via GridSearchCV. Implementation is based on Qiskit QuantumCircuit simulations, with reported average circuit depth of about 7.3 for the first 10 records.
    [codes: C-012, C-002, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-003, C-022, C-036, C-047, C-048, C-037, C-038, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-026, C-031, C-032, C-039, C-043, C-007, C-053, C-042, C-049]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: On the Credit Card Fraud Detection dataset, the hybrid quantum–classical model is reported to achieve 92% overall accuracy, with precision and recall of 0.92 for the non‑fraud class and 0.65 and 0.68, respectively, for the fraud class, and a false positive rate kept below 10%. The authors state that quantum-inspired feature engineering and the hybrid architecture improve data representation and classification accuracy on this highly imbalanced dataset.
    [codes: C-001, C-004, C-005, C-013, C-041, C-044, C-045, C-046, C-050, C-051, C-054]

Existing baselines such as rule-based systems, statistical models (e.g., logistic regression, Bayesian networks), and classical ML algorithms (decision trees, random forests, SVMs) are described, but the reported performance comparison remains qualitative rather than giving head‑to‑head metric tables.
    [codes: C-020, C-021, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-027, C-051]

This architecture is presented as a new contribution that enhances data representation power, improves classification accuracy on imbalanced fraud data, and shows potential advantages such as reduced false positives and near real-time detection.
    [codes: C-001, C-012, C-014, C-015, C-022, C-031, C-032, C-037, C-050, C-051, C-054]

The hybrid pipeline, including network architecture and some hyperparameters, is described, and model selection is performed via GridSearchCV. Evaluation relies on standard metrics (accuracy, precision, recall, F1), a detailed classification report for both classes, and additional quantum‑side diagnostics such as density matrix representations, measurement counts, and average circuit depth; however, explicit classical baseline metrics and runtime comparisons are not reported in detail.

They also discuss general hybrid and ensemble approaches that combine rule-based, statistical, and machine learning methods for improved fraud detection.

=== PAPER c6d03068fd05 (type: central) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper addresses financial fraud detection as a binary classification problem and proposes a Quantum Federated Neural Network for Financial Fraud Detection (QFNN-FFD) that merges Quantum Machine Learning with Federated Learning to detect fraudulent transactions while preserving data privacy. The authors explicitly motivate this formulation by emphasizing the critical confidentiality requirements in the financial industry and the need for decentralized, privacy-preserving learning across multiple clients.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-005, C-013]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The method is a hybrid quantum–federated learning framework where each client trains a parameterized quantum neural network using angle encoding: each classical feature is mapped to two rotation angles (Ry/Rz) followed by a CNOT-based entanglement chain to capture feature correlations. Training uses the Adam optimizer with parameter-shift gradients and MSE loss; clients’ optimized parameters are then aggregated on a central server via federated averaging, under an IID per-client data assumption, with stated per-client computational complexity O(TDP) driven by qubits, circuit depth/layers, and number of clients. Implementation uses PennyLane for the QNN and Qiskit for simulating six quantum noise models.
    [codes: C-010, C-011, C-012, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-029, C-035]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: On the IEEE-CIS Fraud Detection dataset, QFNN-FFD is reported to achieve validation accuracy around 0.95 with precision consistently above 95%, and validation loss reduced from 0.275 to 0.02, with a pronounced learning phase between iterations 10–20 and stability across clients by around iteration 70. Under noisy simulation, the framework maintains high accuracy (around 0.97) for several noise types up to moderate noise parameters, but performance degrades at extreme depolarizing noise and under strong phase damping, though the authors still emphasize achieving about 80% accuracy under some noisy conditions as evidence of robustness and near-term suitability.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-046, C-049]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The authors claim that QFNN-FFD outperforms existing fraud detection systems and other QML models on similar datasets in terms of accuracy, and that integrating federated learning does not degrade performance: the federated model achieves 95% accuracy compared with 93% for a non-federated QNN baseline. They further argue that, unlike prior models which focus mainly on computational performance and quantum advantage, their framework adds a privacy-preserving FL component, implying a dual advantage in accuracy and privacy over both classical and quantum baselines.
    [codes: C-008, C-040, C-041, C-042]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper acknowledges that, despite generally stable performance under various noise models, sensitivity to certain noise types (e.g., more severe impact from phase damping) highlights the need for continuous monitoring of model performance and potential fine-tuning during deployment. The authors also imply that current robustness is not uniform across all noise regimes, suggesting that additional error-mitigation or correction will be required to address the most vulnerable noise channels.
    [codes: C-038, C-044, C-047]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: For future work, the authors propose implementing an early-exit strategy in training to reduce computational resources without harming effectiveness, and using the detailed noise-behavior analysis to guide the design of enhanced quantum error-correction or mitigation techniques targeting the most harmful noise types. They also speculate that the QFNN-FFD framework could be adapted to other highly sensitive domains such as healthcare and cybersecurity.
    [codes: C-045, C-047, C-050]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The claimed novelty lies in introducing a quantum federated neural network tailored for financial fraud detection that explicitly integrates privacy-preserving federated learning with QML, addressing a stated gap where most prior QML fraud models focus on computational benefits without robust privacy measures. The authors position their framework as a novel approach that significantly improves accuracy and efficiency over conventional methods while enhancing security by keeping data local and only sharing aggregated updates.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-014, C-015, C-041, C-048]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses the public IEEE-CIS Fraud Detection dataset with a detailed preprocessing pipeline including one-hot encoding, standardization, and up-sampling to address class imbalance, and evaluates across 15 IID clients with a clearly specified training configuration (Adam, learning rate 0.1, up to 100 local and global iterations). The authors also provide a complexity analysis linking computational cost to qubit count, circuit depth, and number of clients, and conduct systematic robustness experiments across six distinct quantum noise models to assess practical resilience of the framework.
    [codes: C-016, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-035]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The authors interpret the observed robustness under several noise types as underlining the framework’s potential suitability for deployment on near-term quantum hardware, and they explicitly argue that the federated setup enhances security and privacy by keeping data local and reducing central points of failure. They also emphasize that QFNN-FFD preserves privacy by design in the federated setting, in addition to maintaining or improving accuracy compared with non-federated QNNs.
    [codes: C-005, C-006, C-015, C-041, C-043, C-046, C-049]

=== PAPER c7451962353b (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames the problem as the difficulty of conventional and classical portfolio and risk management methods in handling large, complex, and uncertain financial datasets and high-dimensional asset universes, which become computationally expensive at scale. It positions these tasks as combinatorial optimization and simulation problems that could benefit from quantum parallelism within a hybrid quantum–classical framework for financial risk management and portfolio optimization.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-011, C-030]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors conceptually propose a hybrid quantum–classical approach built on five implementation layers—classical preprocessing, quantum encoding, quantum processing, hybrid integration, and a decision layer—where classical components clean and transform data and quantum routines address core optimization and simulation subproblems. Specifically, they highlight the use of QAOA and VQE for portfolio optimization, QML for pattern recognition, clustering, forecasting, and anomaly detection, and Quantum Monte Carlo for accelerating Monte Carlo-type simulations and VaR calculations, all orchestrated via cloud-based APIs connecting CPUs and QPUs.
    [codes: C-003, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-029, C-030, C-032, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: As a conceptual review, the paper does not report new empirical metrics but synthesizes claimed benefits from prior work and industry pilots, and qualitatively evaluates its proposed framework. It states that hybrid models show strong potential for improving decision-making under uncertainty and can enhance speed, scenario analysis, and reliability by selectively using quantum processors for core optimization problems while minimizing noise-related issues and supporting real-time risk and portfolio computations on NISQ hardware. The authors also report, based on cited studies and industry examples, that hybrid quantum–classical approaches may reduce computational power needs, improve clustering and forecasting accuracy, enhance risk analytics and VaR calculations, and could give early adopters a strategic edge; overall, they conclude that hybrid quantum–classical approaches present a feasible and scalable basis for next-generation financial analytics.
    [codes: C-005, C-010, C-012, C-025, C-026, C-031, C-033, C-034, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-044, C-046, C-047, C-048, C-049, C-050, C-051, C-052, C-053, C-058, C-060]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper contrasts classical methods with quantum and hybrid approaches by noting that conventional techniques struggle with big, complex, uncertain financial data and become computationally expensive in higher asset dimensions, while quantum computing can, in principle, evaluate multiple possibilities in parallel via superposition and entanglement. Drawing on cited work and industry pilots rather than new experiments, the authors report that QAOA-based portfolio methods and hybrid models have been shown to outperform traditional solvers on small portfolios, reduce computational power requirements, and in some cases deliver more accurate customer clustering and risk estimation than classical models; they also assert that quantum Monte Carlo can significantly enhance the speed and accuracy of VaR calculations. At the same time, they emphasize that their hybrid design is meant to extend rather than replace classical systems, with classical hardware continuing to handle data-heavy tasks and overall workflows.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-006, C-007, C-010, C-011, C-012, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-041, C-042, C-044, C-047, C-051]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge that most existing quantum finance studies are still theoretical, with limited empirical validation on live financial data and minimal attention to integrated operational models, which constrains the evidence base for real-world effectiveness. They highlight the technical limitations of current NISQ devices—few qubits, short coherence times, and high noise—which restrict the scale of purely quantum computations and motivate the need for hybrid architectures. The paper also notes challenges around explainability and fairness, warning that QML models can behave as opaque black boxes and may reinforce unfair decisions if trained on biased data, and it recognizes that powerful quantum computers could eventually threaten classical encryption and financial data security. From an economic and infrastructural perspective, the authors point out that high setup costs may exclude smaller institutions and that significant architectural modernization, cybersecurity upgrades, and data-encoding layers are required for adoption.
    [codes: C-014, C-015, C-027, C-054, C-055, C-056, C-057, C-059]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work is implied rather than detailed: the authors identify the need for more empirical validation of quantum approaches on live financial data and for research on integrating quantum and classical systems into cohesive operational decision-making frameworks. They also suggest, through discussion of infrastructural and ethical issues, that further work will be needed on building explainable, fair quantum models and on designing modernized, secure architectures and data-encoding layers suitable for hybrid quantum–classical workflows in financial institutions.
    [codes: C-014, C-015, C-054, C-055, C-059]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper’s main claimed contribution is a high-level hybrid quantum–classical framework, organized into sequential stages and five implementation layers, that conceptually integrates classical preprocessing, quantum encoding and optimization (via QAOA, VQE, QML, QMC), hybrid post-processing, and managerial decision support for financial risk management and portfolio optimization. It also claims to offer accessible, non-mathematical conceptual explanations aimed at a broad academic audience, and to synthesize existing literature and industry pilots into a coherent, descriptive–analytical view of how such hybrid architectures can be feasibly and scalably deployed on NISQ-era hardware.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-031, C-033, C-034, C-039, C-060]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study is explicitly descriptive and analytical, based on secondary sources such as existing literature, industry reports, and theoretical models, and it does not conduct any new empirical simulations or experiments. The proposed framework is developed as a conceptual flow through sequential stages and layered architecture rather than via implementation and benchmarking, and the authors intentionally avoid complex mathematics to keep the exposition accessible. As a result, the paper does not report datasets, quantitative performance metrics, or reproducible experimental protocols, focusing instead on conceptual design and synthesis.
    [codes: C-004, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper provides broader context about industry experimentation and enabling infrastructure, noting that major financial institutions such as Goldman Sachs, JPMorgan Chase, Nasdaq, and BBVA are piloting hybrid quantum–classical approaches for option pricing, portfolio analysis, anomaly detection, and customer clustering, and that cloud platforms from IBM, Google, Amazon, D-Wave, and others have helped democratize access to quantum tools. It also emphasizes foundational quantum concepts like superposition and entanglement as motivations for financial applications, describes how a toolkit of quantum algorithms can complement each other within hybrid architectures, and underscores that early adopters may gain competitive advantages from enhanced optimization, forecasting, and risk analytics.
    [codes: C-006, C-007, C-008, C-009, C-013, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-043, C-045, C-046, C-047, C-049, C-050, C-052, C-053, C-058]

=== PAPER c8872ea43678 (type: central) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper addresses financial fraud detection on high-dimensional, imbalanced transaction datasets and investigates whether Quantum Machine Learning (QML) can improve pattern recognition over traditional machine learning, particularly for low-frequency fraud signatures. The problem is formulated as a binary classification task on anonymized transaction records containing both genuine and fraudulent entries, with attention to non-linear and subtle patterns that conventional methods struggle to capture.
    [codes: C-001, C-004, C-007, C-027, C-038, C-052, C-054]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The study employs Quantum Support Vector Machines (QSVM) and a Variational Quantum Classifier (VQC) within a hybrid quantum–classical pipeline implemented in Qiskit on IBM’s simulator backend. Preprocessing includes normalization, mutual-information-based feature selection, and SMOTE oversampling, with models trained on an 80/20 train–test split and evaluated using classification metrics plus training time and quantum gate depth; additional linear regression relates performance to qubit count and feature dimension, and ARIMA is used to analyze fraud pattern recurrence.
    [codes: C-002, C-005, C-006, C-008, C-025, C-026, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-043]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The authors report that QSVM achieves the best performance with accuracy 0.89 and F1 score 0.88, while VQC attains accuracy 0.86 and F1 0.84 with the fastest training time (11.8 seconds). Both quantum models exhibit higher F1 scores and more balanced precision–recall than the classical SVM, which the authors interpret as robustness on imbalanced fraud data, and regression analysis shows accuracy increasing with qubit count (R² = 0.91) with diminishing returns beyond 12 qubits; time-series analysis reveals repeated fraud patterns consistent with known attack tactics.
    [codes: C-003, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-044, C-045, C-046, C-047, C-048, C-054]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The quantum models are explicitly benchmarked against a classical SVM baseline, with the authors stating that QSVM and VQC significantly outperform the classical SVM in accuracy and F1 score, and that QSVM’s F1 score (0.88 vs. 0.75) reflects better boundary discrimination in sparse regions. In the broader literature review, the authors also cite prior work where QSVC and VQC showed superior F1 and recall compared to classical baselines such as Random Forests, Logistic Regression, and classical NNs in fraud-related tasks.
    [codes: C-004, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-015, C-016, C-035, C-037, C-041, C-042, C-044, C-054]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper acknowledges several limitations: current Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum (NISQ) hardware lacks scalability for enterprise-level deployment, and optimal quantum feature encoding remains an open problem, with poorly chosen feature maps potentially degrading performance. Practical deployment is further constrained by data privacy and encryption challenges in federated quantum architectures, increased model complexity and longer inference times due to VQC circuit depth, and diminishing accuracy gains beyond 12 qubits because of noise and decoherence. The experiments are run on IBM’s simulator rather than real hardware due to qubit noise, underscoring the gap between simulated and deployable systems.
    [codes: C-017, C-018, C-019, C-029, C-047, C-049]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The authors call for hardware-aware QML model design tailored to NISQ systems and development of explainable QML methods to satisfy regulatory compliance. They propose integrating QML with blockchain and privacy-preserving quantum cryptography, exploring hybrid quantum–classical pipelines in real-time fraud analytics dashboards, and advancing scalability through quantum error correction and integration with live transaction streams. Future research directions also include federated quantum learning architectures for privacy-preserving cross-platform fraud detection and quantum graph neural networks for network-based fraud scenarios, along with modular, scalable QML frameworks embedded in fintech ecosystems.
    [codes: C-014, C-016, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-050, C-055]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims to extend QML for fraud detection beyond conceptual promise to tested application by combining empirical benchmarking, statistical modeling, and quantum simulation on a realistic, imbalanced fraud dataset. It positions its contribution as providing evidence for the transformative potential and practical viability of QML—particularly hybrid architectures—for financial fraud detection and surveillance, emphasizing improved detection metrics and interpretability in high-stakes financial contexts.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-005, C-007, C-008, C-023, C-024, C-051]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses anonymized transaction records with both genuine and fraudulent entries, applies normalization, mutual-information-based feature selection, and SMOTE oversampling, then performs an 80/20 train–test split. Evaluation includes multiple metrics (accuracy, precision, recall, F1, training time, quantum gate depth), and is complemented by linear regression relating performance to qubit count and feature dimension and ARIMA-based time-series analysis of fraud recurrence. The authors also state that all code and data are publicly available to support reproducibility and peer validation, and explicitly design comparisons under equivalent computational budgets, though experiments are conducted on IBM’s simulator due to hardware noise.
    [codes: C-007, C-027, C-028, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-039, C-046, C-048]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper includes broader contextual and theoretical claims, such as the role of quantum parallelism and Hilbert space representations in enabling exploration of vast feature spaces and capturing non-linear patterns often missed by classical algorithms, and it invokes superposition and entanglement as mechanisms promising exponential speedups in detecting subtle fraud patterns, although these speedups are presented as theoretical potential rather than empirically demonstrated.
    [codes: C-006, C-043, C-053]

=== PAPER c94ec3a9d152 (type: central) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper tackles phishing detection as a major cyber threat that causes monetary loss, reputational damage, and business disruption, emphasizing that many organizations still lack sufficient technology to identify bogus URLs and that traditional blocklist-based detection struggles with evolving techniques such as DGAs. It frames a broader research gap as the shortage of comprehensive phishing models that jointly consider multiple elements of phishing schemes, while this work focuses primarily on URL and webpage-code–based detection within that wider context.
    [codes: C-001, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The core classifier is a shallow hybrid quantum-classical convolutional neural network (SHQCNN) that, according to the authors, combines advantages of classical and quantum computing, using a variational quantum circuit (VQC) to reduce parameters and operate in the NISQ regime. The variational quantum algorithm (VQA) is described as combining kernel encoding and multiple output methods to support effective multi-class classification, with kernel coding claimed to lower computational complexity and improve feature separability. However, the authors explicitly state that no real quantum experiment was carried out: the quantum component is simulated using classical frameworks rather than executed on quantum hardware.
    [codes: C-007, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Across four public phishing URL datasets, the proposed OptSHQCNN framework reportedly achieves very high performance, with overall accuracy, precision, recall, and F1/FI-score values above 99% and MCC around 99%, and false positive rates well below 1%. Detailed results show accuracies of 99.36% on Mendeley_2020, 99.18% on ISCX-URL-2016, 99.12% on a URL-based dataset, and 99.17% on PhishStorm, alongside low computational time (around 9–13 ms) in feature selection and optimization. The authors also report that kernel coding improves phishing classification by reducing computational complexity and that an optimizer configuration using their shuffled shepherd algorithm yields the best accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score among tested optimizers.
    [codes: C-010, C-025, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The study claims that the proposed OptSHQCNN-based method outperforms a range of popular phishing detection and optimization methods, including traditional machine learning (e.g., Random Forest), deep learning variants (e.g., DNN+LSTM), and standard optimizers. On each benchmark dataset, the authors report that their model achieves the best performance across accuracy, precision, recall, F1/FI-score, and MCC compared to these classical baselines, and they further highlight that their custom optimizer configuration yields higher accuracy and related metrics than standard optimizers.
    [codes: C-010, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge that their approach is limited by focusing primarily on URL-based information, explicitly noting that it does not fully incorporate other informative aspects of phishing such as visual similarity of web content and user interaction behavior. They also concede a methodological limitation on the quantum side: no real quantum simulation or execution was carried out in their experimental setup, meaning all quantum components were evaluated only via classical simulation rather than on actual quantum hardware.
    [codes: C-022, C-040]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: For practical deployment, the authors suggest that their findings could be used to develop security plugins for clients, browsers, and instant messaging applications running on endpoints such as PCs and smartphones, implying future engineering work to embed the model at network edges and personal terminals.
    [codes: C-011]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper positions its main contribution as a dual-phase deep learning framework (pre-deployment and post-deployment) featuring a novel OptSHQCNN pipeline that integrates advanced preprocessing, attention-based feature extraction, meta-heuristic feature selection, a shallow hybrid quantum-classical CNN classifier, and optimizer-driven hyperparameter tuning. Within this pipeline, the SHQCNN is presented as a state-of-the-art hybrid architecture that leverages both classical and quantum computing principles, complemented by CBAM for focusing on important URL and code regions, RKOA for feature selection, SSOA for hyperparameter tuning, and OptBERT for URL encoding in the deployment phase.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-005, C-006, C-007, C-008, C-009, C-016, C-018, C-019, C-021, C-023, C-024, C-027, C-028, C-026]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the work uses a two-phase (pre-deployment/post-deployment) pipeline with substantial preprocessing, including cleaning invalid and irrelevant features (removal of 19 attributes), handling missing values, and addressing class imbalance via SPE-ACGAN, as well as an explicitly defined feature-selection fitness function to combine multiple objectives. Feature extraction relies on CBAM to focus on crucial URL and webpage-code elements, with RKOA used as a meta-heuristic feature selector inspired by red kite social behavior, and SSOA performing hyperparameter tuning over a clearly specified search space (filters, batch sizes, dropout rates, kernel sizes). The post-deployment phase employs BERT-based URL encoding to capture contextual semantics, a RESTful server-side workflow for detection, and a concrete rule that missing external features are imputed with a default value of -1; experiments use multiple named public datasets with an 80/20 train-test split and an ablation study indicating that adding more processing stages monotonically improves accuracy.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-005, C-028, C-006, C-023, C-024, C-008, C-026, C-027, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-038]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: None; all verified codes could be allocated to the other reporting dimensions.
    [codes: —]

=== PAPER cbc953d2f213 (type: central) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames credit card fraud detection as a cybersecurity problem where traditional information security and classical ML techniques are increasingly inadequate given the volume and complexity of modern transaction data; it aims to evaluate quantum computing, specifically quantum machine learning, as a new strategy for fraud detection and classification in this context.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-010, C-014, C-015, C-017, C-018]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The study implements a hybrid quantum–classical fraud detection framework that integrates three quantum approaches: (1) a Quantum Support Vector Classifier (QSVC) using Pauli feature maps and quantum kernels to map normalized and scaled transaction features into quantum states; (2) a QSVC with a ZZ feature map that entangles qubits in a linear pattern to create high-dimensional data representations; and (3) a Variational Quantum Circuit (VQC) classifier based on RealAmplitudes, optimized in a quantum–classical loop. All models use the same preprocessed, balanced version of the European credit card fraud dataset, prepared via correlation-based feature ranking, random undersampling, normalization, PCA, and Min–Max scaling.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-005, C-006, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: On the balanced credit card fraud dataset, the QSVC with Pauli feature maps achieved an accuracy of 0.9189, precision of 0.96, and recall of 0.83, while the QSVC with ZZ feature maps and linear entanglement performed best with accuracy 0.9459, precision 1.00, and recall 0.87, notably minimizing false positives. The VQC with RealAmplitudes underperformed the QSVC variants with an accuracy of 0.8514, but is still presented as showing potential for fraud detection. The authors emphasize that the quantum models overall provide a strong balance between precision and recall, with the 1.00 precision indicating exceptionally low false positives and supporting claims that quantum feature maps and VQCs can improve discrimination and classification in credit card fraud detection.
    [codes: C-007, C-008, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-038, C-039]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The authors argue that traditional ML and classical computing approaches face scalability and performance challenges in fraud detection and suggest that their quantum models achieve superior behavior on the studied dataset, including improvements over prior studies that used the same credit card fraud data; however, explicit metric-by-metric comparisons to concrete classical baselines are only briefly referenced rather than systematically analyzed.
    [codes: C-010, C-014, C-016, C-037]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The main explicit limitation acknowledged is that all experiments are conducted in simulated quantum environments, and the authors stress that it is imperative to move from simulators to real quantum devices, implicitly recognizing that current results may not directly transfer to noisy, hardware-constrained settings.
    [codes: C-040]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: For future work, the authors call for transitioning from quantum simulators to real quantum hardware to validate and extend their findings under realistic device noise and resource constraints.
    [codes: C-040]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The contribution is positioned as a quantum-assisted machine learning framework for credit card fraud detection that integrates multiple quantum classification techniques (two QSVC variants with different feature maps and a VQC) and demonstrates, on a standard fraud dataset, that quantum feature maps and variational circuits can enhance classification performance—particularly reducing false positives—thus illustrating the potential of quantum computing to significantly impact cybersecurity.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-007, C-008, C-033, C-034, C-037, C-039, C-009]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the paper uses the public European credit card fraud dataset and applies a clear preprocessing pipeline: correlation-based feature analysis, random undersampling of non-fraud cases to create a balanced subset of 300 fraudulent and 300 non-fraudulent transactions, a 75/25 train–test split, normalization to zero mean and unit variance, PCA for dimensionality reduction, and Min–Max scaling to [0,4]. The same processed data are used across quantum models, and performance is reported via accuracy, precision, and recall; however, results are obtained exclusively on a simulator without reported resource metrics or robustness analysis.
    [codes: C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-032, C-033, C-035, C-040]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: Beyond the core experimental narrative, the paper includes conceptual framing that portrays quantum computing as a fundamental shift in computational capacity, highlighting qubit properties such as superposition and entanglement and suggesting that quantum computers can perform intricate computations at unprecedented speeds, which underpins their proposed use in cybersecurity and fraud detection. The authors also claim that specific quantum feature maps, like Pauli maps, can capture intricate data linkages and that entangling feature maps like ZZ produce high-dimensional representations beneficial for classification.
    [codes: C-011, C-012, C-013, C-027, C-028, C-036]

=== PAPER cd5dc35d5f39 (type: central) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper addresses credit card fraud detection in financial transactions using a large, highly imbalanced real-world dataset from Stone, a Brazilian fintech, containing 145 million transactions with only 19 thousand fraudulent cases (highly unbalanced) and frames the task as a binary classification problem suitable for RBM-based modeling and QUBO formulation for annealing-based optimization.
    [codes: C-002, C-004, C-019, C-013]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors use a quantum-assisted Restricted Boltzmann Machine framework where the RBM/GRBM energy function, mathematically equivalent to a quantum annealing Hamiltonian, is cast as a QUBO and solved via quantum annealing on D-Wave hardware, with QA replacing Gibbs sampling in the PCD negative phase; simulated annealing on D-Wave simulators is used as an intermediate step to approximate quantum fluctuations, all implemented through the QARBoM.jl and QUBO.jl software stack and constrained by minor-embedding to real hardware.
    [codes: C-005, C-006, C-007, C-013, C-016, C-018, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-022, C-023]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The authors claim that the quantum-assisted RBM method achieves superior performance in most figures of merit compared to classical approaches, though in their detailed experiments they report that all three training methodologies (strictly classical PCD, simulated-annealing-assisted PCD, and quantum-annealing-assisted PCD) reach similar accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score, with simulated-annealing-assisted training slightly higher on all metrics while quantum annealing requires about 1 hour 50 minutes for 50 epochs; they also find that manual undersampling was ultimately required for class balancing and that different optimal hyperparameters emerge, with classical training favoring 200 hidden units versus 65 hidden units for the annealing-assisted approaches, and observe a general trade-off where higher performance is associated with longer runtime.
    [codes: C-001, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-034, C-036, C-037, C-038]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The study explicitly compares three training regimes—strictly classical PCD with Gibbs sampling, simulated-annealing-assisted PCD, and quantum-annealing-assisted PCD—evaluated on the same balanced fraud dataset using standard classification metrics, and reports that all models achieve similar performance with the simulated-annealing-assisted method slightly outperforming the others across all metrics, while using lighter configurations (fewer hidden units and fewer samples) than the classical model; nevertheless, quantum and simulated-annealing-assisted approaches incur significantly longer runtimes, illustrating a performance–runtime trade-off relative to the fast classical baseline.
    [codes: C-001, C-011, C-016, C-024, C-027, C-028, C-031, C-032, C-036, C-037, C-038]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge several limitations: the standard binary RBM is incompatible with their continuous-valued transaction data, motivating a GRBM extension and Gaussian assumptions on the visible units; computing the partition function and exact model expectations is intractable on classical hardware, requiring approximate methods such as PCD with Gibbs sampling or annealing-based sampling; representing Gaussian visible units in QUBO form necessitates a binary expansion that increases the number of variables, which in turn becomes a bottleneck for quantum implementation, especially given minor-embedding constraints that make embedding the RBM on real QA hardware the primary bottleneck and force aggressive feature restriction. They also note that quantum annealing does not always remain in the ground state and may sample from excited states, that using the same learning rate schedule as in classical and simulated-annealing-assisted training causes the quantum-assisted model to diverge (requiring adjusted learning rates), and that manual undersampling was required due to dataset length and imbalance. Finally, they explicitly state that whether the observed performance advantage of annealing-assisted methods is universal or specific to this particular dataset remains an open question, highlighting concerns about generalizability.
    [codes: C-008, C-010, C-012, C-014, C-015, C-017, C-019, C-022, C-023, C-033, C-039]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The paper suggests extending the quantum-assisted RBM framework beyond credit card fraud to more general fault detection tasks in financial systems and indicates plans to explore other quantum-assisted machine learning approaches, specifically quantum kernel methods for support vector machine training.
    [codes: C-004, C-040]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The authors present their work as demonstrating, on a large real-world credit card dataset, that quantum-assisted RBMs trained via quantum annealing can achieve superior or at least comparable performance to classical RBM training across standard fraud-detection metrics, and they position their study as paving the way for implementing quantum-assisted RBMs for broader financial fault detection tasks, while also establishing a comparative framework spanning classical, simulated-annealing-assisted, and quantum-annealing-assisted PCD training using a unified QARBoM.jl/QUBO.jl stack.
    [codes: C-001, C-004, C-026, C-027, C-036, C-037]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses a substantial real-world dataset from a major fintech but ultimately trains on a manually undersampled 39,000-transaction balanced subset to address extreme class imbalance and hardware constraints; categorical features are processed via one-hot encoding after evaluating several encoding schemes, and numerical features are normalized to a zero-mean, unit-variance Gaussian distribution to align with GRBM assumptions. The authors filter features to limit problem size and enable minor embedding on QA hardware, and they formalize an experimental protocol comparing three training methodologies with consistent evaluation metrics (accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score) and selecting final configurations based on the best F1-score epoch, while performing explicit hyperparameter searches that reveal different optimal hidden-layer sizes for classical versus annealing-based training.
    [codes: C-002, C-008, C-009, C-011, C-012, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-035]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: None.
    [codes: —]

=== PAPER cf0d84ba3b6a (type: central) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper addresses credit card fraud detection, motivated by the need for credit card firms to accurately identify fraudulent transactions, and frames this as a binary classification task on a public credit card transaction dataset with severely imbalanced fraud/non-fraud classes that has been PCA-transformed into 28 features and then balanced for experimentation. The study is situated within the broader context of emerging quantum machine learning methods entering the AI/ML domain.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-011, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The quantum approach is a hybrid quantum neural network in which a classical neural network first processes the input and transforms 10 features into 14 outputs that serve as gate parameters for the first layer of a quantum neural network implemented using Xanadu’s Strawberry Fields; this QNN section then consists of four standard quantum neural network layers. The authors state that this hybrid scheme helps handle the hurdle of encoding classical input data into the quantum model.
    [codes: C-002, C-007, C-009, C-019, C-025, C-026]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: On the balanced subset of the credit card fraud dataset, the hybrid QNN model reportedly achieves an accuracy of 89.5%, while the TDA Mapper-based approach attains 94% accuracy. A full metric table further shows the hybrid QNN with accuracy 0.895, precision 0.90, recall 0.89, ROC 0.87, and the TDA method with accuracy 0.94, precision 0.99, recall 0.98, and ROC 0.935. The authors also note that these performance figures are based on initial experimentation.
    [codes: C-008, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-027]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The work is framed as a comparative study between a hybrid QNN and a Topological Data Analysis (TDA) Mapper-based classifier on the same publicly available fraud detection dataset, with both models receiving the same input data and performance compared using metrics such as accuracy, precision, recall, and ROC. The authors explicitly state that the TDA approach outperforms the hybrid QNN in their experiments and suggest that a combination of these approaches might generate improvements over other existing (implicitly classical) models, although they do not provide a direct benchmark against standalone classical machine learning models.
    [codes: C-002, C-019, C-020, C-023, C-024, C-027, C-028]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors qualify their findings by emphasizing that the reported performance scores stem from initial experimentation, implying limited experimental maturity and suggesting that results may change with further tuning or different data. They also acknowledge that larger and more complicated datasets would be needed to obtain more appropriate results and to substantiate improvements over other existing models, indicating that scalability and robustness to realistic data distributions remain untested within this work.
    [codes: C-027]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: For future work, the authors propose combining the hybrid QNN and TDA approaches, arguing that such a combined framework, applied to larger and more complex datasets, could yield more appropriate results and deliver improvements over existing models for fraud detection.
    [codes: C-027, C-028]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper positions its contribution as a comparative study between a hybrid quantum neural network and a TDA Mapper-based classifier for credit card fraud detection on a public dataset, including a novel probabilistic approach for labeling unknown data points in the TDA framework. It also claims that the hybrid architecture, where a classical neural network feeds gate parameters into a QNN implemented in Strawberry Fields, addresses the encoding hurdle in quantum machine learning.
    [codes: C-002, C-007, C-012, C-024, C-025, C-026]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses a publicly available dataset of European cardholder transactions from September 2013, originally highly skewed with only 0.172% fraud cases, but then rebalanced to 982 samples with an equal split of 492 fraud and 492 genuine instances. The data comprise 28 PCA-transformed features, and the authors adopt a 3:1 train–test split yielding 785 known and 197 unknown points, with the TDA Mapper configured using 20 hypercubes, 30% overlap, and two clusters to reflect the binary classification task; the same input data are fed to both the TDA and hybrid QNN models. Performance is evaluated using accuracy, precision, recall, and ROC, but the authors only report a single set of metric values for each method and do not provide implementation code, multiple runs, or comparisons to standard classical baselines, while also explicitly noting that the experiments are initial.
    [codes: C-010, C-011, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-023, C-027]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The authors contextualize their hybrid QNN work within the broader landscape of quantum neural networks, noting that such hybrid QNNs have been used in tasks like natural language processing and image processing, and they argue that exploring the topological structure of input data via TDA can support noise reduction and visualization, which may aid in credit card fraud classification. These statements primarily serve to motivate the methodological choices rather than to report new empirical findings in this paper.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-005, C-006]

=== PAPER cf9049abcad1 (type: central) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets detection of coordinated seller fraud in online marketplaces, emphasizing that fraud rings exploit relational patterns across buyers, products, and transactions and often appear as dense, anomalous subgraphs in an interaction graph. It formalizes the task as assigning each seller node an anomaly score and ranking sellers by fraud likelihood, i.e., a graph-based anomaly-ranking problem. The context is framed by the growth of e-commerce and the difficulty of traditional per-transaction anomaly detection in capturing such networked behaviors.
    [codes: C-001, C-005, C-012, C-020, C-006, C-007, C-011]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The quantum component consists of a Quantum Neural Network implemented as a parameterized quantum circuit that encodes classical GNN embeddings into quantum states via angle encoding and trains variational parameters for anomaly scoring. Specifically, QNNs are described as parameterized quantum circuits that encode data into quantum states and use variational circuits (e.g., PQCs with single-qubit rotations such as Ry(φj) and entangling layers) to produce enriched feature spaces, with GNN outputs compressed to at most four dimensions and mapped to qubit rotation angles; hybrid training alternates classical GNN updates with QNN parameter optimization using methods such as SPSA or COBYLA on IBM-simulated backends via PennyLane. The quantum module is positioned as a plug-in to the broader hybrid AI framework, with current experiments conducted on simulators due to NISQ hardware constraints.
    [codes: C-004, C-010, C-018, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The authors report that a GAT-based GNN combined with an XGBoost classifier achieves an AUC of 0.94 and precision@50 of 0.82 on e-commerce fraud data, outperforming a random forest baseline. They further claim that adding the hybrid QNN module yields a 6% absolute increase in precision@50 in low-label scenarios, interpreted as evidence for potential quantum benefit in small-data regimes. For edge deployment, a TinyML-compressed model reportedly fits in 70 KB, achieves 25 ms inference time, and maintains 80% recall, satisfying their sub-100 KB and millisecond-latency targets for on-device fraud monitoring.
    [codes: C-016, C-019, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-017, C-003, C-009]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: Classically, the paper contrasts its graph-based approach against rule-based filters and conventional machine-learning classifiers such as logistic regression and random forests on engineered features, which are said to detect only simple anomalies and struggle with coordinated, networked fraud. It also notes that traditional graph metrics like community detection or PageRank provide relational insights but lack end-to-end learning. Empirically, the authors report that their GAT + XGBoost hybrid model attains AUC=0.94 versus a random forest baseline AUC=0.88, and they situate this within prior literature where GNNs in financial fraud and telecommunications applications have demonstrated superior performance over classical methods.
    [codes: C-013, C-014, C-016, C-042]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper explicitly acknowledges that current NISQ quantum devices suffer from noise and limited qubit counts, constraining practical deployment of the proposed QNN module and motivating the use of simulators and small embedding dimensions. It further notes that graph data are vulnerable to adversarial issues such as poisoning via fake edges, which can undermine the reliability of the learned fraud signals, implying the need for robustness measures in real-world settings. While distributed frameworks are proposed to handle large-scale graphs, the work also implicitly recognizes scalability challenges in training complex GNN–QNN hybrids at the scale of billions of edges under current hardware and software limitations.
    [codes: C-037, C-047, C-028]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: For the near term (0–18 months), the authors propose scaling hybrid GNN plus classical pipelines, piloting TinyML models on seller portals, and benchmarking QNN modules on simulators. In the medium term (18–36 months), they call for development of federated GNN frameworks to enable cross-platform fraud information sharing, alongside advances in quantum error mitigation for more robust QNN use. Long-term directions (36+ months) include fault-tolerant QNNs capable of large-scale combinatorial anomaly detection and the establishment of quantum-secure communication channels to protect fraud-detection pipelines.
    [codes: C-048, C-049, C-050]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The contribution is framed as an expanded, scholarly framework that unifies formalization of fraudulent seller detection as a graph anomaly-ranking problem with a hybrid AI stack combining GNN-based graph analytics, a QNN module for potentially enriched feature spaces in low-label settings, and TinyML deployment for on-device, low-latency inference. Novelty is claimed in the specific composition of this hybrid pipeline: formal graph-based fraud scoring; multiple learning paradigms (semi-supervised, unsupervised, self-supervised); a GNN-to-QNN embedding interface; and compression and deployment workflows that bring fraud detection to microcontroller-class seller devices while incorporating explainability and accountability tooling.
    [codes: C-004, C-005, C-010, C-015, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-033, C-035, C-036, C-003, C-017, C-038, C-039, C-046]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the paper specifies a reasonably detailed end-to-end pipeline: multi-source data ingestion (transaction logs, review text, device telemetry, social signals) with deduplication, normalization, and entity resolution; rich feature engineering spanning transactional, textual, and behavioral signals; dynamic graph snapshots for temporal analysis; and use of distributed frameworks like Spark GraphX and DGL to scale to billions of edges and millions of nodes. It experiments with several GNN architectures (GCN, GAT, GraphSAGE, GAEs) and a hybrid classifier where GNN embeddings are concatenated with tabular features and fed to a gradient boosting model, and it evaluates performance using ranking-oriented metrics (AUC, precision@50, recall@50, F1) and latency. However, for the QNN module and TinyML compression, evaluation details such as train/test splits, cross-validation, or repeated-run protocols are not fully specified in the codes, and QNN results are reported from simulator experiments rather than physical hardware. The inclusion of model cards and local explanation tools (e.g., SHAP, counterfactuals) is also noted as part of the deployment and governance design.
    [codes: C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-043, C-044, C-046]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper also situates its work within broader fraud typologies, covering schemes such as fictitious transactions, collusive review rings, and triangulation scams, and emphasizes that fraud rings form dense subgraphs that differ from benign patterns—observations that conceptually motivate its graph-based approach beyond the strictly methodological and result-focused aspects.
    [codes: C-011, C-012]

=== PAPER d0176849806b (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames quantum computing as an emerging enabler for FinTech applications that require high-performance optimization, security, and analytics, amid rapidly growing computational demands from algorithmic trading, DeFi, real-time risk modeling, and fraud detection. It argues that classical HPC and distributed systems increasingly struggle with the complexity of global markets, quantum-era cryptographic challenges, and ultra-low-latency analytics, and that many FinTech applications need simultaneous, multi-user access to quantum resources. The central problem is thus how to provide scalable, shared quantum computing capabilities to support diverse, concurrent FinTech workloads.
    [codes: C-001, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-013]

The architecture virtualizes underlying quantum hardware using layered abstraction, dynamic qubit allocation, and workload-adaptive circuit scheduling, allowing concurrent execution of FinTech workloads such as portfolio optimization, fraud detection, blockchain verification, and quantum-safe cryptographic operations. Quantum computing is positioned as a complement to classical infrastructure by exploiting large state spaces and faster optimization and sampling for next-generation FinTech systems.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-005, C-010]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Based on benchmark simulations, the authors report that enabling multi-tenancy via qVMs can improve quantum hardware utilization by up to 40%, reduce queuing latency, and maintain isolation between concurrent clients without degrading quantum circuit fidelity. They further claim that these performance characteristics indicate quantum virtualization could support scalable, secure, and cost-efficient quantum-as-a-service ecosystems for FinTech.
    [codes: C-006, C-007]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper contrasts its quantum virtualization approach with classical HPC and distributed architectures by arguing that classical systems only partially meet current FinTech needs and increasingly struggle with market complexity, quantum-resilient cryptography, and ultra-low-latency analytics. However, it does not report direct, quantitative head-to-head benchmarks against specific classical systems; the comparison is primarily conceptual and motivational.
    [codes: C-009, C-010]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors emphasize that practical deployment of quantum computing in FinTech is constrained by scarce quantum hardware, noisy environments, and the challenge of supporting heterogeneous users and workloads. They identify structural barriers to widespread adoption, namely limited quantum hardware availability and inefficient resource-sharing models. Additionally, they note that current quantum processors are expensive, noisy, and have limited qubit counts, making it infeasible to dedicate entire devices to individual workflows, which motivates the need for virtualization and sharing.
    [codes: C-002, C-011, C-012]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Not addressed in this paper.
    [codes: —]

Through benchmark simulations, it argues that such virtualization can substantially increase hardware utilization, lower queuing latency, and preserve client isolation and circuit fidelity, thereby highlighting quantum virtualization as a key enabler for industrial adoption of quantum computing in FinTech and for building quantum-as-a-service ecosystems.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-006, C-007]

=== PAPER d186493da11a (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames class imbalance as a core challenge that disrupts the symmetry of data distributions and degrades predictive performance by increasing false negatives, which is particularly problematic for minority classes such as hazardous outcomes or fraud cases. It positions quantum machine learning as a way to address this imbalance without relying on resampling while explicitly noting that QML’s role in this problem remains underexplored and that prior QSVM evidence on imbalanced data is inconclusive.
    [codes: C-001, C-006, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-032]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors use a Variational Quantum Classifier (VQC) configured with a PauliFeatureMap to encode classical features into Hilbert space and a RealAmplitudes variational circuit with linear entanglement as the classifier ansatz, optimized using COBYLA. They justify choosing VQC for its flexibility via parameterized quantum circuits, robustness to NISQ noise through cost-function-based optimization, and its symmetry-aware framing; measurement is tailored to binary outputs, and evaluation is recall-focused with macro averaging to handle imbalance.
    [codes: C-003, C-005, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Across experiments, the quantum models—especially VQC with PauliFeatureMap—are reported to significantly improve recall for minority classes on imbalanced datasets, including a case where VQC achieved perfect recall in identifying hazardous food items. The PauliFeatureMap outperformed other feature maps, gate choice was shown to affect both runtime and performance, and poor gate selection (e.g., Pauli X) degraded results. The authors highlight that VQC handled imbalanced datasets better, consistently improved macro-average recall over baselines in the main food inspection setting, and showed strong robustness on financial datasets, where the quantum algorithm outperformed SMOTE-applied baselines in non-SMOTE scenarios.
    [codes: C-004, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper reports that the quantum algorithm, particularly VQC, significantly improved recall compared to traditional machine learning algorithms on the imbalanced food inspection dataset and that a no-resampling VQC configuration achieved higher recall than classical SVMs and QSVMs, including SMOTE-based variants. For the bank dataset, classical algorithms had better recall when SMOTE was applied, whereas the quantum algorithm was stronger without SMOTE; for the credit card fraud dataset, the quantum model outperformed both SMOTE and non-SMOTE classical baselines in recall and F1 without using SMOTE.
    [codes: C-022, C-024, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors explicitly acknowledge that their findings need further validation across a wider variety of real-world datasets before the utility of QML for class imbalance can be firmly established. They also note an explainability limitation: the optimization process in QML currently lacks a mathematically rich explanation for why specific quantum circuit states outperform others, leaving a conceptual gap in understanding the mechanisms behind observed performance.
    [codes: C-036, C-037]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work is directed toward enhancing the explainability of QML optimization processes so that the reasons particular quantum circuit configurations perform better can be more rigorously understood, thereby strengthening the credibility and practical applicability of QML for imbalanced classification problems.
    [codes: C-038]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims novelty in proposing a symmetry-aware quantum machine learning approach—specifically a VQC configuration—that addresses class imbalance without resampling and demonstrates that QML can, in some cases, outperform classical methods under these conditions. It further argues that leveraging Pauli-based feature maps and careful axis alignment in the encoding process provides a mechanism for exploiting data symmetries to enhance classification performance.
    [codes: C-003, C-005, C-008, C-033, C-034, C-035]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses a real imbalanced imported food inspection dataset and performs dimensionality reduction (PCA and MCA) down to four features to fit current quantum hardware constraints, then evaluates multiple feature maps, entanglement patterns, and gate choices to study their impact on performance and runtime. The evaluation emphasizes recall and macro averaging as appropriate metrics for imbalanced classification, and the authors conduct robustness tests on additional financial datasets (bank marketing and credit card fraud) to probe generality, while also noting that more diverse real-world validation remains necessary.
    [codes: C-010, C-011, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-025, C-026, C-031, C-032, C-036]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The discussion links improved classification performance to quantum-specific theoretical aspects such as Pauli decomposition—expressing Hamiltonians as linear combinations of Pauli matrices during encoding—and the importance of aligning rotation axes with data distributions, suggesting these design principles are pivotal for achieving better performance in QML-based classifiers on imbalanced data.
    [codes: C-034, C-035]
